<meta>This page begins the biographical account of Plotinus by his student Porphyry, following the preceding table of contents which outlined the structure of his philosophical works.</meta>

<sig>B</sig>

# PORPHYRY: ON THE LIFE OF PLOTINUS AND THE ARRANGEMENT OF HIS WORK

->(Plotinus born A.D. 205 in Egypt, at Lycopolis according to Eunapius,<-
->died near Rome A.D. 270.)<-

1.

Plotinus, the philosopher who was our contemporary, seemed ashamed of being in a body.

This feeling was so deeply rooted that he could never be persuaded to tell of his ancestry, his parents, or his birthplace.

He also showed an invincible reluctance to sit for a painter or a sculptor; when Amelius persisted in urging him to allow a portrait to be made, he asked him: “Is it not enough to carry around this image in which nature has enclosed us? Do you really think I must also consent to leave behind, as a desirable sight for posterity, an image of the image?”

In view of this determined refusal, Amelius brought his friend Carterius—the best artist of the day—to the lectures, which were open to every visitor. He ensured that through long observation of the philosopher, Carterius caught his most striking personal traits. From the impressions thus stored in his mind, the artist drew a first sketch; Amelius made various suggestions to help bring out the resemblance, and in this way, without the knowledge of Plotinus, the talent of Carterius gave us a life-like portrait.

2.

Plotinus was often distressed by an intestinal ailment, but he refused enemas, declaring the use of such remedies inappropriate for an elderly man; in the same way, he refused such medications as con-

<summary>Porphyry introduces Plotinus as a philosopher who disdained his physical form, refusing to discuss his origins or sit for a portrait. Despite this, his student Amelius conspired with an artist to capture Plotinus’s likeness in secret during his public lectures.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Porphyry, biography, Amelius, Carterius, Neoplatonism, portraiture, Lycopolis, asceticism</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's account of Plotinus’ life, specifically describing his final illness, his death in Campania, and the determination of his age and birth year.</meta>

<header>PLOTINUS</header>

tain any substance taken from wild beasts or reptiles. He remarked that he felt this all the more since he could not approve of eating the flesh of animals reared for the table.

He abstained from the use of the bath, contenting himself with a daily massage at home. When the terrible epidemic carried off his masseurs, he renounced all such treatment; in a short while, he contracted malignant diphtheria.

During the time I was with him, there was no sign of any such illness, but after I sailed for Sicily, the condition grew acute. His close friend, Eustochius, who was with him until his death, told me upon my return to Rome that he became hoarse, so that his voice completely lost its clear, sonorous tone; his sight grew dim, and ulcers formed on his hands and feet.

As he still insisted on addressing everyone in person, his condition prompted his friends to withdraw from his company. He therefore left Rome for Campania, retiring to a property which had belonged to Zethos, an old friend of his who was dead by this time. His needs were provided for in part from Zethos’ estate, and the rest were supplied from Minturnae, where Castricius’ property lay.

Eustochius has given me an account of Plotinus’ last moments.

He himself was staying at Puteoli and was late in arriving; when he at last came, Plotinus said: “I have been waiting for you a long time; I am striving to give back the Divine in myself to the Divine in the All.” As he spoke, a snake crept under the bed on which he lay and slipped away into a hole in the wall; at 그 same moment, Plotinus died.

This was at the end of the second year of the reign of Claudius, and, as Eustochius tells me, Plotinus was then sixty-six. I myself was at Lilybaeum at the time, Amelius was at Apamea in Syria, and Castricius was in Rome; only Eustochius was by his side.

Counting sixty-six years back from the second year of Claudius, we can fix Plotinus’ birth in the thirteenth year of Severus; but he never disclosed the month or day. This was because he did not desire any birthday sacrifice or feast; yet he himself sacrificed on the traditional birthdays of Plato and of Socrates, afterwards giving a banquet at which

<summary>Plotinus suffers a fatal illness after refusing standard medical treatments and dies in Campania at the age of sixty-six. His final words emphasize his philosophical goal of returning his inner divinity to the universal Divine.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, death, Eustochius, Campania, Porphyry, last words, Plato, Socrates, biography</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's account of Plotinus, transitioning from the details of his death back to his early life, his education in Alexandria under Ammonius Saccas, his travels to the East, and his eventual arrival in Rome.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>PORPHYRY</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

Every member of the circle who was able was expected to deliver an address.

3.
Despite his general reluctance to talk about his own life, he often related a few details to us in the course of conversation. Thus, he told how at the age of eight, when he was already attending school, he still clung to his nurse and loved to uncover her breasts and nurse. One day he was told he was a "perverse child," and so he was shamed out of the habit.

At twenty, he was seized by a passion for philosophy. He was directed to the most highly regarded professors to be found in Alexandria, but he used to return from their lectures saddened and discouraged. A friend to whom he confided his feelings recognized his natural inclination and suggested Ammonius, whom he had not yet tried. Plotinus went, heard a lecture, and exclaimed to his comrade: "This is the man I was looking for."

From that day on, he followed Ammonius constantly, and under his guidance, he made such progress in philosophy that he became eager to study the Persian methods and the system adopted among the Indians. It happened that the Emperor Gordian was at that time preparing his campaign against Persia; Plotinus joined the army and went on the expedition. He was then thirty-nine, for he had spent eleven full years under Ammonius. When Gordian was killed in Mesopotamia, it was only with great difficulty that Plotinus escaped safely to Antioch.

At forty, during the reign of Philip, he settled in Rome.

Erennius, Origen, and Plotinus had made a pact not to reveal any of the doctrines that Ammonius had disclosed to them. Plotinus kept this promise, and in all his interactions with his associates, he revealed nothing of Ammonius' system. But the pact was broken, first by Erennius and then by Origen following his example. Origen, it is true, put nothing in writing except the treatise *On the Spirit-Beings*, and during Galienus' reign, the work entitled *The King the Sole Creator*. Plotinus himself remained a

<summary>Porphyry recounts Plotinus' early education in Alexandria, his discovery of his mentor Ammonius, his participation in a military expedition to Persia, and his early years in Rome maintaining a vow of secrecy regarding his teacher's doctrines.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Plotinus, Ammonius Saccas, Alexandria, Emperor Gordian, Persia, Rome, Origen, Neoplatonism, philosophical education</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s account, describing Plotinus’ first ten years in Rome, during which he taught through oral conferences rather than writing, and introduces his students Amelius and Porphyry.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>->PLOTINUS<-</header>

[He continued for a] long time without writing, but he began to base his conferences on what he had gathered from his studies under Ammonius. In this way, writing nothing but constantly conferring with a specific group of associates, he passed ten years.

He used to encourage his listeners to ask questions—a freedom which, as Amelius told me, led to a great deal of wandering and useless talk.

Amelius had joined the circle in the third year of Philip's reign, which was also the third year of Plotinus' residence in Rome, and remained with him until the first year of Claudius—twenty-four years in total. He had come to Plotinus after effective training under Lysimachus; in hard-working diligence, he surpassed all his associates. For example, he transcribed and organized nearly all the works of Numenius and had almost memorized most of them. He also took notes of the conferences and wrote them out in about a hundred treatises, which he has since presented to Hostilianus of Apamea, his adopted son.

### 4.

I myself arrived from Greece in the tenth year of Gallienus' reign, accompanied by Antonius of Rhodes, and found that Amelius had been an associate of Plotinus for eighteen years, but he still lacked the courage to write anything except for the notebooks, which had not yet reached a hundred in number. Plotinus, in this tenth year of Gallienus, was about fifty-nine; when I first met him, I was thirty.

From about the first year of Gallienus, Plotinus had begun to write on the subjects that had arisen at the conferences; when I first came to know him in this tenth year of the reign, he had composed twenty-one treatises.

I obtained these, though they were by no means distributed freely. In fact, the distribution was reluctant and secret; those who obtained them had passed the strictest scrutiny.

Plotinus had given no titles to these treatises; everyone titled them for themselves. I cite them here under the titles which eventually became standard, quoting the first words of each to make identification easier.<note>These first words are of course omitted and the Ennead reference is added.</note>

<summary>Porphyry describes Plotinus' early teaching career in Rome, characterized by oral conferences and a reluctance to publish, and introduces his key students, Amelius and Porphyry himself.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Porphyry, Amelius, Gallienus, Ammonius, Numenius, Rome, philosophical treatises, Enneads</keywords>

7. On the Emanation of the Non-Primal from the Primal Being; and on The One (V. 4).
8. Whether all souls constitute one soul (IV. 9).

These are the twenty-one <term>treatises</term> which, as I have said, <term>Plotinus</term> had already written by his fifty-ninth year, when I first came to him.

### 5.

I had been in Rome, it is true, a little before this tenth year of <term>Gallienus</term>, but at that time <term>Plotinus</term> was taking a summer holiday, simply engaging in conversation with his friends. After coming to know him, I passed six years in close relation with him. Many questions were thoroughly examined in the conferences of those six years and, through the persuasion of <term>Amelius</term> and myself, he composed two <term>treatises</term> to establish:—

Directly after these, he composed two more; one is titled:

24. On the Absence of the <term>Intellectual-Act</term> in the <term>Transcendental</term>; and on Which Being has the Intellectual-Act Primarily and Which Being has the Intellectual-Act Secondarily (V. 6);

25. Existence, Potential and Actual (II. 5).

Following these are the next twenty:

<summary>This page continues Porphyry's chronological list of Plotinus’s works, documenting treatises twenty-two through forty-five. These titles cover essential Neoplatonic themes such as the nature of the soul, the Intellectual-Principle, and the relationship between time and eternity.</summary>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page concludes the chronological catalog of Plotinus’s writings provided by Porphyry, listing treatises forty-six through fifty-four and providing a qualitative assessment of his work across different stages of his life.</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>PORPHYRY</header>

Thus we have twenty-four treatises composed during the six years of my association with him, dealing—as the titles indicate—with such problems as happened to arise at the conferences. Adding the twenty-one composed before my arrival, we have accounted for forty-five treatises.

6.

Plotinus wrote the following five more and sent them to me while I was living in Sicily, where I had gone around the fifteenth year of the reign of Galienus:

46. On Happiness (I. 4).
47. On Providence, First (III. 2).
48. On Providence, Second (III. 3).
49. On the Conscious <term>Hypostases</term> <note>Underlying realities or substances</note> and the <term>Transcendental</term> (V. 3).
50. On Love (III. 5).

He sent these five to me in the first year of Claudius's reign. In the early months of the second year, shortly before his death, I received the following four:

51. On Evil (I. 8).
52. Whether the Stars have Causal Operation (II. 3).
53. On the Animate and the Man (I. 1).
54. On the First Good; or, On Happiness (I. 8).

Adding these nine to the forty-five of the first and second sets, we have a total of fifty-four treatises.

The tractates vary in power according to the time of writing—whether in early manhood, his vigorous prime, or when his constitution was worn out. The first twenty-one pieces manifest a slighter capacity, as his talent was not yet matured to its full strength. The twenty-four produced in his middle period display the utmost reach of his powers and, except for the short treatises among them, attain the highest perfection. The last nine were written when his mental strength was already waning, and of these, the last four show even less vigor than the five preceding them.

<summary>Porphyry completes the catalog of Plotinus's fifty-four treatises, noting those written during his final years and sent to Sicily. He categorizes the works into three periods, observing that the middle period represents the peak of Plotinus's intellectual power.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Plotinus, Galienus, Claudius, treatises, Sicily, Hypostases, Providence, chronological catalog, intellectual development</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s account of Plotinus’s life, moving from a catalog of his writings to a description of his circle of students and close associates.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

7. Plotinus had a large following. Notable among the more zealous students, who were truly devoted to philosophy, was Amelius of Tuscany, whose family name was Gentilianus. Amelius preferred to call himself Amerius, changing L for R, because, as he explained, it suited him better to be named from *Amereia* (Unification) than from *Ameleia* (Indifference).

The group also included one Paulinus, a doctor of Scythopolis, whom Amelius used to call Mikkalos in allusion to his blundering habit of mind.

Among closer personal friends was Eustochius of Alexandria, also a doctor, who came to know Plotinus toward the end of his life and attended him until his death; Eustochius consecrated himself exclusively to Plotinus’s system and became a true philosopher.

Then there was Zoticus, both a critic and a poet, who amended the text of Antimachus’s works and is the author of an exquisite poem on the Atlantis story; his sight failed, and he died a little before Plotinus, as did Paulinus.

Another friend was Zethos, an Arabian by descent, who married a daughter of Ammonius’s friend Theodosius. Zethos, too, was a doctor; Plotinus was deeply attached to him and was always trying to divert him from the political career in which he held a high position. Plotinus was on the most familiar terms with him and used to stay with him at his country estate, six miles from Minturnae, a property which had formerly belonged to Castricius Firmus.

Castricius was surpassed by none of the group in his appreciation of the finer side of life; he venerated Plotinus; he devoted himself in the most faithful comradeship to Amelius in every need, and was in all matters as loyal to me as though I were his own brother.

This was another example of a politician venerating the philosopher. There were also among Plotinus’s listeners quite a few members of the Senate, among whom Marcellus Orontius and Sabinillus showed the greatest diligence in philosophical studies.

Another Senator, Rogatianus, advanced to such detachment from political ambitions that he gave up all his property, dismissed all his

<meta>This page continues the list of Plotinus’s associates, specifically detailing the transformation of the politician Rogatianus and the background of Serapion, before moving into a description of Plotinus’s personal writing habits and Porphyry’s role as his editor.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>PORPHYRY</header>

slaves, renounced every honor, and, on the point of taking up his praetorship—with the lictors already at the door—refused to come out or to have anything to do with the office. He even abandoned his own house, spending his time here and there at his friends' and acquaintances' houses, sleeping and eating with them and eating only one meal a day. He had suffered from gout and had to be carried in a chair, but this new regime of abstinence and self-denial restored his health: he had been unable to stretch out his hands, but he came to use them as freely as men who live by manual labor. Plotinus took a great liking to Rogatianus and frequently praised him very highly, holding him up as a model to those aiming for the philosophical life.

Then there was Serapion, an Alexandrian, who began life as a professional orator and later took up the study of philosophy, but was never able to conquer the vices of greed and usury.

I myself, Porphyry of Tyre, was one of Plotinus' very closest friends, and it was to me that he entrusted the task of revising his writings.

### 8.

Such revision was necessary: Plotinus could not bear to go back over his work even for a single re-reading; and indeed, the condition of his eyesight would scarcely allow it. His handwriting was messy; he joined words incorrectly; he cared nothing about spelling; his only concern was for the idea. In these habits, to our general surprise, he remained unchanged to the very end.

He used to work out his design mentally from start to finish; when he came to set down his ideas, he wrote out in one continuous flow all he had stored in his mind as though he were copying from a book.

If interrupted, perhaps, by someone entering on business, he never lost hold of his plan; he was able to meet all the demands of the conversation and still keep his own train of thought clearly before him. When he was free again, he never looked over what he had previously written—his eyesight, as mentioned, did not allow for such re-reading—but he connected what was to follow as if no distraction had occurred.

<sig>c</sig>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Plotinus, shifting from a description of his writing habits and Porphyry’s role as editor to an account of his personal discipline, his role as a guardian for orphans, and his reputation in Rome.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

Thus he was able to live at once within himself and for others; he never relaxed his inner focus except during sleep; and even his sleep was kept light by a self-denial that often prevented him from taking even a piece of bread, and by this unbroken concentration upon his own highest nature.

### 9.

Several women were greatly attached to him, among them Gemina, in whose house he lived, and her daughter, also called Gemina after her mother, and Amphiclea, the wife of Ariston, son of Iamblichus; all three devoted themselves diligently to philosophy.

Many men and women of high rank, as death approached, had left their sons and daughters, along with all their property, in his care, feeling that with Plotinus as a guardian, the children would be in holy hands. His house, therefore, was filled with boys and girls, among them Polemon, in whose education he took such an interest that he often heard the boy recite verses of his own composition.

He always found time for those who came to submit financial reports regarding the children’s property, and he looked closely at the accuracy of the accounts: “Until the young people take up philosophy,” he used to say, “their fortunes and revenues must be kept intact for them.” And yet all this labor and thought over the worldly interests of so many people never interrupted, during his waking hours, his focus on the Supreme.

He was gentle and always available to those who had even the slightest acquaintance with him. After spending twenty-six whole years in Rome, acting also as an arbitrator in many disputes, he had never made an enemy of any citizen.

### 10.

Among those practicing philosophy in Rome was one Olympius, an Alexandrian, who had been a pupil of Ammonius for a short time.

This man’s jealous envy showed itself in continual insolence, and

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s biography of Plotinus, specifically describing the failed magical attacks by his rival Olympius and the revelation of Plotinus’s divine nature through an Egyptian priest’s ritual.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>PORPHYRY</header>

Finally, he grew so bitter that he even ventured into sorcery, seeking to crush Plotinus with astrological spells. But he found his experiments recoiling on him, and he confessed to his associates that Plotinus possessed "a mighty soul, so powerful as to be able to hurl every assault back upon those who sought his ruin." Plotinus had felt the attempt and declared that at that moment Olympius' "limbs were convulsed and his body shrivelling like a money-bag pulled tight." Olympius, perceiving after several attempts that he was endangering himself rather than Plotinus, stopped.

In fact, Plotinus possessed by birth something more than is granted to other men. An Egyptian priest who had arrived in Rome and, through some friend, had been presented to the philosopher, became eager to display his powers to him, and he offered to summon a visible manifestation of Plotinus' presiding spirit. Plotinus readily consented, and the summoning was made in the Temple of Isis, the only place, they say, which the Egyptian could find pure in Rome.

At the summons a Divinity appeared, not a being of the lower ranks of spirits, and the Egyptian exclaimed: "You are exceptionally blessed; the guiding spirit within you is not of the lower order, but is a God." It was not possible, however, to question or even to observe this God any further, for the priest's assistant, who had been holding the birds to prevent them from flying away, strangled them, whether out of jealousy or in terror. Thus Plotinus had for an indwelling spirit a Being of the more divine degree, and he kept his own divine spirit unceasingly focused upon 그 inner presence. It was this preoccupation that led him to write his treatise upon *Our Tutelary Spirit*, an essay explaining the differences among spirit-guides.

Amelius was careful to observe the day of the New Moon and other holy days, and once asked Plotinus to join in such a celebration. Plotinus refused: "It is for those Beings to come to me, not for me to go to them."

What was in his mind in such a noble statement we could not explain to ourselves, and we did not dare to ask him.

<summary>Plotinus's spiritual power causes a rival's magical attacks to backfire and is confirmed when an Egyptian priest summons his presiding spirit, revealing it to be a God. This divine connection leads Plotinus to view himself as someone whom spiritual beings should visit, rather than someone who must seek them out through ritual.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s biography of Plotinus, moving from the account of his divine indwelling spirit to his remarkable insight into human character and his influence at the imperial court.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

## 11.

He had a remarkable insight into character.

Once, a valuable necklace was stolen from Chione, who was living in honorable widowhood with her children in the same house as Plotinus. The servants were called before him; he scrutinized them all, then indicated one: “This man is the thief.” The man was whipped but for some time persisted in his denial; finally, however, he confessed and restored the necklace.

Plotinus also foretold the future of each of the children in the household. For instance, when questioned as to Polemon’s character and destiny, he said: “He will be amorous and short-lived”; and so it proved to be.

I myself, at one period, had formed the intention of ending my life. Plotinus discerned my purpose. He came unexpectedly to my house where I had secluded myself and told me that my decision did not spring from reason but from mere melancholy, and he advised me to leave Rome. I obeyed and left for Sicily, which I chose because I heard that one Probus, a man of scholarly repute, was living there not far from Lilybæum. Thus I was induced to abandon my first intention, but I was prevented from being with Plotinus between that time and his death.

## 12.

The Emperor Galienus and his wife Salonina greatly honored and venerated Plotinus, who thought to turn their friendly feeling to some good purpose. In Campania there had once stood, according to tradition, a City of Philosophers, which was then a ruin. Plotinus asked the Emperor to rebuild this city and to grant the surrounding district to the newly founded state. The population was to live under Plato’s laws; the city was to be called Platonopolis, and Plotinus undertook to settle down there with his associates. He would have had his way without further delay, but opposition at court—prompted by jealousy, spite, or some such petty motive—put an end to the plan.

<summary>Porphyry describes Plotinus's keen ability to judge character and predict the future, his intervention in Porphyry's own suicide attempt, and his failed attempt to establish a utopian "City of Philosophers" called Platonopolis.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Chione, Polemon, Porphyry, suicide, Sicily, Lilybæum, Emperor Galienus, Salonina, Platonopolis, Campania, Plato's laws</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s account of Plotinus, detailing his teaching style, his intellectual habits, and the philosophical influences found in his work.</meta>

<margin>13.</margin>
At the meetings he showed the most remarkable power of going to the heart of a subject, whether in exposition or in explanation, and his phrasing was appropriate; but he made mistakes in certain words; for example, he said “anamnemisketai” for “anamimnesketai”—exactly the same kind of errors he committed in his writing.

When he was speaking, his intellect visibly lit up his face: always possessing a charming presence, he became still more engaging at these times; a slight moisture gathered on his forehead; he radiated kindness.

He was always as ready to listen to objections as he was powerful in answering them. At one time I myself kept questioning him for three days about how the soul is associated with the body, and he continued explaining; a man named Thomasius entered in the midst of our discussions; the visitor was more interested in the general direction of the system than in specific points, and said he wished to hear Plotinus explain some theory as he would in a formal treatise, but that he could not endure Porphyry’s questions and answers: Plotinus asked, “But if we cannot first solve the difficulties Porphyry raises, what could go into the treatise?”

<margin>14.</margin>
In style, Plotinus is concise, dense with thought, brief, more full of ideas than of words, most often expressing himself with a passionate inspiration. He followed his own path rather than that of tradition, but in his writings both the Stoic and Peripatetic doctrines are incorporated; Aristotle’s <term>Metaphysics</term>, especially, is condensed in them, almost in its entirety.

He had a thorough theoretical knowledge of Geometry, Mechanics, Optics, and Music, though it was not in his nature to engage practically with these subjects.

At the meetings he used to have treatises by various authors read aloud—among the Platonists these might be Severus or Cronius, Numenius, Caius, or Atticus; and among the Peripatetics, Aspasius, Alexander, Adrastus, or some such writer, as the occasion required. But it was far from his way to follow any of these authors blindly; he took

<keywords>Plotinus, Porphyry, teaching style, soul and body, Aristotle, Metaphysics, Stoicism, Peripatetics, Platonism, philosophy, Thomasius</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's biography of Plotinus, focusing on his intellectual agility, his reactions to contemporary scholars, and his involvement in academic debates.</meta>

# PLOTINUS

a personal, original view, applying Ammonius's method to the investigation of every problem.

He was quick to absorb information; a few words were enough for him to clarify the significance of some profound theory and thus move on. After hearing Longinus's work *On Causes* and his *Philarchaios*, he remarked: “Longinus is a man of letters, but in no sense a philosopher.”

One day Origen came to the lecture room; Plotinus blushed deeply and was about to bring his lecture to an end. When Origen begged him to continue, he said: “The enthusiasm dies down when the speaker feels that his listeners have nothing to learn from him.”

15.

Once, on Plato's feast day, I read a poem, “The Sacred Marriage”; my piece was full of mystic doctrine expressed in veiled words and was written in a tone of enthusiasm. Someone exclaimed: “Porphyry has gone mad”; Plotinus said to me so that everyone could hear: “You have shown yourself to be at once a poet, a philosopher, and a <term>hierophant</term>.” <note>A priest or interpreter of sacred mysteries.</note>

The orator Diophanes one day read a defense of the *Alcibiades* from Plato's *Banquet* and argued that the pupil, for the sake of advancement in virtue, should submit to the teacher without reserve, even to the point of sexual relations. Plotinus started to get up several times to leave the room but forced himself to remain. When the company broke up, he directed me to write a refutation. Diophanes refused to lend me his speech, and I had to rely on my memory of his argument; but my refutation, delivered before the same audience, delighted Plotinus so much that during the very reading he repeatedly quoted: “So strike and be a light to men.”

When Eubulus, the Platonic Successor, wrote from Athens sending treatises on some questions in Platonism, Plotinus put the writings into my hands with instructions to examine them and report back to him.

He paid some attention to the principles of astronomy, though he did not study the subject very deeply from the mathematical side. He went more searchingly into horoscopy; once he was convinced

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's account of Plotinus’s intellectual battles, specifically his opposition to Gnostic influences and accusations of plagiarism concerning Numenius.</meta>

<header>PORPHYRY</header>

that its results were not to be trusted, he had no hesitation in frequently attacking the system both during the lectures and in his writings.

16.

Many Christians of this period—among them sectarians who had abandoned the old philosophy, men from the schools of Adelphius and Aquilinus—had obtained works by Alexander of Lydia, Philocomus, Demostratus, and Lydus. They also displayed "Revelations" bearing the names of Zoroaster, Zostrianus, Nikotheus, Allogenes, Mesus, and others of that sort. In this way, they deceived many, being deceived themselves first; according to them, Plato had failed to penetrate into the depth of <term>Intellectual Being</term>.

Plotinus frequently attacked their position during the lectures and finally wrote the treatise which I have titled *Against the Gnostics*; he left it to those of us in his circle the task of examining the points he himself passed over. Amelius proceeded as far as a fortieth treatise in his refutation of the book of Zostrianus. I myself have shown on many counts that the Zoroastrian volume is a modern forgery, concocted by the sectarians to pretend that the doctrines they had embraced were those of that ancient sage.

17.

Some of the Greeks began to accuse Plotinus of appropriating the ideas of Numenius.

Amelius, being informed of this charge by the Stoic and Platonist Trypho, challenged it in a treatise which he titled *The Difference between the Doctrines of Plotinus and Numenius*. He dedicated the work to me under the name of Basileus (or King). This really is my name; it is equivalent to Porphyry (Purple-robed) and translates the name I carry in my own tongue; for I am called Malchos, like my father, and "Malchos" would be "Basileus" in Greek. Longinus, in dedicating his work *On Impulse* to Cleodamus and myself, addressed us as "Cleodamus and Malchus," just as Numenius translated the Latin "Maximus" into its Greek equivalent "Megalos."

<summary>Porphyry describes Plotinus's efforts to refute Gnostic sectarians and their forged revelations, while Amelius defends Plotinus against charges of plagiarizing Numenius. Porphyry also explains the etymology of his own name, Malchos, and its Greek translation, Basileus.</summary>

<keywords>Plotinus, Gnostics, Porphyry, Amelius, Numenius, Zoroaster, Zostrianus, Intellectual Being, Malchos, Basileus</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's biography of Plotinus, specifically addressing the defenses written by his students against accusations that his philosophy was merely copied from Numenius of Apamea.</meta>

<header>PLOTINUS</header>

Here follows Amelius' letter:

> Amelius to Basileus, with all good wishes.
> 
> You have been, as you put it, pestered by the persistent claim that our friend’s doctrine is derived from Numenius of Apamea.
> 
> Now, if it were only for those "illustrious" individuals who spread this charge, you can be very sure I would never say a word in reply. It is clear enough that they are motivated solely by that famous and astonishing gift for talk of theirs when they claim, at one moment, that he is an idle babbler, next that he is a plagiarist, and finally that he bases the universe on the lowliest of beings. Clearly, in all this, we have nothing but mockery and insults.
> 
> However, your judgment has convinced me that we should take this opportunity first to provide ourselves with a useful record of the doctrines we have embraced, and second to promote a more complete knowledge of the system—celebrated though it has long been—to the glory of our friend, a man as great as Plotinus.
> 
> Therefore, I now bring you the promised reply, written, as you yourself know, in three days. You must judge it with reasonable leniency; this is not a formal or elaborate defense written point-by-point in response to the written indictment. I have simply set down my recollections of our frequent discussions as they occurred to me. You will also admit that it is by no means easy to grasp the meaning of a writer like Plotinus—who is now being accused for the views we also hold—because he varies the terms he uses to express the same idea.
> 
> If I have misrepresented any essential part of the doctrine, I trust your good nature to correct me. I am reminded of the line from the tragedy: *A busy man and far from our master's teachings, I must need correct and recant.* Consider how much I wish to please you. Wishing you good health.

18.

This letter seemed worth including to show not only that some contemporary judgment declared Plotinus to be showing off based on Numenius’s ideas, but that he was even looked down upon as a mere weaver of words.

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's account of his time with Plotinus, focusing on his own initial resistance to the master's teachings and his eventual conversion to Plotinus's philosophical system.</meta>

<header>PORPHYRY</header>

The fact is that these people did not understand his teaching: he was entirely free from all the inflated pomp of a professor. His lectures had the air of conversation, and he never forced the severely logical framework of his thesis upon his listeners.

I myself, when I first heard him, had the same experience. It led me to oppose his doctrine in a paper in which I tried to show that the <term>Intelligibles</term> exist outside of the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>. He had Amelius read my work to him; at the end, he smiled and said: “You must clear up these difficulties, Amelius; Porphyry does not understand our position.” Amelius wrote a fairly long tract, “In Answer to Porphyry’s Objections”; I wrote a reply to that reply; Amelius replied to my reply. At my third attempt I managed—though even then with difficulty—to grasp the doctrine. Only then was I converted; I wrote a recantation and read it before the circle. From that time on, I put faith in Plotinus’ writings and tried to stir in the master himself the ambition of organizing his doctrine and setting it down in a more extended form. Amelius, too, was encouraged to write more under my prompting.

19.
Longinus’ opinion of Plotinus, formed largely by the information I had given him in my letters, can be gathered from the following extract from one of his letters to me. He is asking me to leave Sicily and join him in Phoenicia, and to bring Plotinus’ works with me. He says:

> “And send them at your convenience or, better yet, bring them; for I can never stop urging you to prioritize the road toward us over any other. If there is no better reason—and what intellectual gain can you expect from a visit to us?—at least there are old acquaintances and the mild climate, which would do you good given the weak state of health you report. Whatever else you may be expecting, do not hope for anything new of my own, or even for the earlier works which you tell me you have lost; for there is a sad shortage of copyists here. I assure you it has taken me all this time to complete my set of Plotinus, and it was only accomplished by pulling my scribe away from all his routine work and keeping him steadily at this one task.”

He also includes a letter from the scholar Longinus, who requests copies of Plotinus's works while inviting Porphyry to Phoenicia.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the letter from Longinus to Porphyry, where Longinus expresses his high regard for Plotinus's work despite his initial skepticism and his difficulties with the quality of the manuscripts he received.</meta>

<header>PLOTINUS</header>

> I think that now, with what you have sent me, I have everything, though it is in a very imperfect state because the manuscript is extremely faulty. I had expected our friend Amelius to correct the copying errors, but he clearly had something better to do. The copies are quite useless to me; I have been particularly eager to examine the treatises on the Soul and on the <term>Authentic-Existent</term>, and these are exactly the ones that are most corrupted. It would be a great satisfaction to me if you would send me accurate transcripts for comparison and return—though again I suggest that you do not just send them but come in person, bringing me the correct copies of these treatises and any that Amelius may have skipped. I have been careful to acquire all the works that have reached me; how could I be satisfied not to own all the writings of a man so worthy of the deepest respect?
> 
> I repeat what I have often said, both in your presence and in your absence—as on that occasion when you were at Tyre—that while much of the theory does not convince me, I am still filled with admiration and delight at the general character of the work, the man's profound thinking, and his philosophical handling of problems. In my judgment, researchers must rank Plotinus’s work among the very highest class.”

<margin>20.</margin> This long quotation from the most perceptive critic of our time—a writer who has passed judgment on nearly all his contemporaries—serves to show the high estimate he eventually placed on Plotinus. Initially, misled by ignorant rumors, he had held a poor opinion of him.

Incidentally, his idea that the transcripts Amelius sent him were faulty arose from his own misunderstanding of Plotinus’s style and phrasing; if there were ever any accurate copies, these were they, being faithful reproductions of the author’s own manuscript.

Another passage from a work by Longinus, dealing with Amelius, Plotinus, and other <term>metaphysicians</term> of the day, must be included here to provide a complete view of the opinion formed about these philosophers by this most authoritative and thorough of critics. The work was

<meta>This page continues the introduction to the life of Plotinus by Porphyry, specifically quoting Longinus's preface to his work addressing Plotinus and Amelius.</meta>

<header>PORPHYRY</header>

entitled *On the End: in Answer to Plotinus and Gentilianus Amelius*. It opens with the following preface:

> In our time, Marcellus, there have been many philosophers—especially in our youth—for there is a strange scarcity of them at present. When I was a boy, my parents' long journeys gave me the opportunity to see all the better-known teachers; and in later life, those who still lived became known to me as my visits to various cities and peoples brought me where they happened to live.
>
> Some of these undertook the labor of developing their theories in formal works and so have bequeathed to the future the means of profiting from their services. Others thought they had done enough when they had convinced their own immediate listeners of the truth of their theories.
>
> First, among those who have written:
>
> Among the Platonists, there are Euclides, Democritus, Proclinus the philosopher of the Troad, and the two who still practice philosophy at Rome, Plotinus and his friend Gentilianus Amelius. Among the Stoics, there are Themistocles and Phoibion, and the two who flourished only a little while ago, Annius and Medius. And there is the Peripatetic, Heliodorus of Alexandria.
>
> As for those who have not written, there are among the Platonists Ammonius and Origen, two teachers whose lectures I myself attended for a long period—men greatly surpassing their contemporaries in mental power; and there are the Platonic Successors at Athens, Theodorus and Eubulus.
>
> No doubt some writing of a metaphysical nature stands to the credit of this group: Origen wrote on Spirit-Beings; Eubulus commented on both the *Philebus* and *Gorgias*, and examined the objections raised by Aristotle to Plato's *Republic*; but this is not enough to classify either of them with systematic authors. This was a side-interest; authorship was not the main plan of their careers.
>
> Among Stoic teachers who refrained from writing, we have Herminus and Lysimachus, and the two living at Athens, Musonius and Athenæus; among Peripatetics, there are Ammonius and Ptolemæus.

<summary>Longinus provides a survey of the notable philosophers of his era, categorizing them by their schools of thought and whether they committed their teachings to formal writing or preferred oral instruction.</summary>
<keywords>Longinus, Platonists, Stoics, Peripatetics, Plotinus, Origen, Ammonius, philosophical schools, written vs. oral tradition</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues Longinus's preface, evaluating the philosophical writers of his time and contrasting those who merely compiled earlier works with the original authorship of Plotinus and Amelius.</meta>

<header>PLOTINUS</header>

The last two were the most accomplished scholars of their time, Ammonius especially being unsurpassed in the breadth of his learning; but neither produced any systematic work. We have from them only verses and formal speeches; I do not think these were preserved with their consent. They did not concern themselves with the formal statement of their doctrine, and it is unlikely they would wish to be known in later times by compositions of such a trivial nature.

To return to the writers; some of them, such as Euclides, Democritus, and Proclinus, confined themselves to the mere compilation and transcription of passages from earlier authorities. Others diligently worked through various minor points in the investigations of the ancients and put together books dealing with the same subjects. Such were Annius, Medius, and Phoibion, the last especially choosing to be distinguished for his style rather than for systematic thinking. Heliodorus must be ranked in the same class; his writings contribute nothing to the organization of the thought which he inherited from the teaching of earlier workers.

Plotinus and Gentilianus Amelius alone display the true spirit of authorship; they treat a great number of questions and apply a method of their own to the treatment.

Plotinus, it seems, set the principles of Pythagoras and Plato in a clearer light than anyone before him; on the same subjects, Numenius, Cronius, Moderatus, and Thrasyllus fall far short of him in precision and fullness. Amelius set himself to follow in Plotinus’ steps and adopted most of Plotinus’ opinions; his method, however, was diffuse, and unlike his friend, he indulges in an extravagance of explanation.

Only these two seem worth study to me. What profit can anyone expect from troubling themselves with the works of any of the others while neglecting the originals upon which they drew? Content with setting the most generally adopted theories side by side and distinguishing the better from the worse, they bring us nothing of their own—not even a new argument, much less a leading idea.

My own method has been different; for example, when I replied to Gentilianus regarding Plato’s treatment of Justice, and in a review I under-

<summary>Longinus critiques his contemporary philosophers, dismissing most as mere compilers or stylicians while praising Plotinus and Amelius for their original and systematic contributions. He specifically highlights Plotinus's superior clarity in explaining Pythagorean and Platonic principles compared to earlier thinkers.</summary>

<keywords>Longinus, Plotinus, Gentilianus Amelius, Ammonius, Plato, Pythagoras, authorship, Neoplatonism, philosophical method, Hellenistic philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the excerpt from Longinus' preface, where he evaluates the contemporary philosophical landscape and specifically addresses his interactions with Plotinus’ students, including Porphyry (Basileus of Tyre) and Amelius.</meta>

# PORPHYRY

...regarding Plotinus' theory of the Idea. This latter work took the form of a reply to Basileus of Tyre, a friend to me as well as to them. He had preferred Plotinus' system to mine and had written several works in the style of his master, including a treatise supporting Plotinus' theory of the Idea over the one I taught. I endeavored—and I believe not unsuccessfully—to show that his change of mind was a mistake.

In these two essays, I have covered the doctrines of this school extensively, as I also did in my *Letter to Amelius*. Despite the simple title I chose, that letter is as long as a book, being a reply to a treatise he sent me from Rome titled "*On Plotinus' Philosophic Method*."

<margin>21.</margin>
This preface leaves no doubt about Longinus' final verdict: he ranks Plotinus and Amelius above all authors of his time because of the many questions they discuss. He credits them with an original method of investigation. In his judgment, they certainly did not take their system from Numenius or prioritize his opinions; instead, they followed the Pythagorean and Platonic schools. Finally, he declares that the writings of Numenius, Cronius, Moderatus, and Thrasyllus are far inferior in precision and depth to those of Plotinus.

Note, incidentally, that while Amelius is described as following in Plotinus’ footsteps, it is noted that his natural wordiness led him to delight in an excessive detail that was foreign to his master. In the reference to me—even though I was then only at the start of my association with Plotinus—Longinus says: "Basileus of Tyre, my friend as well as theirs, who has written extensively, has taken Plotinus as his model." Thus, Longinus recognizes that I completely avoided Amelius’ unphilosophical wordiness and adopted Plotinus’ style as my standard.

Such a judgment on the value of Plotinus’ work, coming from such a great authority—the leading critic of that time and this—must certainly carry weight. I might also remark that if I had been able to meet with him during the visit he proposed, he would not have written to oppose doctrines that he had not yet thoroughly understood.

<summary>Porphyry discusses Longinus' high regard for Plotinus and Amelius over other philosophers like Numenius, noting their original methods and Plotinus' superior precision. Longinus also distinguishes Porphyry’s clear style from Amelius’ wordiness, though he initially disagreed with some of Plotinus' doctrines.</summary>

22. But why talk—to use Hesiod’s phrase—"About Oak and Rock"? If we are to accept the evidence of the wise—and who could be wiser than a God? And here the witness is the same God who spoke with truth:
> “I have numbered the sands and taken the measure of the sea; I understand the mute and hear where there has been no speech.”

Apollo was consulted by Amelius, who desired to learn where Plotinus’ soul had gone. And Apollo, who spoke that great praise of Socrates—"Of all men, Socrates is the wisest"—you shall hear what a full and lofty oracle Apollo delivered regarding Plotinus.

> I raise an undying song to the memory of a gentle friend, a hymn of praise woven to the honey-sweet tones of my lyre under the touch of the golden plectrum.

>

>

<margin>23.</margin>
Good and kindly, exceptionally gentle and engaging: this is how the oracle presents him, and so in fact we found him. Sleeplessly alert—as Apollo tells—pure of soul, ever striving towards the divine which he loved with all his being, he worked hard to free himself and rise above the bitter waves of this blood-drenched life: and this is why to Plotinus—

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's commentary on the Delphic Oracle's description of Plotinus, specifically addressing his mystical experiences and his reception in the afterlife, before transitioning to Porphyry's editorial work.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

God-like and often raising himself through meditation and the methods Plato teaches in the *Banquet* toward the first and all-transcendent God—that God appeared, the God throned above the Intellectual-Principle and the entire Intellectual-Sphere.

> “To Plotinus, the Goal was shown to be ever near”: for the Goal, the single end of his life, was to become united, to approach the God who is over all. Four times during the period I spent with him, he achieved this Goal, not by mere latent potential but by the ineffable Act.

I, Porphyry, also declare that during my sixty-eighth year, I too was once admitted to this God and entered into Union.

We are told that often, when he was wandering from the way, the Gods set him on the true path again by pouring down a dense shaft of light before him. Here we are to understand that in his writing he was overseen and guided by divine powers.

> “In this sleepless vision, both within and without,” the oracle says, “your eyes have seen many beautiful sights not granted to all who follow the path of philosophy.” Human contemplation may sometimes be more than human, but compared to the True-Knowledge of the Gods, wonderful as it is, it can never plunge into the depths that their divine vision fathoms.

So far, the Oracle recounts what Plotinus accomplished and the heights he reached while still in the body. Once freed from the body, we are told how he entered the celestial circle where all is friendship, tender delight, happiness, and loving union with God. There, Minos, Rhadamanthus, and Aeacus—the sons of God—are enthroned as judges of souls—not, however, to bring him to judgment, but to welcome him into their company, to which spirits pleasing to the Gods are invited: Plato, Pythagoras, and all those in the Choir of Immortal Love. There, the blessed spirits have their home and live in days made happy by the Gods.

<sig>E</sig>

...imposed upon me during his lifetime, and I had pledged myself to him and to the circle to carry it out.

I decided that for these treatises—which, like these, had been published without any regard for logical sequence—it was best to ignore the chronological order.

Apollodorus the Athenian edited the collected works of the comedy writer Epicharmus into ten volumes; Andronicus the Peripatetic classified the works of Aristotle and Theophrastus by subject, grouping together the discussions of related topics. I have followed a similar plan.

I had fifty-four treatises before me. I divided them into six sets of nine, an arrangement that pleased me because of the fortunate combination of the perfect number six with the nines. To each such "ennead," I assigned a general subject, beginning with the themes that are the easiest to understand.

The **FIRST ENNEAD**, using this method, contains the treatises with a more ethical focus:

1. On the Living Being and the Man.
2. On the Virtues.
3. On Dialectic.
4. On Happiness.
5. Whether Happiness depends on the Extension of Time.
6. On Beauty.
7. On the Primary Good and Secondary forms of Good.
8. On Evil.
9. On the Rational Withdrawal from Life.

The **SECOND ENNEAD**, following the strictly ethical First, focuses on physics, containing the discussions on the world and everything that belongs to the world:

1. On the World.
2. On Circular Motion.
3. On the Stars.
4. On the Two Orders of Matter.

These first three Enneads make up one self-contained section in my arrangement. The treatise on Our Tutelary Spirit is placed in the Third Ennead because this Spirit is not discussed as it exists in itself, and the essay’s main content belongs to the category dealing with the origin of man. Similar reasons led to the inclusion of the treatise on Love in this set. The work on Time and Eternity is placed in this Third Ennead because of its treatment of Time; the one "On Nature, Contemplation, and The One" is included because of the discussion of Nature contained within it.

Following the two Enneads dealing with the world comes the **FOURTH ENNEAD**, which contains the treatises dealing with the Soul:

1. On the Essence of the Soul (I.).
2. On the Essence of the Soul (II.).

To some of the treatises I have also added commentaries—occasionally, as friends asked for clarification on various points—and finally, for all the treatises except the one on Beauty, which was not at hand, I have written Summaries that follow the chronological order. In this section of my work, besides the Summaries, there will be found Developments; the numbering of these also follows the chronological order.

Now I have only to go once more through the entire work, check the punctuation, and correct any verbal errors; the reader will discover for himself whatever else has required my attention.

<meta>Following Porphyry’s introduction and explanation of the editorial arrangement, this page marks the beginning of the first tractate of the First Ennead, in which Plotinus begins his investigation into the nature of the soul and its relation to the human experience.</meta>

# THE FIRST ENNEAD

## FIRST TRACTATE

### THE ANIMATE AND THE MAN

1.
Pleasure and distress, fear and courage, desire and aversion—where do these affections and experiences have their seat?

Clearly, it is either in the Soul alone, in the Soul as it employs the body, or in some third entity derived from both. And for this third entity, there are again two possible modes: it might be either a blend or a distinct form resulting from the blending.

Whatever applies to these affections also applies to any acts, whether physical or mental, that spring from them.

We must, therefore, examine <term>discursive reason</term> and the ordinary mental activity directed toward objects of sense, and inquire whether these share the same seat as the affections and experiences, or perhaps sometimes have one seat and sometimes another.

We must also consider our acts of <term>Intellection</term>, their manner and their seat.

And this very principle of examination, which investigates and decides these matters, must be brought to light.

First, what is the seat of <term>Sense Perception</term>? This is the obvious starting point, since the affections and experiences are either sensations of some kind or, at least, never occur apart from sensation.

2.
This first inquiry obliges us to consider at the outset the nature of the <term>Soul</term>—that is, whether a distinction should be made between the Soul and the <term>Essential Soul</term> (between an individual soul and the Soul-Kind in itself).

<summary>Plotinus begins an investigation into the location of human emotions, sensations, and reasoning, questioning whether they reside in the soul, the body, or a combination of both. He establishes that understanding these experiences requires first defining the nature of the Soul and its distinction from the "Essential Soul."</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, First Ennead, Soul, Essential Soul, Intellection, Sense Perception, discursive reason, philosophy of mind, dualism, affections</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>Following the initial inquiry into whether the soul or a composite entity experiences emotions, this page examines the consequences of defining the Soul as an unchanging Ideal-Form versus a composite recipient of experiences.</meta>

->PLOTINUS<-

If such a distinction holds, then the Soul (in man) is some sort of a composite; we may then agree that it is a recipient and—if reason permits—that all the affections and experiences truly have their seat in the Soul, along with every state and mood, both good and bad alike.

But if the human Soul and the <term>Essential Soul</term> are one and the same, then the Soul will be an <term>Ideal-Form</term> that does not receive any of the activities it imparts to another kind of being, but instead possesses within itself its own natural activity, which Reason manifests.

If this is so, then indeed we may think of the Soul as immortal—if the immortal and imperishable must be unaffected, giving out something of itself but taking nothing from the outside, except for what it receives from the <term>Existents</term> prior to itself. Because these Existents are nobler, the Soul cannot be separated from them.

Now, what could bring fear to a nature so unreceptive to everything external? Fear requires feeling. Nor is there a place for courage: courage implies the presence of danger. And such desires as are satisfied by the filling or emptying of the body must belong to something very different from the Soul—only to that which allows for being filled and emptied.

And how could the Soul allow itself to be mixed with anything? An essential being is not mixed. Or how could anything foreign intrude? If it did, the Soul would be seeking the destruction of its own nature. Pain must be equally far from it. And grief—how or for what could it grieve? Whatever possesses Existence is supremely free, dwelling unchangeably within its own unique nature. Can any increase bring joy where nothing, not even anything good, can be added? What such an <term>Existent</term> is, it remains unchangeably.

Thus, assuredly, <term>Sense-Perception</term>, <term>Discursive-Reasoning</term>, and all our ordinary <term>mentation</term> are foreign to the Soul; for sensation is a process of receiving—whether of an Ideal-Form or of an unaffected body—and reasoning and all ordinary mental action deal with sensation.

The question still remains to be examined regarding the <term>intellections</term>—whether these are to be assigned to the Soul—and as to

<summary>Plotinus argues that if the Soul is an unchanging Ideal-Form, it must be immortal and unaffected by external sensations, pains, or desires. This leads to the conclusion that sense-perception and discursive reasoning, which rely on external input, may be foreign to the Soul's true nature.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Soul, Essential Soul, Ideal-Form, Existents, Sense-Perception, Discursive-Reasoning, mentation, intellections, immortality, impassivity</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>Following the discussion of the Soul as an unchanging Ideal-Form, this page explores the relationship between the Soul and the body, investigating how an agent uses an instrument without necessarily sharing its experiences.</meta>

# I. 1. 3] THE ANIMATE AND THE MAN 31
<meta>pencil checkmark below first paragraph</meta>

Pure pleasure (pleasure apart from sense)—whether this belongs to the Soul in its solitary state.

3.
We may consider the Soul as being in the body—whether it is placed above it or actually within it—since the association of the two forms the single thing called the living organism, the <term>Animate</term>.

Now, from this relationship, where the Soul uses the body as an instrument, it does not follow that the Soul must share the body's experiences: a man does not himself feel all the experiences of the tools with which he works.

It may be objected that the Soul must, however, have sense perception, since the use of its instrument must acquaint it with external conditions, and such knowledge comes by way of the senses. Thus, it will be argued, the eyes are the instrument of seeing, and seeing may bring distress to the soul; therefore, the Soul may feel sorrow, pain, and every other affection that belongs to the body. From this, desire will also arise, as the Soul seeks the repair of its instrument.

But we ask: how, possibly, can these affections pass from body to Soul? A body may communicate qualities or conditions to another body; but can a body communicate them to a Soul? If something happens to A, does that make it happen to B? As long as we have an agent and an instrument, there are two distinct entities; if the Soul uses the body, it is separate from it.

But apart from philosophical separation, how does the Soul stand in relation to the body?

Clearly, there is a combination. For this, several modes are possible. There might be a complete coalescence: the Soul might be interwoven through the body; or it might be an <term>Ideal-Form</term> detached, or an <term>Ideal-Form</term> in governing contact like a pilot; or there might be one part of the Soul detached and another part in contact, with the separate part being the agent or user and the joined part ranking with the instrument or thing used.

In this last case, it will be the double task of philosophy to direct this lower Soul toward the higher agent; and except insofar as the

<vocab>Soul, Animate, Sense-Perception, Ideal-Form, philosophy</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the relationship between the Soul and the body, questioning whether the Soul experiences the body's physical affections or merely uses the body as a separate instrument. It proposes several models for their union, such as the Soul acting as a pilot or being partially interwoven with the physical form.</summary>
<keywords>Soul, body, Animate, sense perception, Ideal-Form, philosophy, agent and instrument, dualism</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>Continuing the inquiry into whether the Soul and body merge or remain distinct, this page examines the concept of coalescence and interweaving, concluding that the Soul remains an unaffected agent using the body as an instrument.</meta>

-> PLOTINUS <-

conjunction is absolutely necessary, to separate the agent from the instrument, the body, so that it does not always have to act upon or through this inferior entity.

### 4.

Let us consider, then, the hypothesis of a coalescence.

Now if there is a coalescence, the lower is elevated and the higher is degraded; the body is raised in the scale of being by participating in life; the Soul, by being associated with death and unreason, is brought lower. How can a reduction in the quality of life produce an increase such as Sense-Perception?

No: the body has acquired life, and it is the body that will acquire, along with life, sensation and the emotions resulting from sensation. Desire, then, will belong to the body, since the objects of desire are to be enjoyed by the body. And fear, too, will belong to the body alone; for it is the body’s fate to fail in its joys and to perish.

Then again, we would have to examine how such a coalescence could be conceived: we might find it impossible. Perhaps all this is like claiming a coalescence of things utterly different in kind—for example, of a line and the color white.

Next, consider the suggestion that the Soul is interwoven through the body: such a relation would not give the "woof and warp" a shared sensation. The interwoven element might very well suffer no change; the permeating soul might remain entirely untouched by what affects the body—just as light always remains free of all it illuminates—and all the more so since we are specifically asked to consider it as diffused throughout the entire frame rather than confined to any one part.

Under such an interweaving, then, the Soul would not be subjected to the body’s emotions and experiences; it would be present instead as an <term>Ideal-Form</term> within Matter.

Let us then suppose the Soul to be in the body as an <term>Ideal-Form</term> in Matter. Now if—the first possibility—the Soul is an essence, a self-existent entity, it can be present only as a separable form and will therefore even more certainly be the <term>Using-Principle</term> (and therefore remain unaffected).

<vocab>Plotinus, Soul, coalescence, Sense-Perception, Ideal-Form, Matter, Using-Principle</vocab>

<summary>Plotinus argues against the idea that the Soul and body merge into a single substance, as such a coalescence would degrade the Soul without explaining sensation. He suggests instead that the Soul remains a separate, unaffected entity—a "Using-Principle"—that illuminates or informs the body like light or an Ideal-Form.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Soul-body dualism, coalescence, Sense-Perception, Ideal-Form, Using-Principle, metaphysics, philosophy of mind</keywords>

Suppose, next, that the Soul is present like the form of an axe on iron: here, no doubt, the form is all-important, but it is still (not one member alone but) the axe—the complement of iron and form—that performs whatever is done by the iron thus modified. On this analogy, therefore, we are even more strictly compelled to assign all the experiences of the combination to the body: their natural seat is the material member, the instrument, the potential recipient of life (and not the Life-Principle itself).

Compare the passage (in Plato) where we read that "it is absurd to suppose that the Soul weaves"; it is equally absurd to think of it as desiring or grieving. All this belongs instead to the province of something which we may call the Animate.

### 5.

Now this Animate might be merely the body as it possesses life; it might be the Couplement of Soul and body; or it might be a third and different entity formed from both.

The Soul in turn—apart from the nature of the Animate—must be either impassive, merely causing Sense-Perception in its partner, or sympathetic; and, if sympathetic, it may have identical experiences with its fellow or merely corresponding experiences: for example, desire in the Animate may be something quite distinct from the accompanying movement or state in the desiring faculty.

The body, the living body as we know it, we will consider later.

Let us take first the Couplement of body and Soul. How could suffering, for example, be seated in this Couplement?

It may be suggested that some unwelcome state of the body produces a distress which reaches a Sensitive-Faculty, which in turn merges into the Soul. But this account still leaves the origin of the sensation unexplained.

Another suggestion might be that everything is due to an opinion or judgment: some evil seems to have befallen the man or his belongings, and this conviction sets up a state of trouble in the body and in the entire Animate. But this account still leaves a question as to the source and seat of the judgment: does it belong to the Soul or to the Couplement? Besides, the judgment that evil is present does not involve the feeling

<summary>This page explores the concept of the "Animate" as a composite of Soul and body, arguing through the analogy of an axe that sensations and emotions belong to this combined entity rather than the Soul itself. It questions whether the "Couplement" or the Soul is the true seat of judgment and suffering.</summary>
<keywords>Soul, Animate, Plato, Life-Principle, Couplement, Sense-Perception, Sensitive-Faculty, judgment, dualism, Plotinus</keywords>

of grief: the judgment might very well arise and yet the grief not follow at all: one may think oneself slighted and yet not be angry; and the appetite is not necessarily excited by the thought of a pleasure. We are, therefore, no closer than before to any justification for assigning these feelings to the Couplement.

Is it any explanation to say that desire is located in a Faculty-of-desire and anger in the Irascible-Faculty and, collectively, that all tendency is seated in the Appetitive-Faculty? Such a statement of the facts does not help toward making these feelings common to the Couplement; they might still be located either in the Soul alone or in the body alone. On the one hand, if the appetite is to be stirred, as in physical passion, there must be a heating of the blood and the bile—a well-defined state of the body; on the other hand, the impulse toward The Good cannot be a joint feeling, but, like certain others as well, it would necessarily belong to the Soul alone.

Reason, then, does not permit us to assign all the feelings to the Couplement.

In the case of physical desire, it will certainly be the Man who desires, and yet, on the other hand, there must also be desire in the Desiring-Faculty. How can this be? Are we to suppose that, when the man begins the desire, the Desiring-Faculty moves at his command? How could the Man have come to desire at all unless through some prior activity in the Desiring-Faculty? Is it the Desiring-Faculty, then, that takes the lead? Yet how could it, unless the body is first in the appropriate condition?

### 6.

It may seem reasonable to establish as a law that when any powers are held by a recipient, every action or state expressing them must be the action or state of that recipient; the powers themselves remain unaffected, merely providing the efficiency.

But if this were so, then—since the Animate is the recipient of the Causing-Principle (that is, the Soul) which brings life to the Couplement—this Cause must itself remain unaffected, while all the experiences and activities expressing that life are located in the recipient, the Animate.

<summary>Plotinus argues that emotions and desires cannot be simply attributed to the union of soul and body, as some impulses are purely spiritual while others require a specific physical state. He suggests that the Soul acts as an unaffected "Causing-Principle," while the "Animate" serves as the recipient that undergoes the actual experiences of life.</summary>

But this would mean that life itself belongs not to the Soul but to the Couplement; or at least the life of the Couplement would not be the life of the Soul; Sense-Perception would belong not to the Sensitive-Faculty but to the container of that faculty.

But if sensation is a movement traversing the body and culminating in the Soul, how can the Soul lack sensation? The very presence of the Sensitive-Faculty must guarantee sensation to the Soul.

Once again, where is Sense-Perception seated? 
In the Couplement. 
Yet how can the Couplement have sensation independently of action in the Sensitive-Faculty, if the Soul and the Soul-Faculty are left out of the account?

### 7.
The truth lies in the consideration that the Couplement exists by virtue of the Soul's presence.

This, however, does not mean that the Soul gives itself, as it is in itself, to form either the Couplement or the body.

No; from the organized body and something else—let us say a light which the Soul gives off from itself—it forms a distinct Principle, the Animate; and in this Principle are vested Sense-Perception and all the other experiences found to belong to the Animate.

But what of the "We"? How do We have Sense-Perception? 
By the fact that We are not separate from the Animate so constituted, even though certainly other and nobler elements go to make up the entire many-sided nature of Man.

The faculty of perception in the Soul cannot act by the immediate grasping of sensible objects, but only by discerning the impressions printed upon the Animate by sensation: these impressions are already Intelligibles, while the outer sensation is a mere phantom of the other (the one in the Soul) which is nearer to Authentic-Existence as being an impassive reading of Ideal-Forms.

And by means of these Ideal-Forms, by which the Soul wields single lordship over the Animate, we have Discursive-Reasoning, Sense-Know—

The human "We" experiences these sensations because we are connected to this Animate principle, though the Soul itself perceives through Ideal-Forms rather than direct contact with physical objects.</summary>

knowledge and Intellection. From this moment, we specifically have the "We"; before this, there was only the "Ours"; but at this stage stands the WE (the authentic Human-Principle) loftily presiding over the Animate.

There is no reason why the entire compound entity should not be described as the Animate or Living-Being—mingled in a lower phase, but above that point lies the beginning of the true man, distinct from all that is kin to the lion and all that belongs to the order of the multiple brute. And since "The Man," so understood, is essentially the associate of the reasoning Soul, in our reasoning it is this "We" that reasons, because the use and act of reason is a characteristic Act of the Soul.

## 8.

And what is our relation toward the Intellectual-Principle? By this I mean, not that faculty in the soul which is one of the emanations from the Intellectual-Principle, but the Intellectual-Principle itself (the Divine-Mind).

This also we possess as the summit of our being. And we have it either as common to all or as our own immediate possession; or again, we may possess it in both degrees—that is, in common, since it is indivisible (being one, everywhere and always its entire self) and individually, in that each personality possesses it entirely in the First-Soul (that is, in the Intellectual as distinguished from the lower phase of the Soul).

Hence we possess the Ideal-Forms also in two modes: in the Soul, as if unrolled and separate; and in the Intellectual-Principle, concentrated and one.

And how do we possess the Divinity?

In that the Divinity is contained in the Intellectual-Principle and Authentic-Existence; and "We" come third in order after these two, for the "We" is constituted by a union of the supreme, undivided Soul—as we read—and that Soul which is divided among living bodies. For, note, we inevitably think of the Soul, though one and undivided in the All, as being present to bodies in division: insofar as any bodies are Animates, the Soul has given itself to each of the separate material masses; or rather, it appears to be present in the bodies by the fact that

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "We" (the Human-Principle) and its relationship to the Soul and the Animate, specifically how the Soul acts through the reasoning faculty and its connection to the higher Intellectual-Principle.</meta>

<header>I. 1. 9] THE ANIMATE AND THE MAN</header>

It shines into them: it makes them living beings not by merging into the body, but by giving forth—without any change in itself—images or likenesses of itself, like one face caught by many mirrors.

The first of these images is the faculty of Sense-Perception seated in the Couplement; and from this point downward, all successive images are to be recognized as phases of the Soul in a diminishing sequence, until the series ends in the faculties of generation, growth, and the production of offspring. This offspring is active in its own turn, in contrast to the engendering Soul, which <note>has no direct action within matter but</note> produces simply by leaning toward what it fashions.

### 9.

That Soul, then, within us, will by its nature stand apart from everything that can cause any of the evils which a person does or suffers; for all such evil, as we have seen, belongs only to the Animate, the Couplement.

But there is a difficulty in understanding how the Soul can remain guiltless if our thinking and reasoning are rooted in it; for all this lower kind of knowledge is delusion and is the cause of much that is evil.

When we have done evil, it is because we have been defeated by our lower side—for a person is many-sided—by desire, or rage, or some evil mental image. The misnamed reasoning that accepts what is false is actually just imagination; it has not waited for the judgment of the Reasoning-Principle. We have acted at the call of the less worthy part of ourselves, just as in matters of the sensory world we sometimes see falsely because we trust only the lower perception—that of the Couplement—without applying the tests of the Reasoning-Faculty.

The Intellectual-Principle has held aloof from the act and so is guiltless; or, as we may state it, all depends on whether we ourselves have or have not put ourselves in touch with the Intellectual-Realm, either in the Intellectual-Principle or within ourselves; for it is possible to possess something and not use it at the same time.

Thus, we have distinguished what belongs to the Couplement from what stands by itself: one group has the character of the body and never exists apart from the body, while all that has no need of the body for its
