<meta>This page continues the investigation into the impassivity of the Soul, explaining how the Soul remains at peace while the "Couplement" of soul and body experiences emotion and change, and defining the dual nature of the human "We."</meta>

<header>PLOTINUS</header>

Manifestation belongs specifically to the Soul; and the Understanding, by passing judgment upon sense-impressions, reaches the point of the vision of Ideal-Forms, seeing them, as it were, with a corresponding sensation (that is, with consciousness). This is certainly true of the Understanding in the Veritable Soul. For true Understanding is the Act of the Intellections; in many of its manifestations, it is the assimilation and reconciliation of the outer world with the inner world.

Thus, despite everything, the Soul is at peace with itself and within itself. All the changes and all the turmoil we experience are the result of what is attached to the Soul, and are, as we have said, the states and experiences of this elusive "Couplement."

### 10.
It will be objected that if the Soul constitutes the "We" (the personality) and "We" are subject to these states, then the Soul must be subject to them; and similarly, that whatever "We" do must be done by the Soul.

But it has been observed that the Couplement, too—especially before our liberation—is a member of this total "We," and in fact, what the body experiences, we say that "We" experience. This "We" then covers two distinct notions; sometimes it includes the brute part, and sometimes it transcends the brute. The body is matter touched into life; the true human is the other, remaining pure of the body and naturally endowed with the virtues which belong to Intellectual-Activity—virtues whose seat is the Separate Soul, the Soul which may be kept apart even while it dwells here. (This Soul constitutes the human being), for when it has wholly withdrawn, that other Soul which is a radiation (or emanation) from it withdraws also, following after it.

On the other hand, those virtues which spring not from contemplative wisdom but from custom or practical discipline belong to the Couplement. To the Couplement, too, belong the vices; they are its aversions, desires, and sympathies.

And Friendship?

This emotion belongs sometimes to the lower part, and sometimes to the interior man.

<summary>Plotinus argues that the true Soul remains at peace and separate from bodily turmoil, which instead affects the "Couplement" of soul and body. He distinguishes between the higher human self, rooted in intellectual virtue, and the lower self, which experiences worldly emotions and habits.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of the Soul and the "We," specifically focusing on how the higher principles relate to childhood, animals, and the concept of sin.</meta>

## 11.

In childhood, the primary activity occurs in the Couplement, and there is very little radiation from the higher principles of our being. But when these higher principles do act on us—even if feebly or rarely—their action is directed toward the Supreme; they affect us only when they stand at the mid-point.

But does not the "We" include that phase of our being which exists above the mid-point?

It does, but only on the condition that we grasp it. Our entire nature is not ours at all times; rather, it belongs to us only as we direct the mid-point upward or downward, or bring some particular phase of our nature from potentiality or native character into action.

And as for animals, in what way or to what extent do they possess the Animate?

If, as some believe, they contain human souls that have sinned, then the Animating-Principle in its separable phase does not enter directly into the animal; it is present, but not present to them. They are aware only of the image of the Soul (the lower soul), and they perceive even that only by being aware of the body, which is organized and defined by that image.

If there is no human soul in them, the Animate is formed for them by a radiation from the All-Soul.

## 12.

But if the Soul is sinless, why do expiations exist? There certainly seems to be a contradiction here: on one hand, the Soul is beyond all guilt; on the other, we hear of its sin, its purification, and its atonement; it is reportedly doomed to the lower world and passes from body to body.

We may take either view as we choose; they are easily reconciled.

When we speak of the sinless Soul, we treat the Soul and the Essential-Soul as one and the same: it is the simple, unbroken Unity.

By the "Soul subject to sin," we are referring to a grouping; we include that other part—the phase of the Soul that experiences all states and passions. In this sense, the Soul is a composite, all-inclusive entity; it falls under the conditions of the entire living experience. It is this composite that sins, and it is this, rather than the other, that pays the penalty.

<vocab>Soul, Animate, All-Soul, Essential-Soul, Couplement, Animating-Principle, mid-point, expiations</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that the higher soul is often inactive in children and animals and clarifies that while the Essential-Soul remains pure, the composite soul is responsible for sin and subject to punishment.</summary>
<keywords>Soul, Animate, All-Soul, Essential-Soul, sin, expiation, childhood, animals, Couplement, mid-point</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the Soul, addressing the apparent contradiction of a sinless soul that nonetheless undergoes expiation and descent.</meta>

# PLOTINUS

It is in this sense that we read of the Soul: “We saw it as those others saw the sea-god Glaucus.” “And,” reading on, “if we mean to discern the nature of the Soul, we must strip it free of all that has gathered about it; we must see into the philosophy of it, examining which <term>Existences</term> it touches and by kinship to which <term>Existences</term> it is what it is.”

Thus, the Life is one thing, the Act is another, and the Expiator yet another. The retreat and separation, then, must be not from this body only, but from every alien accumulation. Such accumulation takes place at birth; or rather, birth is the coming-into-being of that other (lower) phase of the Soul. For the meaning of birth has been indicated elsewhere; it is brought about by a descent of the Soul—something being given off by the Soul other than that which actually comes down in the <term>declension</term>.

Then has the Soul let this image fall? And is this <term>declension</term> not certainly sin?

If the <term>declension</term> is no more than the illuminating of an object beneath, it constitutes no sin: the shadow is to be attributed not to the light source but to the object illuminated; if the object were not there, the light could cause no shadow.

And the Soul is said to go down, to decline, only in the sense that the object it illuminates lives by its life. And it lets the image fall only if there is nothing nearby to receive it; and it lets it fall, not as a thing cut off, but as a thing that ceases to exist: the image has no further being when the whole Soul is looking toward the Supreme.

The poet, too, in the story of Hercules, seems to give this image a separate existence; he puts the shade of Hercules in the lower world and Hercules himself among the gods: treating the hero as existing in the two realms at once, he gives us a twofold Hercules.

It is not difficult to explain this distinction. Hercules was a hero of practical virtue. By his noble service, he was worthy to be a God. On the other hand, his merit was action and not the <term>Contemplation</term> which would place him unreservedly in the higher realm. Therefore, while he has a place above, something of him remains below.

<summary>Plotinus explains that the Soul's descent into birth is an illumination of the lower self rather than a sin, using the dual nature of Hercules to illustrate how a soul can exist simultaneously in higher and lower realms.</summary>
<keywords>Soul, Glaucus, Existences, declension, Hercules, practical virtue, Contemplation, the Supreme, illumination, dual nature</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the Soul, moving from the soul's relationship with its lower image to the nature of reasoning and the beginning of a treatise on virtue and "Likeness to God."</meta>

13.
And what is the principle that reasons out these matters? Is it "We" or the Soul?
It is We, but through the Soul.
But how "through the Soul"? Does this mean that the Soul reasons by possession (by coming into contact with the subjects of inquiry)?
No; it reasons by the very fact of being the Soul. Its Act exists without movement; or any movement that can be ascribed to it must be utterly distinct from all bodily movement and be simply the Soul’s own life.
And <term>Intellection</term> in us is twofold: since the Soul is intellective, and <term>Intellection</term> is the highest phase of life, we have <term>Intellection</term> both by the characteristic Act of our Soul and by the Act of the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> upon us—for this <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> is a part of us no less than the Soul, and we are always rising towards it.

### SECOND TRACTATE
### ON VIRTUE

I.
Since Evil is here, “haunting this world by necessary law,” and it is the Soul’s intention to escape from Evil, we must escape from here.
But what is this escape?
“In attaining Likeness to God,” we read. And this is explained as “becoming just and holy, living by wisdom,” with our entire nature grounded in Virtue.
But does not Likeness through Virtue imply Likeness to some being that possesses Virtue? To what Divine Being, then, would our Likeness be? Must we not think it is to the Being in which, above all, such excellence seems to exist—that is, to the Soul of the <term>Kosmos</term> and to the Principle ruling within it, the Principle endowed with a most wonderful wisdom? What could be more fitting than that we, living in this world, should become like its ruler?
But, at the beginning, we are met by the doubt of whether all the virtues even find a place in this Divine Being—Moral Balance (<term>Sophrosyny</term>),

If, indeed, that aspiration toward the Intelligible which is in our nature exists also in this Ruling-Power, then we do not need to look elsewhere for the source of order and of the virtues within ourselves.

But does this Power possess the Virtues?

We cannot expect to find *there* what are called the Civic Virtues: the <term>Prudence</term> which belongs to the reasoning faculty; the <term>Fortitude</term> which directs the emotional and passionate nature; the <term>Sophrosyny</term> <note>Temperance or self-control</note> which consists in a certain pact, in a concord between the passionate faculty and the reason; or <term>Rectitude</term> <note>Justice</note> which is the proper application of all the other virtues as each in turn should command or obey.

Is Likeness, then, attained perhaps not by these virtues of the social order but by those greater qualities known by the same general name? And if so, do the Civic Virtues give us no help at all?

It is against reason to utterly deny Likeness by these while admitting it by the greater: tradition at least recognizes certain men of civic excellence as divine, and we must believe that these too had in some way attained Likeness. On both levels there is virtue for us, though not the same virtue.

Now, if it is admitted that Likeness is possible—though by a varying use of different virtues and though the civic virtues do not suffice—there is no reason why we should not, by virtues peculiar to our state, attain Likeness to a model in which virtue has no place.

But is that conceivable?

When warmth comes in to make anything warm, must there necessarily be something to warm the source of the warmth?

If a fire is to warm something else, must there be a fire to warm that fire?

Against the first illustration it may be retorted that the source of the warmth does already contain warmth—not by an infusion but as an essential phase of its nature—so that, if the analogy is to hold, the argument would make Virtue something communicated to the Soul but an

<summary>This page explores whether the Divine Ruling-Power possesses civic virtues and argues that humans can achieve "Likeness to God" through virtues, even if the Divine model itself exists in a state beyond the need for such virtues.</summary>

# I. 2. 2] VIRTUE

is an essential constituent of the Principle from which the Soul, attaining Likeness, absorbs it.

Against the illustration drawn from fire, it may be argued that the analogy would make that Principle identical with virtue, whereas we hold it to be something higher.

The objection would be valid if what the soul takes in were one and the same with the source, but in fact virtue is one thing, and the source of virtue is quite another. The material house is not identical with the house conceived in the intellect, and yet it stands in its likeness: the material house has distribution and order while the pure idea is not constituted by any such elements; distribution, order, and symmetry are not parts of an idea.

So it is with us: it is from the Supreme that we derive order and distribution and harmony, which are virtues in this sphere. The Existences there, having no need of harmony, order, or distribution, have nothing to do with virtue; and, nonetheless, it is by our possession of virtue that we become like them.

This much is to show that the principle that we attain Likeness through virtue in no way involves the existence of virtue in the Supreme. But we have not merely to make a formal demonstration: we must persuade as well as demonstrate.

**2.**

First, then, let us examine those good qualities by which we hold Likeness comes, and seek to establish what this thing is which—as we possess it in transcription—is virtue, but as the Supreme possesses it, is in the nature of an exemplar or archetype and is not virtue.

We must first distinguish two modes of Likeness. There is the likeness demanding an identical nature in the objects which, further, must draw their likeness from a common principle: and there is the case in which B resembles A, but A is a Primal, not concerned about B and not said to resemble B. In this second case, likeness is understood in a distinct sense: we no longer look for identity of nature,

<summary>Plotinus argues that while humans achieve "Likeness" to the Divine through virtue, the Supreme source itself does not possess virtue, acting instead as the non-virtuous archetype for order and harmony. He distinguishes between likeness shared through a common principle and the likeness of a derivative to its primal source.</summary>
<keywords>Likeness, Virtue, The Supreme, Archetype, Plotinus, Neoplatonism, Soul, Order, Harmony</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of Likeness to God, specifically distinguishing between the Civic Virtues and the higher virtues as discussed by Plato.</meta>

but, on the contrary, it is for divergence since the likeness has come about by the mode of difference.

What, then, precisely is Virtue, collectively and in particular? The clearer method will be to begin with the particular, for in this way the common element by which all the forms hold the general name will readily appear.

The Civic Virtues, which we have touched upon above, are a principle of order and beauty in us as long as we continue to spend our lives here: they ennoble us by setting bounds and measures to our desires and to our entire sensibility, and by dispelling false judgment—and this by the sheer efficacy of the better, by the very setting of the bounds, and by the fact that what is measured is lifted outside of the sphere of the unmeasured and lawless.

And, further, these Civic Virtues—measured and ordered themselves and acting as a principle of measure to the Soul, which is as Matter to their forming—are similar to the measure reigning in the over-world, and they carry a trace of that Highest Good in the Supreme; for, while utter measurelessness is brute Matter and wholly outside of Likeness, any participation in an Ideal-Form produces some corresponding degree of Likeness to the formless Being There. And participation happens by nearness: the Soul is nearer than the body, and therefore more closely related; it participates more fully and shows a godlike presence, almost tricking us into the delusion that in the Soul we see God entirely.

This is the way in which those who possess the Civic Virtues attain Likeness.

### 3.

We come now to that other mode of Likeness which, we read, is the fruit of the higher virtues: by discussing this, we shall penetrate more deeply into the essence of the Civic Virtue and be able to define the nature of the higher kind whose existence we shall establish beyond doubt.

To Plato, unmistakably, there are two distinct orders of virtue, and the civic does not suffice for Likeness: "Likeness to God," he says, "is a flight from this world’s ways and things": in dealing with

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of Likeness to God, specifically focusing on how the Soul achieves this likeness through purification and the intellectual virtues, distinguishing between the states of the Soul and the nature of the Divine.</meta>

# VIRTUE
<meta>I. 2. 4]</meta>

In discussing the qualities of good citizenship, he does not use the simple term Virtue but adds the distinguishing word *civic*; and elsewhere he declares all the virtues without exception to be purifications.

But in what sense can we call the virtues purifications, and how does purification result in Likeness?

As the Soul is evil by being mixed with the body, and by coming to share the body’s states and to think the body’s thoughts, so it would be good—it would be possessed of virtue—if it threw off the body’s moods and devoted itself to its own Act: the state of Intellection and Wisdom. In this state, it would never allow the passions of the body to affect it—the virtue of Sophrosyny—would know no fear at the parting from the body—the virtue of Fortitude—and reason and the Intellectual-Principle would rule—in which state is Righteousness. Such a disposition in the Soul, having thus become intellective and immune to passion, would not be wrong to call Likeness to God; for the Divine, too, is pure, and the Divine Act is such that Likeness to it is Wisdom.

But would this not make virtue a state of the Divine also?

No: the Divine has no states; the state is in the Soul. The Act of Intellection in the Soul is not the same as in the Divine: of the things in the Supreme, the Soul grasps some in its own way, and some not at all.

Then once again, does the single word, Intellection, cover two distinct Acts?

Rather, there is a primal Intellection, and there is an Intellection deriving from the Primal which has a different scope.

As speech is the echo of the thought in the Soul, so thought in the Soul is an echo from elsewhere: that is to say, as the uttered thought is an image of the soul-thought, so the soul-thought images a thought above itself and is the interpreter of the higher sphere.

Virtue, in the same way, is a thing of the Soul: it does not belong to the Intellectual-Principle or to the Transcendence.

### 4.

We come, then, to the question of whether Purification is the entirety of this human quality, virtue, or merely the forerunner that virtue follows? Does virtue imply the achieved state of purification or does

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the examination of the Soul's purification, exploring whether the process of cleansing itself constitutes the "Good" or if it simply prepares the Soul to receive a higher vision of the Intellectual-Principle.</meta>

# PLOTINUS

Does the mere process of purification suffice for it, Virtue being something of less perfection than the accomplished purity which is almost the Goal <note>The "Term" or final end-point of the Soul's journey.</note>?

To have been purified is to have cleansed away everything alien; but Goodness is something more.

If Goodness existed before the impurity entered, then that Goodness suffices; but even so, it is not the act of cleansing, but the cleansed thing that emerges which will be The Good. And it remains to establish what (in the case of the cleansed Soul) this emergent thing is.

It can scarcely prove to be The Good itself; the Absolute Good cannot be thought to have taken up its residence with Evil. We can think of it only as something in the nature of good, but paying a double allegiance and unable to rest in the Authentic Good.

The Soul’s true Good lies in devotion to the Intellectual-Principle, its own kin; evil to the Soul lies in associating with strangers. There is no other way for it than to purify itself and so enter into relation with its own kind; the new phase begins with a new orientation.

After the Purification, then, is there still this orientation to be made? No: through the purification, the true alignment stands accomplished.

The Soul’s virtue, then, is this alignment? No: it is what the alignment produces within.

And this is . . . ?

It is that the Soul sees; that, like sight affected by the object seen, the Soul accepts the imprint—engraved upon it and working within it—of the vision it has reached.

But did the Soul not possess all this always, or had it forgotten?

What it now sees, it certainly always possessed, but as if lying away in the dark, not as an active force within it; to dispel the darkness, and thus come to knowledge of its inner content, it must press toward the light.

Besides, it possessed not the originals but images and pictures; and these it must bring into closer harmony with the truths they represent. And, further, if the Intellectual-Principle is said to be a possession of the Soul, this is only in the sense that It is not alien and that the link becomes very close when the Soul’s sight is turned toward It:

<summary>This page argues that purification is not the "Good" itself but a necessary alignment that allows the Soul to perceive the Intellectual-Principle. While the Soul always possesses these truths, it must turn toward the light to move from mere images to a direct vision of reality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the Soul achieves likeness to the Divine through purification and the control of passions, following the previous page's inquiry into whether purification itself is the "Good" or merely a preparation for it.</meta>

I. 2. 5] VIRTUE

otherwise, ever-present though It may be, It remains foreign, just as our knowledge is dead to us if it does not determine our action.

5.
So we come to the scope of the purification: once that is understood, the nature of Likeness becomes clear. To what Principle is this Likeness? With what God is this Identity?

The question is substantially this: how far does purification dispel the two orders of passion—anger, desire, and the like, along with grief and its kin—and to what degree is detachment from the body possible?

Detachment means simply that the soul withdraws to its own place.

It will hold itself above all passions and affections. It will use necessary pleasures and all the activity of the senses only as medicine and relief, lest its work be impeded. It may combat pain, but if a cure fails, it will bear it meekly and ease it by refusing to give inward consent to it. It will check all passionate action: the suppression will be complete if that is possible, but at the very least the Soul will never itself catch fire; instead, it will keep involuntary and uncontrolled impulses outside its own boundaries, ensuring they are rare and weak. The Soul has nothing to dread, though no doubt the involuntary still has some power here; fear, therefore, must cease, except insofar as it is purely a warning. Whatever desire there may be can never be for anything base; even the food and drink necessary for restoration will lie outside the Soul’s attention, as will sexual appetite: or if such desire must exist, it will turn only upon the actual needs of nature and be entirely under control; or if any uncontrolled motion takes place, it will reach no further than the imagination, being no more than a fleeting fancy.

The Soul itself will be inviolably free and will work to set the irrational part of its nature above all attack; or if that may not be, then at least to preserve it from violent assault, so that any wound it receives may be slight and be healed at once by virtue of the Soul’s presence, just as a man living next door to a Sage would profit from the neighborhood,

It explains that a purified Soul remains free and protects its irrational nature from harm through its constant presence and self-control.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Soul's purification, specifically focusing on how the Soul reaches a state where the lower nature is completely governed by Reason, leading toward a divine state beyond mere human virtue.</meta>

# PLOTINUS

either in becoming wise and good himself or, for sheer shame, never venturing any act which the nobler mind would disapprove.

There will be no battling in the Soul: the mere intervention of Reason is enough. The lower nature will stand in such awe of Reason that for even the slightest movement it has made, it will grieve and censure its own weakness for not having kept low and still in the presence of its lord.

6.

In all this, there is no sin—there is only a matter of discipline—but our concern is not merely to be sinless but to be God.

As long as there is any such involuntary action, the nature is twofold: God and <term>Demi-God</term>, or rather God in association with a nature of a lower power. When all involuntary action is suppressed, there is God unmingled, a Divine Being among those that follow upon <term>The First</term>.

For at this height, the man is the very being that came from the <term>Supreme</term>. With the primal excellence restored, the essential man is <term>There</term>. Entering this sphere, he has associated himself with the reasoning phase of his nature, and this he will lead up into likeness with his highest self, as far as the earthly mind is capable, so that if possible it shall never be inclined toward—and at the very least never adopt—any course displeasing to its overlord.

What form, then, does virtue take in one so lofty?

It appears as <term>Wisdom</term>, which consists in the contemplation of all that exists in the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>, and as the immediate presence of the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> itself.

And each of these has two modes or aspects: there is <term>Wisdom</term> as it is in the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> and as it is in the Soul; and there is the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> as it is present to itself and as it is present to the Soul. This results in what is <term>Virtue</term> in the Soul, but not <term>Virtue</term> in the <term>Supreme</term>.

In the <term>Supreme</term>, then, what is it?

Its proper <term>Act</term> and Its <term>Essence</term>.

That <term>Act</term> and <term>Essence</term> of the <term>Supreme</term>, manifested in a new form, constitute the virtue of this sphere. For the <term>Supreme</term> is not self-existent

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of virtue in the Soul, specifically how it relates to its higher archetypes in the Intellectual-Principle, moving from the state of the purified individual to the absolute origins of Rectitude and Justice.</meta>

<page-num>49</page-num>
# VIRTUE
<meta>I. 2. 7]</meta>
<sig>H</sig>

Justice is the Absolute of any defined virtue; it is, so to speak, an exemplar—the source of what becomes virtue in the soul. For virtue is dependent, residing in something other than itself, while the Supreme is self-standing and independent.

But if we take Rectitude to be the proper ordering of a faculty, does it not always imply the existence of different parts?

No: there is a Rectitude of Diversity appropriate to that which has parts, but there is another kind of Rectitude, no less valid than the first, even though it resides in a Unity. And the authentic Absolute Rectitude is the Act of a Unity upon itself—a Unity in which there is no "this" or "that" or "the other."

On this principle, the supreme Rectitude of the Soul is that it directs its Act toward the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>. Its restraint (<term>Sophrosyny</term>) is its inward turning toward the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>; its <term>Fortitude</term> is its remaining unaffected, in the likeness of That toward which its gaze is fixed—Whose nature involves an impassivity that the Soul acquires by virtue. The Soul must acquire this if it is not to be at the mercy of every state arising in its less noble companion <note>referring to the physical body and its passions</note>.

7.

The virtues in the Soul follow a sequence corresponding to that which exists in the higher world—that is, among their exemplars in the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>.

In the Supreme, <term>Intellection</term> constitutes Knowledge and Wisdom; self-concentration is <term>Sophrosyny</term>; Its proper Act is Its Dutifulness; and Its Immateriality, by which It remains untouched within Itself, is the equivalent of <term>Fortitude</term>.

In the Soul, the direction of vision toward the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> is Wisdom and Prudence; these are soul-virtues not appropriate to the Supreme, where Thinker and Thought are identical. All the other virtues have similar correspondences.

And if the goal of purification is the production of a pure being, then the purification of the Soul must produce all the virtues; if any are lacking, then not one of them is perfect.

<summary>This page explains that human virtues are reflections of absolute archetypes found in the Intellectual-Principle, defining Rectitude as the Soul's alignment with its higher source. It concludes that true purification must establish all virtues simultaneously for any one of them to be perfect.</summary>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the relationship between civic and higher virtues, explaining how the Sage moves beyond human standards toward the divine. It then introduces the Third Tractate, which explores the method of Dialectic as the path to the Supreme.</meta>

<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

And to possess the greater is potentially to possess the minor, though the minor does not necessarily carry the greater with it.

Thus we have indicated the dominant theme in the life of the Sage; but whether his possession of the minor virtues is actual as well as potential, or whether even the greater virtues are in Act <note>Active or in a state of completion</note> in him or give way to qualities higher still, must be decided afresh in each individual case.

Take, for example, <term>Contemplative-Wisdom</term>. If other guides of conduct must be called upon to meet a specific need, can this virtue maintain its ground even as a mere potentiality?

And what happens when the virtues, by their very nature, differ in scope and province? Where, for example, <term>Sophrosyny</term> would allow certain acts or emotions under due restraint, and another virtue would eliminate them altogether? And is it not clear that all virtues may have to yield once <term>Contemplative-Wisdom</term> comes into action?

The solution lies in understanding the virtues and what each has to offer: in this way, the man will learn to work with one or another as each individual need demands. And as he reaches toward loftier principles and other standards, these in turn will define his conduct: for example, restraint in its earlier form will no longer satisfy him; he will strive for final disengagement; he will no longer live the human life of the good man—such as <term>Civic Virtue</term> commends—but, leaving this behind, will instead take up another life: that of the Gods.

For it is to the Gods, not to merely good men, that our likeness must look: to model ourselves upon good men is to produce an image of an image. We must fix our gaze above the image and attain likeness to the <term>Supreme Exemplar</term>.

### THIRD TRACTATE

### ON DIALECTIC (THE UPWARD WAY)

1. What art is there, what method, what discipline to bring us to where we must go?

The goal at which we must arrive we may take as agreed: we have established elsewhere, through many considerations, that our journey is

<page-num>51</page-num>
<header>I. 3. 1] DIALECTIC 51</header>

to the Good, to the Primal-Principle; and, indeed, the very reasoning which discovered the Term <note>The final goal or end-point</note> was itself something like an initiation.
But what order of beings will attain the Term?
Surely, as we read, those who have already seen all or most things, those who at their first birth have entered into the life-germ from which is to spring a metaphysician, a musician, or a born lover—the metaphysician taking to the path by instinct, while the musician and the nature peculiarly susceptible to love need outside guidance.

But how does the course lie? Is it the same for all, or is there a distinct method for each class of temperament?
For all, there are two stages of the path: as they are moving upward or have already gained the upper sphere.
The first degree is the conversion from the lower life; the second—held by those who have already made their way to the sphere of the <term>Intelligibles</term>, have set a footprint there, as it were, but must still advance within the realm—lasts until they reach the furthest point of the place, the Term attained when the topmost peak of the Intellectual realm is won.
But this highest degree must wait its time: let us first try to speak of the initial process of conversion.
We must begin by distinguishing the three types. Let us take the musician first and indicate his temperamental equipment for the task.
The musician we may think of as being exceedingly sensitive to beauty, drawn to it in a total rapture: somewhat slow to stir by his own impulse, he answers at once to the outer stimulus. Just as the timid are sensitive to noise, so he is sensitive to tones and the beauty they convey; all that offends against unison or harmony in melodies and rhythms repels him; he longs for measure and shapely pattern.
This natural tendency must be made the starting point for such a man; he must be drawn by the tone, rhythm, and design in things of sense. He must learn to distinguish the material forms from the <term>Authentic-Existent</term>, which is the source of all these correspondences and of the entire reasoned scheme in the work of art: he must be led to the

Beauty that manifests itself through these forms; he must be shown that what delighted him was nothing other than the Harmony of the Intellectual world and the Beauty in that sphere—not just one shape of beauty but the All-Beauty, the Absolute Beauty. The truths of philosophy must be implanted in him to lead him to faith in that which he possesses within himself without knowing it. We will show what these truths are later.

2.
The born lover—whose level the musician may also reach, and then either stop there or move beyond—has a certain memory of beauty. However, because he is now separated from it, he no longer understands it; enchanted by visible loveliness, he clings in wonder to that. His lesson must be to no longer fall down in bewildered delight before a single physical form. Under a system of mental discipline, he must be led to beauty everywhere and made to recognize the <term>One Principle</term> underlying it all—a Principle separate from material forms, coming from another source, and more truly present elsewhere. For example, the beauty in a noble way of life and in an admirably organized social system may be pointed out to him; this is a first training in the loveliness of the immaterial. He must learn to recognize beauty in the arts, sciences, and virtues. Then these separate and specific forms must be brought under the single principle by explaining their origin. From the virtues, he is to be led to the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>, to the <term>Authentic-Existent</term>; from there, he continues on the upward path.

3.
The metaphysician, equipped by his very character, is already "winged" and—unlike the others—does not need to be detached from the physical world. He moves toward the divine on his own but is uncertain of the way, so he only needs a guide. He must be shown and instructed; he is a willing traveler by his very temperament and is almost self-directed. Mathematics—which as a natural student he will master very easily—will be assigned to train him in abstract thought and in faith in the...

<header>DIALECTIC</header>

unembodied; a moral being by native disposition, he must be led to make his virtue perfect; after studying Mathematics, he must be put through a course in Dialectic and made an expert in the science.

### 4.

But what, in essence, is this science of Dialectic, which is essential to all three classes alike?

It is the Method, or Discipline, that brings with it the power of pronouncing with final truth upon the nature and relation of things—what each is, how it differs from others, what common quality all have, to what Kind each belongs and what rank each stands in its Kind, and whether its being is <term>Real-Being</term>, and how many Beings there are, and how many non-Beings are to be distinguished from Beings.

Dialectic also treats the Good and the not-Good, and the particulars that fall under each, and what is Eternal and what is not-Eternal—and these must be understood, not by "sense-knowledge" (which is only the appearance of knowledge) but with authentic science.

When all this is accomplished, it gives up its journeying through the realm of sense and settles down in the <term>Intellectual Cosmos</term>, and there carries out its own peculiar work: it has abandoned the entire realm of deceit and falsehood, and nourishes the Soul in the "Meadows of Truth." It employs the Platonic division to discern the <term>Ideal-Forms</term>, the <term>Authentic-Existence</term>, and the <term>First-Kinds</term> (or <term>Categories of Being</term>). It establishes, in the light of <term>Intellection</term>, the unity that exists in all that issues from these Firsts, until it has traversed the entire Intellectual Realm. Then, resolving that unity into particulars once more, it returns to the point from which it started.

Now it rests. Instructed and satisfied regarding the Being in that sphere, it is no longer busy with many things; it has arrived at Unity and it contemplates. It leaves to another science all that web of premises and conclusions called the <term>art of reasoning</term>, much as it leaves behind the art of writing. No doubt it considers some of the matter of <term>logic</term> necessary to clear the ground, but it makes itself the judge here as in everything else; where it sees a use, it uses it; anything it finds superfluous, it leaves behind.

<summary>This page defines Dialectic as the supreme science that allows the soul to distinguish between Real-Being and non-being, eventually leading it to rest in the contemplation of Unity within the Intellectual Cosmos. It distinguishes Dialectic from the mere art of reasoning or formal logic, positioning it as the ultimate method for reaching the Meadows of Truth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on Dialectic, moving from its function in the Intellectual Realm to its relationship with the laws of the Intellectual-Principle and its status as the most precious part of Philosophy.</meta>

<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

to whatever department of learning or practice may turn that matter to account.

### 5.
But from where does this science derive its own initial laws?

The <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> provides standards, the most certain ones for any soul that is able to apply them. Whatever else is necessary, Dialectic organizes for itself, combining and dividing until it has reached perfect <term>Intellection</term>. “For,” we read, “it is the purest perfection of <term>Intellection</term> and <term>Contemplative-Wisdom</term>.” And, being the noblest method and science that exists, it must necessarily deal with <term>Authentic-Existence</term>, the highest there is: as <term>Contemplative-Wisdom</term> (or true knowing), it deals with <term>Being</term>; as <term>Intellection</term>, it deals with what transcends <term>Being</term>.

What, then, is Philosophy?

Philosophy is supremely precious.

Is Dialectic, then, the same as Philosophy?

It is the most precious part of Philosophy. We must not think of it as a mere tool for the <term>metaphysician</term>: Dialectic does not consist of empty theories and rules; it deals with truths. <term>Existences</term> are, as it were, the matter it works with—or at least it proceeds methodically toward <term>Existences</term> and acquires both the concepts and the realities in a single step.

It knows untruth and sophistry, not directly or by its own nature, but merely as something produced outside of itself—something it recognizes as foreign to the truths stored within itself. In any falsehood presented to it, it perceives a conflict with its own standard of truth. That is to say, Dialectic has no knowledge of propositions—mere collections of words—but it knows the truth; and in that knowledge, it understands what the academic schools call their "propositions." Above all, it knows the workings of the soul; by virtue of this knowledge, it also knows what is affirmed and what is denied, whether a denial applies to what was asserted or to something else, and whether propositions agree or differ. It approaches everything submitted to it with the directness of sense-perception, leaving the petty precision of logical processes to other sciences that may care for such exercises.

<summary>This page defines Dialectic as the most vital part of Philosophy, deriving its laws from the Intellectual-Principle to reach pure Intellection. It distinguishes Dialectic from mere formal logic, emphasizing its direct grasp of truth and reality over linguistic propositions.</summary>
<keywords>Dialectic, Intellectual-Principle, Intellection, Contemplative-Wisdom, Authentic-Existence, Being, Philosophy, truth vs. sophistry, logic vs. reality</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on Dialectic, moving from its definition as the core of Philosophy to its practical application in ethics, reasoning, and the development of virtue.</meta>

<page-num>55</page-num>
<header>DIALECTIC</header>
<meta>I. 3. 6]</meta>

### 6.

Philosophy has other branches, but Dialectic is its most valuable part. In its study of the laws of the universe, Philosophy relies on Dialectic much as other studies and crafts use Arithmetic, though, of course, the connection between Philosophy and Dialectic is closer.

And in Ethics, too, Philosophy uses Dialectic. Through Dialectic, it arrives at contemplation, although Philosophy itself originates the moral state—or rather, the discipline from which the moral state develops.

Our reasoning faculties use the data of Dialectic almost as if it were their own property, for they are mainly concerned with Matter (whose place and value Dialectic establishes).

And while the other virtues apply reason to specific experiences and actions, the virtue of Wisdom (that is, the virtue specifically produced by Dialectic) is a kind of higher reasoning much closer to the Universal. It deals with abstract concepts such as correspondence and sequence, the choice of when to act or remain still, and the adoption of one course over the rejection of another. Wisdom and Dialectic have the task of presenting all things as Universals, stripped of matter, so that they may be processed by the Understanding.

But can these lower kinds of virtue exist without Dialectic and philosophy?

Yes—but only imperfectly and inadequately.

And is it possible to be a Sage, a Master of Dialectic, without these lower virtues?

That would not happen; the lower virtues will arise either before or alongside the higher ones. It is likely that everyone naturally possesses the basic virtues from which, when Wisdom intervenes, perfected virtue develops. Following the natural virtues, then, comes Wisdom, and with it the perfecting of the moral nature. Once the natural virtues exist, both levels—the natural and the higher—mature side by side toward their final excellence; as one advances, it carries the other forward toward perfection.

However, natural virtue is always imperfect in its vision and its strength—and for both levels of virtue, the essential matter is the principles from which we derive them.

<summary>This page explores Dialectic's role as the most precious part of Philosophy, explaining how it informs both intellectual understanding and the development of moral virtue. <meta>This page transitions from the discussion of Dialectic and its role in forming perfected virtue to the exploration of the nature of True Happiness and its accessibility to different forms of life.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

## FOURTH TRACTATE
### ON TRUE HAPPINESS

I.
Are we to consider True Happiness the same thing as Welfare or Prosperity, and therefore within the reach of other living beings as well as ourselves?

There is certainly no reason to deny well-being to any of them, as long as their circumstances allow them to flourish unhindered according to their kind.

Whether we define Welfare as consisting of pleasant living conditions or the accomplishment of some appropriate task, by either account it may belong to them as much as to us. For certainly, they may be both pleasantly situated and engaged in a function that is natural to them. Take, for example, living beings that have the gift of music; finding themselves well-off in other ways, they also sing according to their nature, and so their day is pleasant to them.

And if we even define Happiness as some ultimate Goal pursued by an inborn tendency, then on this point, too, we must allow it to animals from the moment they attain this Ultimate. In them, nature comes to a rest, having fulfilled its vital course from beginning to end.

It may be an unpleasant notion, this bringing down of happiness so low as to include the animal world—granting it, as we then must, even to the lowest of them, and not withholding it even from plants, since they are also alive and have a life unfolding toward a Goal.

But to begin with, it is surely unsound to deny that "good of life" to animals simply because they do not seem to be of great importance to humans. And as for plants, we do not necessarily have to grant them what we accord to other forms of life, since they have no feeling. It is true that some people might be found to declare prosperity possible even to plants: they have life, and life may bring good or evil; plants may thrive or wither, bear fruit or be barren.

No: if Pleasure is the Goal, if that is where the good of life lies, it is impossible to deny the good of life to any order of living things; if the

<summary>Plotinus investigates whether true happiness is synonymous with prosperity or the fulfillment of natural functions, questioning if such a definition would extend happiness to animals and plants. He argues that if happiness is merely defined by pleasure or reaching a biological end-point, it cannot be easily denied to lower forms of life.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Happiness, Welfare, Prosperity, animals, plants, Goal, Pleasure, well-being, natural functions</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fourth Tractate, "On True Happiness," further examining the argument that happiness (well-being) depends on sensation or consciousness, specifically addressing whether plants or lower forms of life can be considered "happy."</meta>

<page-num>57</page-num>
<header>HAPPINESS</header>
<meta>I. 4. 2]</meta>

If the goal is inner peace, it is equally impossible; it is also impossible if the good life is defined as living in accordance with the purpose of nature.

2. Those who deny the happy life to plants on the grounds that they lack sensation are really denying it to all living things.

By sensation, one can only mean the perception of one's own state, and the state of well-being must be a Good in itself, quite apart from the perception of it. To be a part of the natural plan is good, whether one acts knowingly or without knowledge. There is good in achieving the appropriate state even if there is no recognition of its fitness or desirable quality—for it must be inherently desirable.

This Good exists, then; it is present. That in which it is present possesses well-being without further requirement. Why, then, is there a need to demand sensation in addition?

Perhaps, however, the theory is that the Good of any state consists not in the condition itself but in the knowledge and perception of it.

But at this rate, the Good is nothing but mere sensation—the bare activity of the sentient life. In that case, it would be possessed by everything that feels, regardless of the circumstances. Perhaps it will be said that two components are needed to make up the Good: that there must be both the feeling and a specific state being felt. But how can it be argued that bringing together two neutral elements can produce the Good?

They will explain, possibly, that the state must be a state of Good and that such a condition constitutes well-being upon the discernment of that present good. But then they invite the question of whether well-being comes from discerning the presence of the Good that is already there, or whether there must further be a dual recognition: that the state is agreeable and that this agreeable state constitutes the Good.

If well-being demands this recognition, it no longer depends upon sensation but upon another, higher faculty. Well-being then resides not in a faculty that merely receives pleasure, but in one competent to discern that pleasure is the Good.

Then the cause of the well-being is no longer pleasure but the

<sig>I</sig>

<vocab>well-being, sensation, Good, natural plan, sentient life, faculty, inner-peace</vocab>

<summary>Plotinus argues that well-being is an inherent state of being rather than a mere sensation or the conscious perception of that state. He suggests that if happiness required conscious recognition, it would depend on a higher intellectual faculty rather than the feeling of pleasure itself.</summary>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues Plotinus's inquiry into the nature of happiness, specifically challenging the idea that sensation or mere existence is the root of well-being, and beginning an investigation into the role of Reason.</meta>

<page-num>58</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

a faculty competent to judge pleasure’s value. Now, a judging entity is nobler than one that merely accepts a state: it is a principle of Reason or of Intellection. Pleasure is a state; the irrational can never be closer to the Good than reason is. How can reason abdicate its position and declare that something belonging to a contrary order is nearer to the good than itself?

No: those who deny the good life to the vegetable world, and those who make it consist in some precise quality of sensation, are in reality seeking a loftier well-being than they realize, and are placing their highest ideal in a more luminous phase of life.

Perhaps, then, those are in the right who base happiness not on mere existence or even on sensory life, but on the life of Reason?

But they must tell us why it should be restricted in this way, and why exactly they make Reason an essential to the happiness of a living being:—

> “When you insist on Reason, is it because Reason is resourceful, swift to discern and achieve the primary needs of nature; or would you demand it even if it were powerless in that domain?”

If you call it in as a provider, then the irrational, equally with the rational, may possess happiness after their own kind, so long as nature supplies their wants without any thought of their own. In that case, Reason becomes a servant; there is no longer any worth in it for itself and no worth in that consummation of reason which, we hold, is virtue.

If you say that reason is to be cherished for its own sake and not for supplying these human needs, you must tell us what other services it renders, what its proper nature is, and what makes it the perfect thing it is.

For, by this admission, its perfection cannot reside in any such planning and providing: its perfection will be something quite different, something of quite another class. Reason cannot itself be one of those primary needs of nature; it cannot even be a cause of those primary needs of nature or belong to that order at all. It must be nobler than any and all such things: otherwise, it is not easy to see how we can be asked to rate it so highly.

<summary>Plotinus argues that Reason is superior to mere sensation or pleasure because it possesses the faculty of judgment. He questions whether Reason's value lies in its practical ability to provide for natural needs or in its own inherent, noble nature.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Plotinus's exploration of whether happiness is found in mere life or a specific kind of life, transitioning from the critique of sensory-based happiness to the definition of happiness through the "fullness of life."</meta>

<page-num>59</page-num>
<header>I. 4. 3] HAPPINESS</header>

Until these people discover some nobler principle than any at which they currently stop, they must be left where they are and where they choose to be, never understanding what the Good of Life is to those who can make it theirs, never knowing to what kind of beings it is accessible.

->What then is happiness? Let us try basing it upon Life.<-

<margin>3.</margin>Now if we draw no distinction between kinds of life, everything that lives will be capable of happiness, and those will be truly happy who possess that one common gift of which every living thing is by nature receptive. We could not deny it to irrational beings while allowing it to rational ones. If happiness were inherent in mere existence, the common ground in which the cause of happiness could always take root would simply be life.

Those, then, who place happiness not in mere living but in the reasoning life seem to overlook the fact that they are not really making it depend upon life at all: they admit that this reasoning faculty, around which they center happiness, is a property (not the subject of a property): the subject, to them, must be the Reasoning-Life, since it is in this double term that they find the basis of happiness. So they are making it consist not in life but in a particular kind of life—not, of course, a species formally opposite, but, in our terminology, standing as an "earlier" to a "later" form within the one Kind.

Now, in common use, this word "Life" embraces many forms which shade down from primary to secondary and so on, all massed under the common term—the life of plants and the life of animals—each phase brighter or dimmer than the next. And so it evidently must be with the Good-of-Life. And if one thing is ever the image of another, then every Good must always be the image of a higher Good.

If mere Being is insufficient, if happiness demands fullness of life and exists, therefore, where nothing is lacking of all that belongs to the idea of life, then happiness can exist only in a being that lives fully.

And such a being will possess not merely the good, but the Supreme Good—if, that is to say, in the realm of existents, the Supreme Good can be

True happiness, therefore, can only exist in a being that possesses the "fullness of life" and the Supreme Good.</summary>

no other than the authentically living, no other than Life in its greatest fullness, life in which the good is present as something essential, not as something brought in from the outside; a life needing no foreign substance called in from a foreign realm to establish it in good.

For what could be added to the fullest life to make it the best life? If anyone should answer "The nature of Good" (The Good, as a Divine <term>Hypostasis</term>), the reply would certainly be close to our thought, but we are not seeking the Cause but the primary constituent.

It has been said more than once that the perfect life and the true life—the essential life—is in the Intellectual Nature beyond this sphere, and that all other forms of life are incomplete. They are phantoms of life, imperfect, not pure, and no more truly life than they are its opposite. Let it be said briefly here that since all living things proceed from the one principle but possess life in different degrees, this principle must be the first life and the most complete.

### 4.

If, then, the perfect life is within human reach, the person attaining it attains happiness; if not, happiness must be granted to the gods, for the perfect life is for them alone.

But since we hold that happiness is for human beings too, we must consider what this perfect life is. The matter may be stated thus:

It has been shown elsewhere that a human being, when they command not merely the life of sensation but also Reason and Authentic Intellection, has realized the perfect life.

But are we to picture this kind of life as something foreign imported into their nature?

No: there exists no single human being who does not, either potentially or effectively, possess this thing which we hold to constitute happiness.

But are we to think of a human being as including this perfect form of life merely as a partial constituent of their entire nature?

We say, rather, that while in some people it is present as a mere portion of their total being—specifically in those who have it potentially—there is,

<summary>Plotinus defines the perfect life as the "Intellectual Nature" which contains the Good essentially and argues that human happiness is possible because this state exists potentially or effectively within every person.</summary>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the perfect life, concluding that once a human being identifies with their highest intellectual nature, they achieve a state of happiness that is self-contained and independent of external circumstances.</meta>

<page-num>61</page-num>
<header>I. 4. 5] HAPPINESS</header>

...too, the person, already in possession of true happiness, who is this perfection realized, who has passed over into actual identification with it. All else is now mere clothing around the person, not to be called part of them since it lies around them unsought—not theirs because it is not appropriated to themselves by any act of the will.

To the person in this state, what is the Good?

They themselves are, by what they have and are.

And the author and principle of what they are and hold is the Supreme, which within Itself is the Good but manifests itself within the human being in this other mode.

The sign that this state has been achieved is that the person seeks nothing else.

What indeed could they be seeking? Certainly none of the less worthy things; and the Best they carry always within them.

Whoever has such a life as this has all they need in life.

Once the person is a Sage, the means of happiness and the way to good are within, for nothing is good that lies outside them. Anything they desire beyond this, they seek as a necessity, and not for themselves but for a subordinate—for the body bound to them, to which, since it has life, they must provide for the needs of life; though these are not needs to the true person of this degree. They know themselves to stand above all such things, and what they give to the lower nature, they give in such a way as to leave their true life undiminished.

Adverse fortune does not shake their happiness: the life so founded is forever stable. Suppose death strikes at their household or at their friends; they know what death is, just as the victims, if they are among the wise, know too. And if death, taking from them their familiars and intimates, does bring grief, it is not felt by them—not by the true person—but by that part of them which stands apart from the Supreme, by that lower person in whose distress they take no part.

### 5.

But what of sorrows, illnesses, and all else that inhibit native activity?

<meta>This page continues the investigation into whether happiness can be maintained in the face of physical suffering or unconsciousness, responding to critics who argue that human happiness requires both soul and body to be at peace.</meta>

<page-num>62</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

What about the suspension of consciousness that drugs or disease may bring about? Could either welfare or happiness be present under such conditions? And this says nothing of misery and disgrace, which will certainly be used as arguments against us, along with the inevitable *“Miseries of Priam.”* <note>A classical reference to the extreme misfortunes of King Priam of Troy, often cited in philosophy as the ultimate test of whether a happy man can be made miserable by external fate.</note>

“The Sage,” we shall be told, “may bear such afflictions and even take them lightly, but they could never be his choice; and the happy life must be one that would be chosen. The Sage, that is, cannot be thought of as simply a wise soul, while taking no account of the bodily principle in the total of the being. He will, no doubt, take everything bravely... until the body’s appeals come before him, and longings and loathings penetrate through the body to the inner man. And since pleasure must be counted toward the happy life, how can someone who knows the misery of misfortune or pain be happy, no matter how wise he may be? Such a state of self-contained bliss is for the Gods; humans, because of the less noble part joined to them, must seek happiness throughout their entire being and not merely in one part. If one constituent is troubled, the other, answering to its partner’s distress, must inevitably suffer a hindrance in its own activity. There is nothing left but to cut away the body or the body’s sensitive life to secure that self-contained unity essential to happiness.”

### 6.

Now, if happiness did indeed require freedom from pain, sickness, misfortune, and disaster, it would be utterly denied to anyone confronted by such trials. But if it lies in the fruition of the **Authentic Good**, why turn away from this Goal and look toward the means? Why imagine that to be happy, a person needs a variety of things, none of which actually enter into happiness? If, in fact, felicity were made up by heaping together everything that is at once desirable and necessary, we would have to strive for those things also. But if the Goal must be one and not many—if, in other words, our quest is for a single Goal and not for many goals—then only that which is ultimate and noblest can be chosen, that which calls to the deepest longings of the soul.

The quest and will of the Soul are not pointed directly towards

<summary>Plotinus addresses the argument that humans cannot be happy while suffering physically because we are composite beings of soul and body. He counters that if happiness is found in the "Authentic Good," then external misfortunes and bodily pains are merely external to that singular goal.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical inquiry into the nature of happiness, specifically addressing the role of physical comforts and "necessities" in the life of the Sage, and how the pursuit of the highest good differs from the mere avoidance of pain.</meta>

freedom from this sphere: the reason which disciplines away our concern about this life has no fundamental quarrel with things of this order; it merely resents their interference; sometimes, even, it must seek them; essentially all aspiration is not so much away from evil as toward the Soul's own highest and noblest: once this is attained, all is won and there is rest—and this is the truly willed state of life.

There can be no such thing as "willing" the acquisition of necessities, if Will is to be taken in its strict sense, and not misapplied to the mere recognition of need.

It is certain that we shrink from the unpleasant, and such shrinking is assuredly not what we would have willed; to have no occasion for any such shrinking would be much closer to our taste; but the things we seek tell the story as soon as they are ours. For instance, health and freedom from pain; which of these has any great charm? As long as we possess them, we place no value on them.

Anything which, when present, has no charm and adds nothing to happiness, and which when lacking is desired only because of the presence of an annoying opposite, may reasonably be called a necessity but not a Good.

Such things can never form part of our final object: our <term>Term</term> <note>The ultimate goal or end-state of human existence.</note> must be such that even if these pleasanter conditions are absent and their opposites are present, it shall remain, still, intact.

### 7.

Then why are these conditions sought and their opposites repelled by the man established in happiness?

Here is our answer:—

These more pleasant conditions cannot, it is true, add any bit to the Sage's happiness: but they do serve toward the integrity of his being, while the presence of the opposites tends against his Being or complicates the <term>Term</term>: it is not that the Sage can be so easily deprived of the <term>Term</term> achieved, but simply that he who holds the highest good desires to have that alone, not something else at the same time—something which, though it cannot banish the Good by its arrival, does nevertheless take up a place by its side.

In any case, if the man who has attained happiness meets some turn of fortune that he would not have chosen, there is not the slightest lessening of his happiness because of it. If there were, his happiness would be shifting or falling from day to day; the death of a child would bring him down, or the loss of some trivial possession. No: a thousand misfortunes and disappointments may befall him and leave him still in the tranquil possession of the <term>Term</term> <note>The ultimate goal or end-state of human existence.</note>.

But, they cry, "what about great disasters, not just the petty daily chances!"

What human thing, then, is great enough not to be despised by one who has mounted above all we know here, and is now bound no longer to anything below?

If the Sage thinks all fortunate events, however momentous, to be no great matter—kingdoms and the rule over cities and peoples, colonizations and the founding of states, even though all be his own handiwork—how can he take any great account of the fluctuations of power or the ruin of his homeland? Certainly, if he thought any such event a great disaster, or any disaster at all, he must have a very strange way of thinking. One who sets great store by wood and stones, or... by Zeus... by mortality among mortals, cannot yet be the Sage; whose estimate of death, we hold, must be that it is better than life in the body.

But suppose that he himself is offered as a victim in sacrifice?

Can he think it an evil to die beside the altars?

But if he goes unburied?

Wherever it lies, under the earth or over the earth, his body will always rot.

But if he has been hidden away, not with costly ceremony but in an unnamed grave, not counted worthy of a towering monument?

The littleness of it!

But if he falls into his enemies’ hands, into prison?

There is always the way towards escape, if there is no way towards well-being <note>Referring to physical safety or comfort.</note>.

But if his nearest and dearest are taken from him, his sons and daughters dragged away to captivity?

What then, we ask, if he had died without witnessing the wrong? Could he have left the world in the calm conviction that none of this could happen? He must be very shallow. Can he fail to see that

For the Sage, even the rise and fall of nations is trivial compared to the soul's ultimate goal.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Sage's resilience, explaining how his inner happiness remains untouched by external calamities, personal suffering, or the misfortunes of others.</meta>

<page-num>65</page-num>
<header>I. 4. 8] HAPPINESS</header>

It is possible for such calamities to overtake his household, but does he cease to be a happy man because he knows what might occur? In the knowledge of that possibility, he may remain at ease; so, too, even when the evil has actually happened.

He would reflect that the nature of this **All** <note>The universe or the whole of reality.</note> is such that it brings these things to pass, and man must bow his head.

Furthermore, in many cases, captivity will certainly prove to be an advantage; and those who suffer have their freedom in their own hands: if they stay, there is either a reason for staying—in which case they have no real grievance—or they stay against reason when they should not, and then they have only themselves to blame. Clearly, the follies of his neighbors, however close they are, cannot plunge the Sage into evil; his state of being cannot depend upon the good or bad fortunes of any other men.

### 8.

As for violent personal sufferings, he will endure them as well as he can; if they exceed his endurance, they will carry him off <note>Meaning the sufferings will lead to his death.</note>.

And so, in all his pain, he asks for no pity: there is always a radiance in the man’s inner soul, untroubled like the light in a lantern when fierce gusts beat around it in a wild turmoil of wind and tempest.

But what if he is pushed beyond his limits? What if pain grows so intense and tortures him so much that the agony almost kills him? Well, when he is subjected to torture, he will decide what must be done: he retains his freedom of action.

Besides, we must remember that the Sage sees things very differently from the average person; neither ordinary experiences nor pains and sorrows—whether affecting himself or others—pierce through to his inner stronghold. To allow them such an entry would be a weakness in our soul.

And it is also a sign of weakness, too, if we should think it a benefit not to hear of miseries, or a benefit to die before they arrive: this is not a concern for the welfare of others, but for our own peace of mind. Here we see our imperfection: we must not indulge it; we must cast it away and cease to tremble over what might perhaps happen.

Anyone who says that it is in human nature to grieve over mis-

We cannot be lazy: this world is an arena for the powerful combatant holding his ground against the blows of fortune, and knowing that, painful though they may be to some natures, they are little to his—nothing dreadful, merely nursery terrors.

So, would the Sage have desired misfortune?

It is precisely to meet the undesired when it appears that he possesses the virtue which gives him, to confront it, his passionless and unshakeable soul.

### 9.

But what when he is out of his mind, his reason quenched by sickness or by magic arts?

If it be granted that in this state, resting as it were in a slumber, he remains a Sage, why should he not equally remain happy? No one excludes him from happiness in the hours of sleep; no one counts up that time and so denies that he has been happy all his life.

If they say that, failing consciousness, he is no longer the Sage, then they are no longer reasoning about the Sage: but we do suppose a Sage, and are inquiring whether, as long as he is the Sage, he is in the state of happiness.

“Well, let him remain a Sage,” they say, “still, having no sensation and not expressing his virtue in action, how can he be happy?”

But a man unconscious of his health may be, nonetheless, healthy: a man may not be aware of his personal attraction, but he remains handsome nonetheless: if he has no sense of his wisdom, shall he be any the less wise?

It may perhaps be urged that sensation and consciousness are essential to wisdom and that happiness is only wisdom brought to action.

Now, this argument might have weight if prudence and wisdom were something fetched in from outside: but this is not so: wisdom is, in its

<summary>Plotinus argues that the Sage's happiness and virtue are internal qualities that persist even during sleep, illness, or loss of consciousness. He contends that just as health or beauty exists without the possessor's constant awareness, wisdom and happiness remain even when not actively perceived or expressed.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that the Sage's happiness and wisdom persist even when he is unconscious or mentally incapacitated, because his true essence lies in the Intellectual-Principle, which is always active.</meta>
