<meta>I. 4. 10]</meta>

essential nature, an Authentic-Existence, or rather is The Authentic-Existent—and this Existent does not perish in one who is asleep or, to take the particular case presented to us, in the man who is out of his mind: the Act of this Existent is continuous within him; and is a sleepless activity: the Sage, therefore, even when unconscious, is still the Sage in Act.

This activity is hidden not from the whole man but merely from one part of him: we have here a parallel to what happens in the activity of the physical or vegetative life in us, which is not made known by the sensitive faculty to the rest of the man: if our physical life really constituted the “We,” its Act would be our Act: but, in fact, this physical life is not the “We”; the “We” is the activity of the Intellectual-Principle so that when the Intellective is in Act we are in Act.

### 10.

Perhaps the reason this continuous activity remains unperceived is that it has no contact whatever with things of sense. No doubt action upon material things, or action dictated by them, must proceed through the sensitive faculty which exists for that use: but why should there not be an immediate activity of the Intellectual-Principle and of the soul that attends it, the soul that precedes sensation or any perception? For, if <term>Intellection</term> and <term>Authentic-Existence</term> are identical, this “priority to perception” must be a thing having Act.

Let us explain the conditions under which we become conscious of this <term>Intellective-Act</term>.

When the Intellect is oriented upward, that (lower part of it) which contains (or, corresponds to) the life of the <term>Soul</term>, is, so to speak, flung down again and becomes like the reflection resting on the smooth and shining surface of a mirror; in this illustration, when the mirror is in place the image appears but, though the mirror be absent or out of gear, all that would have acted and produced an image still exists; so in the case of the <term>Soul</term>; when there is peace in that within us which is capable of reflecting the images of the <term>Rational Principle</term> and <term>Intellectual-Principles</term> these images appear. Then, side by side with the primal

<summary>Plotinus argues that the Sage's true essence and happiness remain active even during sleep or madness because the Intellectual-Principle operates independently of sensory awareness. He uses the metaphor of a mirror to explain that while we only perceive our internal activity when our lower soul is calm, the activity itself remains constant regardless of our perception.</summary>
<keywords>Sage, Intellectual-Principle, Authentic-Existence, consciousness, Intellection, Soul, Rational Principle, mirror metaphor, activity in act</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of consciousness and the Sage's internal activity, explaining how the Intellectual-Principle operates even when not reflected in the "mirror" of the imagination.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

knowledge of the activity of the Rational and the Intellectual-Principles, we also have, as it were, a sense-perception of their operation.

When, on the contrary, the mirror within is shattered through some disturbance of the harmony of the body, Reason and the Intellectual-Principle act without being pictured: intellection is unattended by imagination.

In short, we may safely conclude that while the Intellective-Act may be accompanied by the Imaging Principle, it is not to be confused with it.

And even in our conscious life, we can point to many noble activities, of both mind and hand alike, which at the time in no way force themselves upon our consciousness. A reader will often be quite unconscious of reading when he is most intent; in a feat of courage, there can be no awareness either of the brave action or of the fact that all that is done conforms to the rules of courage. This is true in countless other cases.

So it would even seem that consciousness tends to blunt the activities upon which it is exercised, and that to the degree in which these pass unobserved, they are purer and have more effect and more vitality. Consequently, the Sage who has arrived at this state has the truer fullness of life—a life not poured out in sensation but gathered closely within itself.

## II.

We shall perhaps be told that in such a state the man is no longer alive; we answer that such people show themselves equally unable to understand his inner life and his happiness.

If this does not satisfy them, we must ask them to keep in mind a living Sage and, on these terms, to inquire whether the man is in a state of happiness. They must not whittle away his life and then ask whether he has the happy life; they must not take away the man and then look for the happiness of a man. Once they allow that the Sage lives within, they must not seek him among outer activities, still less look to the outer world for the object of his desires. To consider the outer world to be a field for his desire, or to imagine the Sage desiring any external good, would be to deny Substantial-Existence to happiness; for the Sage would like to see all men prosperous and no evil befalling anyone; but though it proves otherwise, he is still content.

<summary>Plotinus argues that consciousness can actually diminish the purity of intellectual activity and that the Sage's happiness remains self-contained and independent of external circumstances. True intellectual acts do not require the "mirror" of imagination or conscious awareness to be effective and vital.</summary>

<keywords>Intellectual-Principle, consciousness, imagination, the Sage, happiness, Substantial-Existence, internal life, self-sufficiency</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the Sage's life is defined by internal intellectual activity rather than external circumstances, emphasizing that true happiness is found in a state of being rather than in physical or emotional pleasure.</meta>

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>I. 4. 13] HAPPINESS</header>

If we admit that such a desire would be against reason—since evil cannot cease to exist—then there is no escape from agreeing with us that the Sage’s will is always and only directed inward.

### 12.

The pleasure required for the Sage’s life cannot be found in the enjoyments of the dissolute or in any gratifications of the body—there is no place for these, as they stifle happiness—nor in any violent emotions—for what could move the Sage so deeply? It can only be the kind of pleasure that must exist where the Good is: a pleasure that does not arise from movement and is not a matter of process. For all that is good is immediately present to the Sage, and the Sage is present to himself; his pleasure and his contentment remain immovable.

Thus, he is always cheerful, and the order of his life is always untroubled. His state is fixed in happiness, and nothing known as evil can knock it off course—provided only that he is and remains a Sage.

If anyone looks for some other kind of pleasure in the life of the Sage, it is not the life of the Sage they are looking for.

### 13.

His characteristic activities are not hindered by external events but simply adapt to them, remaining always noble, and perhaps all the more noble for dealing with reality. When he has to handle specific cases and objects, he may not be able to put his vision into action without investigation and thought; but the one greatest principle is always present to him, like a part of his very being. It is most present of all even should he be a victim in the famous <term>Bull of Phalaris</term>. No doubt, despite all that has been said, it is useless to pretend that this is a pleasant place to stay; but what cries out in the Bull is the part that feels the torture. In the Sage, there is something else as well: <term>The Self-Gathered</term>, which, as long as it holds itself by its own strength within itself, can never be robbed of the vision of the <term>All-Good</term>.

<vocab>Sage, happiness, Bull of Phalaris, The Self-Gathered, All-Good, Plotinus, Enneads</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that a Sage's happiness is an internal, immovable state of contentment that remains independent of physical pleasure or external suffering. Even under extreme torture, such as in the Bull of Phalaris, the Sage's higher intellectual self remains "Self-Gathered" and focused on the "All-Good."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous section, asserting that the Sage's true life and happiness are centered in the soul, independent of the body's physical condition or external status.</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

### 14.

For man, and especially the Sage, is not the union of soul and body; the proof is that man can be detached from the body and look down upon its superficial goods.

It would be absurd to think that happiness begins and ends with the living body. Happiness is the possession of the good of life; it is centered, therefore, in the Soul and is an Act of the Soul—and not even of the whole Soul. For it certainly is not a characteristic of the <term>vegetative soul</term> (the soul of growth); that would immediately connect it with the body.

A powerful physique, a healthy constitution, or even a happy balance of temperament certainly do not create happiness. In fact, if these advantages are excessive, there is a danger that the man may be weighed down and forced more and more under their power. There must be a kind of counter-pressure in the opposite direction, toward the noblest things. The body must be diminished and reduced so that the true man—the man behind appearances—may reveal himself.

Even if an earth-bound man is handsome, powerful, and rich, and so well-suited to this world that he rules the entire human race, he is still not to be envied; he is merely a fool for such temptations. Perhaps such splendors could never have been gathered by the Sage in the first place; but if they were, he would voluntarily lower his status if he cared for his true life. He will diminish the body's tyranny or wear it away by ignoring its demands, and he will set aside any positions of power. While he will protect his physical health, he will not wish to be entirely untested by sickness, and certainly not to never feel pain. If such troubles do not come to him naturally, he will want to experience them, at least during his youth. In old age, it is true, he will want neither pain nor pleasure to hinder him; he will desire nothing of this world, whether pleasant or painful. His single desire will be to be unaware of the body. If he encounters pain, he will confront it with the inner powers he possesses; but pleasure, health, and a comfortable life will not increase his happiness, nor will their opposites destroy or diminish it.

When a positive quality cannot add anything to a subject, how can the negative take anything away?

<vocab>Plotinus, Sage, union, soul, body, happiness, vegetative soul, happiness</vocab>

<summary>Plotinus argues that the Sage is defined by the soul rather than the body, and that true happiness is an internal state that physical health or worldly success cannot increase. The Sage actively seeks to diminish the influence of the body and remains indifferent to both physical pleasure and pain.</summary>

<header>I. 4. 16] HAPPINESS</header>

## 15.

But suppose there are two wise men, one of them possessing everything considered naturally desirable, while the other meets only with the very opposite: do we assert that they have an equal happiness?

We do, if they are equally wise.

What if one is favored in his body and in everything else that does not help toward wisdom, and even less toward virtue, toward the vision of the noblest things, or toward being the highest—what does all that amount to? The man commanding all such practical advantages cannot flatter himself that he is more truly happy than the man without them; the greatest abundance of such boons would not even help to make a flute-player.

We discuss the happy man based on our own weaknesses; we count as alarming and grave what his happiness takes lightly. He would be neither wise nor in a state of happiness if he had not stopped trifling with such things and become, as it were, another being—having confidence in his own nature and faith that evil can never touch him. In such a spirit, he can be fearless through and through; where there is dread, there is not perfect virtue; such a man is only a sort of half-thing.

As for any involuntary fear rising in him and taking his judgment by surprise, perhaps while his thoughts are elsewhere, the Sage will attack it and drive it out. He will, so to speak, calm the rebellious child within him, whether by reason or by threat, but without passion—just as an infant might feel rebuked by a stern glance.

This does not make the Sage unfriendly or harsh: it is for himself and in his own great concern that he is the Sage. Giving freely to his intimates of all he has to give, he will be the best of friends by his very union with the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>.

## 16.

Those who refuse to place the Sage high in the <term>Intellectual Realm</term> but instead drag him down to the level of the accidental, fearing accidents for him, have substituted the Sage we have in mind for another person altogether; they offer us a tolerable sort of man and they assign to him a life of

<summary>Plotinus argues that true happiness is found in wisdom and internal character rather than external circumstances or physical health. The Sage remains happy and fearless regardless of fortune because his life is centered on the Intellectual-Principle.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Sage, happiness, virtue, Intellectual-Principle, fear, wisdom, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the Sage's happiness is independent of external circumstances, focusing here on the relationship between the Soul, the body, and the ultimate Goal.</meta>

->PLOTINUS<-

...a mixture of good and evil—a case, after all, not easy to imagine. But admitting the possibility of such a mixed state, it could not deserve to be called a life of happiness; it falls short of greatness, both in the dignity of Wisdom and in the integrity of the Good. The life of true happiness is not a mixture. And Plato rightly taught that he who is to be wise and to possess happiness draws his good from the Supreme, focusing his gaze on That, becoming like That, and living by That.

He can care for no other <term>Term</term> <note>The final goal or end (Telos).</note> than That: all else he will attend to only as he might change his residence, not in expectation of any increase to his established happiness, but simply through a reasonable attention to the different conditions surrounding him as he lives here or there.

He will give to the body all that he sees to be useful and possible, but he himself remains a member of another order, not prevented from leaving the body and necessarily leaving it at nature’s hour, he himself always the master to decide regarding it.

Thus, some part of his life considers exclusively the Soul’s fulfillment; the rest is not directly for the sake of the Goal and not for his own sake, but for the thing bound up with him—the thing which he tends and puts up with just as the musician cares for his lyre as long as it can serve him. When the lyre fails him, he will change it, or will give up the lyre and playing the lyre, as he now has another craft that needs no lyre; then he will let it rest neglected at his side while he sings on without an instrument. But it was not in vain that the instrument was given to him in the beginning: he has found it useful many a time until now.

### FIFTH TRACTATE

## HAPPINESS AND THE EXTENSION OF TIME

I.

Is it possible to think that happiness increases with time—happiness which is always understood as a present thing?

The memory of former happiness may surely be ruled out of the account, for happiness is not a matter of words, but a definite condition which must be actually present, like the very fact and act of life.

<vocab>Plotinus, Plato, Happiness, Wisdom, Good, Supreme, Soul, Tractate, Time, felicity, lyre</vocab>

He also begins an inquiry into whether happiness can increase over time, suggesting it must be a present reality rather than a cumulative memory.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation into the nature of happiness, specifically addressing and refuting the argument that the duration of time contributes to or increases one's well-being.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>73</page-num>
<header>TIME AND HAPPINESS</header>
<meta>I. 5. 4]</meta>

### 2.

It may be argued that our will to live and to engage in expressive activity is constant, and that every achievement of such expression is an increase in Happiness.

But first, by this reasoning, every tomorrow’s well-being would be greater than today’s, and every later installment would be progressively larger than the one before; immediately, time would replace moral excellence as the measure of happiness.

Then again, the Gods today would have to be happier than they were in the past; and their bliss, too, would not be perfect and would never be perfect. Furthermore, when the will attains what it is seeking, it attains something in the present; the search is always for something to be actually present until a permanent happiness is finally achieved. The will to life, which is the will to Existence, aims at something present, since Existence must be a stable, present thing. Even when an act of the will is directed toward the future, even the most distant future, its object is an actually present possessing and being. There is no concern for what is past or what is to come; the future state a man seeks is to be a "now" to him. He does not care about "forever"; he asks only that an actual present be truly present.

### 3.

Yes, but what if well-being has lasted a long time, or if that present spectacle has been before one's eyes for a longer duration?

If, in that greater length of time, the man has gained deeper insight, then time has certainly done something for him; but if the entire process has brought him no further vision, then a single glance would provide everything he has gained.

### 4.

Still, has not the one life known pleasure longer than the other?

But pleasure cannot be fairly included in Happiness—unless, indeed, by "pleasure" one means the unhindered Activity <note>A translation of the Greek term *energeia*.</note> of the true man, in which case this pleasure is simply our "Happiness." And even pleasure, though it exists continuously, never possesses anything but the present; its past is over and finished.

He maintains that if time increased happiness, the bliss of the Gods would be incomplete, and moral excellence would be secondary to mere longevity.</summary>

### 5.

It will be objected, then: are we asked to believe that if one man has been happy from beginning to end, another only at the end, and a third—who began with happiness—lost it, their shares of happiness are equal?

This is straying from the question: we were comparing those who are happy among themselves. Now we are asked to compare those who are not happy at the time they lack happiness with those who are in actual possession of it. If the latter are better off, they are so as people in possession of happiness compared to those without it; their advantage is always determined by something in the present.

### 6.

Well, but consider the unhappy man: must not the increase of time bring an increase in his unhappiness? Do not all troubles—long-lasting pains, sorrows, and everything of that type—yield a greater total of misery over a longer time? And if misery’s evil is increased by time, why should time not equally increase happiness when all is well?

Regarding sorrows and pains, there is undoubtedly reason to say that time brings an increase; for example, in a lingering illness, the evil hardens into a fixed condition, and as time goes on, the body is brought lower and lower. But if the constitution did not deteriorate—if the harm grew no worse—then here, too, there would be no trouble except for that of the present moment. We cannot include the past in the tally of unhappiness except in the sense that it has gone toward creating an actually existing state—that is, in the sense that the evil in the sufferer’s condition, having been extended over a longer time, has gained ground. The increase of ill-being, then, is due to the aggravation of the illness, not to the extension of time.

It may also be pointed out that this greater length of time is not something that exists at any given moment; surely a "greater amount" cannot be made by adding something that has passed away to something actually present.

No: true happiness is not vague and fluid; it is an unchanging state.

<vocab>Plotinus, happiness, unhappiness, time, misery, state, lingering illness, ill-being</vocab>

<summary>Plotinus argues that happiness is an unchanging state that does not accumulate over time, and that while misery may seem to increase with duration, this is actually due to the worsening of one's condition rather than time itself.</summary>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical inquiry into whether the duration of time increases the value of happiness, moving from a comparison of happiness and misery to a distinction between time and eternity.</meta>

# TIME AND HAPPINESS

If there is in this matter any increase besides that of mere time, it is in the sense that a greater happiness is the reward of a higher virtue. This is not counting the years of its duration toward the credit of happiness; it is simply noting the high-water mark once and for all attained.

### 7.

But if we are to consider only the present and may not call upon the past to make up the total, why do we not calculate it this way in the case of time itself? In that case, in fact, we do not hesitate to add the past to the present and call the total greater. Why not assume a quantity of happiness equivalent to a quantity of time? This would be nothing more than taking it lap by lap to correspond with time-laps, instead of choosing to consider it as an indivisible whole, measurable only by the content of a given instant.

There is no absurdity in taking count of time which has ceased to exist: we are merely counting what is past and finished, as we might count the dead. But to treat past happiness as actually existing and as outweighing present happiness—that is an absurdity. For Happiness must be an achieved and existing state, whereas any time beyond the present is non-existent; all progress of time means the extinction of all the time that has passed.

Hence, time is aptly described as an imitation of eternity that seeks to break up, in its fragmentary flight, the permanence of its original model. Thus, whatever time seizes and claims for itself from what stands permanent in eternity is annihilated—saved only so far as it still belongs to eternity to some degree, but wholly destroyed if it is unreservedly absorbed into time.

If Happiness demands the possession of the good of life, it clearly relates to the life of Authentic-Existence, for that life is the Best. Now, the life of Authentic-Existence is measured not by time but by eternity; and eternity is not a matter of "more" or "less" or a thing of any magnitude, but is the unchangeable, the indivisible; it is timeless Being.

We must not confuse Being and Non-Being, time and eternity, nor even everlasting time with the eternal; we cannot create stages and laps out of an absolute unity; all must be taken together, where—

<summary>The author argues that happiness is an existing state of "Authentic-Existence" belonging to eternity rather than time, meaning it cannot be increased by duration. Time is viewed as a fragmented imitation of eternity's permanence, and thus past time or past happiness has no real existence in the present.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, eternity vs time, Authentic-Existence, nature of happiness, Being and Non-Being, virtue, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between time and happiness, specifically addressing whether the memory of past experiences or the accumulation of good actions over a long duration increases one's state of felicity.</meta>

...existence, however and in whatever way we handle it; and it must be taken as such—not even as an undivided block of time, but as the Life of Eternity: a duration not made up of periods but completely rounded, existing outside of any concept of time.

### 8.

It may be argued that the actual presence of past experiences, kept present by memory, gives an advantage to the man who has enjoyed a longer period of happiness.

But, what kind of experiences are we remembering?

It is either a memory of formerly attained wisdom and virtue—in which case we simply have a better man, and the argument based on memory is abandoned—or it is a memory of past pleasures. If it is the latter, it implies that the man who has arrived at happiness must roam far and wide in search of gratifications and is not contented by the bliss actually within him.

And what is actually pleasant about the memory of pleasure? What does it matter to recall yesterday’s excellent dinner? It is even more ridiculous to recall one from ten years ago. The same applies to the memory of last year’s morality.

### 9.

But is there not something to be said for the memory of various forms of beauty?

That is the resource of a man whose life is without beauty in the present; because he lacks it now, he grasps at the memory of what once was.

### 10.

But, it may be said, a long duration of time produces an abundance of good actions that are missed by the man whose attainment of the happy state is recent—if indeed we can think at all of a state of happiness where good actions have been few.

Now, to make multiplicity—whether in time or in action—essential to Happiness is to construct it by combining non-existent things (represented by the past) with the one thing that actually exists. This consideration it

<summary>Plotinus argues that memory of past pleasures or actions does not increase present happiness, as happiness is a complete state within the self. He contends that relying on the past to define happiness is a mistake because the past no longer exists.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, eternity, time, memory, happiness, virtue, pleasure, beauty, existence, philosophical ethics</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page concludes the argument that happiness is found in the internal state of the soul rather than the duration or number of external actions, and begins a new tractate on the nature of Beauty.</meta>

...was what led us at the very beginning to place Happiness in what actually exists, and on that basis to launch our inquiry as to whether a higher degree of happiness was determined by a longer time. It might be thought that Happiness of a longer duration must surpass the shorter because of the greater number of actions it included.

But, to begin with, men who live completely outside of the active life may attain the state of felicity—and not to a lesser, but to a greater degree than men of affairs.

Secondly, the good does not derive from the act itself, but from the inner disposition which prompts noble conduct: the wise and good man, in his very actions, harvests the good not through what he does, but through what he is.

A wicked man, no less than a Sage, may save the country, and the benefit of the act is the same for everyone, no matter whose hand performed the saving. The contentment of the Sage does not depend upon such actions and events: it is his own inner habit that creates both his felicity and whatever pleasure may accompany it.

To place Happiness in actions is to place it in things that are outside of virtue and outside of the Soul; for the Soul’s expression is not in action but in wisdom, in a contemplative operation within itself; and this, and this alone, is Happiness.

## SIXTH TRACTATE

### BEAUTY

I.
Beauty addresses itself chiefly to sight; but there is a beauty for hearing as well, such as in certain combinations of words and in all kinds of music, for melodies and cadences are beautiful. Minds that lift themselves above the realm of sense to a higher order are aware of beauty in the conduct of life, in actions, in character, and in the pursuits of the intellect; and there is the beauty of the virtues. What loftier beauty there may be, our argument will yet bring to light.

What, then, is it that gives comeliness to material forms and draws

<meta>This page continues the inquiry into the nature of Beauty, following the introduction of the Sixth Tractate which established that beauty exists in both the sensory realm (sight and sound) and the intellectual realm (virtue and knowledge).</meta>

<header>PLOTINUS</header>

...the ear to the sweetness perceived in sounds? And what is the secret of the beauty found in everything that derives from the Soul?

Is there some Single Principle from which all things derive their grace, or is there a beauty specific to the embodied and another for the bodiless? Finally, whether one or many, what would such a Principle be?

Consider that some things, material shapes for instance, are graceful not by anything inherent in them, but by something communicated to them, while others are lovely in themselves—as, for example, Virtue.

The same bodies appear beautiful at some times and not at others; so there is a great difference between being a body and being beautiful.

What, then, is this "something" that reveals itself in certain material forms? This is the natural starting point for our inquiry.

What is it that attracts the eyes of those to whom a beautiful object is presented? What calls them and lures them toward it, filling them with joy at the sight? If we can grasp this, we will immediately have a vantage point for a broader survey.

Almost everyone claims that the symmetry of parts in relation to each other and to the whole—along with a certain charm of color—constitutes the beauty recognized by the eye. They argue that in visible things, as indeed in everything else universally, a beautiful thing is essentially symmetrical and patterned.

But consider what this implies.

Only a compound can be beautiful; nothing devoid of parts could ever be so. Only a whole could have beauty; its several parts would have no beauty in themselves, but would only work together to produce an attractive total. Yet beauty in an aggregate demands beauty in its details; it cannot be constructed out of ugliness. Its law must run throughout the whole.

If this were true, all the loveliness of color and even the light of the sun—being devoid of parts and therefore not beautiful by symmetry—would have to be ruled out of the realm of beauty. And how does gold come to be a beautiful thing? And lightning by night, or the stars—why are these so fair?

In the realm of sound, simple tones would also have to be forbidden, even though in a noble composition, each individual tone is often delicious in itself.

Again, since the same face, with its symmetry remaining constant, appears sometimes...

<header>BEAUTY</header>

fair and sometimes not, can we doubt that beauty is something more than symmetry, and that symmetry itself owes its beauty to a more fundamental principle?

Look at what is attractive in ways of living or in the expression of thought; are we to call it symmetry here? What symmetry can be found in noble conduct, or excellent laws, or in any form of intellectual pursuit?

What symmetry can there be in points of abstract thought?

Is it the symmetry of being in agreement with each other? But there may be agreement or even entire identity where there is nothing but ugliness: the proposition that honesty is merely a generous simplicity harmonizes perfectly with the proposition that morality means weakness of will; the agreement is complete.

Furthermore, all the virtues are a beauty of the soul, a beauty more authentic than any of these others; but how does symmetry apply here? It is true that the soul is not a simple unity, but still its virtue cannot have the symmetry of size or number: what standard of measurement could govern the balancing or the merging of the soul’s faculties or purposes?

Finally, according to this theory, how could there be beauty in the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>, which is essentially solitary?

## 2.

Let us, then, return to the source and identify at once the Principle that gives beauty to material things.

Undoubtedly this Principle exists; it is something perceived at first glance, something the soul recognizes as if from an ancient knowledge and, recognizing it, welcomes it and enters into harmony with it.

But when the soul encounters the Ugly, it immediately shrinks into itself, rejects the thing, and turns away from it, being out of harmony with it and resenting it.

Our interpretation is that the soul—by the very truth of its nature and its connection to the noblest <term>Existents</term> in the hierarchy of Being—

<vocab>Beauty, symmetry, Intellectual-Principle, soul, Existents, hierarchy of Being, material things</vocab>

<summary>Plotinus argues that beauty cannot be defined solely by symmetry, as beauty exists in non-physical virtues and thoughts where symmetry is irrelevant. He proposes that the soul recognizes beauty through an innate connection to higher principles of Being.</summary>

<warning>Minor show-through from the reverse page; surface wear/fading affecting the word "mastered" in the fourth paragraph.</warning>

When the soul sees anything of that kind, or any trace of that kinship, it thrills with immediate delight, claims it as its own, and is thus stirred once more to the sense of its own nature and all its connections.

But is there any such likeness between the beauty of this world and the splendors of the Supreme? Such a likeness in specific details would make the two orders similar; but what is there in common between beauty here and beauty There?

We maintain that all the beauty of this world comes from participating in the Ideal-Form.

All shapelessness that is capable of receiving pattern and form, as long as it remains outside of Reason and the Idea, is ugly because of that very isolation from Divine-Thought. And this is the Absolute Ugly: an ugly thing is something that has not been entirely mas<unclear>te</unclear>red by pattern—that is, by Reason—because the Matter does not yield at all points and in all respects to the Ideal-Form.

But where the Ideal-Form has entered, it has grouped and coordinated what was once a diversity of parts into a unity. It has transformed confusion into cooperation and made the whole into one harmonious coherence; for the Idea is a unity, and what it shapes must become a unity as far as multiplicity allows.

And upon what has thus been compacted into unity, Beauty enthrones itself, giving itself to the parts as well as to the whole. When it settles on a natural unity, a thing made of similar parts, it gives itself to that whole. For example, there is the beauty conferred by craftsmanship on a house and all its parts, and the beauty that some natural quality may give to a single stone.

This, then, is how a material thing becomes beautiful—by participating in the thought that flows from the Divine.

### 3.

The soul possesses a faculty specifically dedicated to Beauty—one that is incomparably certain in recognizing its own kind, never doubting whenever any lovely thing is presented for judgment.

Or perhaps the soul itself acts immediately, affirming what is Beautiful

where it finds something that matches the Ideal-Form within itself, using this Idea as a standard of accuracy in its judgment.

But what agreement is there between material things and that which exists before all Matter?

On what principle does the architect judge a house to be beautiful when he sees it matching his inner ideal of a house? Is it not because the house before him—setting aside the stones—is the inner idea imprinted upon the mass of external matter, the indivisible shown through diversity?

It is the same with the faculty of perception: when it recognizes in certain objects the Ideal-Form that has restricted and controlled formless matter (which is naturally opposite to the Idea), and when it further sees imprinted upon ordinary shapes a form superior to the ordinary, it collects what is still fragmented into a unity. It takes this up and brings it inside, no longer as something made of parts, and presents it to the Ideal-Principle as something harmonious and suited, a natural friend. The joy here is like that of a good man who recognizes in a youth the early signs of a virtue consistent with the perfection already achieved within his own soul.

The beauty of color is also the result of a unification; it comes from shape, and from the victory over the darkness inherent in Matter by the pouring-in of light—the non-physical, which is a Rational-Principle and an Ideal-Form.

This is why Fire itself is more magnificent than all other physical bodies, holding the rank of an Ideal-Principle compared to the other elements. It is always rising, the finest and most active of all bodies, being very close to the non-physical. It alone admits no other element, while all the others are penetrated by it; for they receive warmth, but fire is never cold. It possesses color primarily; the others receive the Form of color from it. This is the source of the brilliance of its light—a brilliance that belongs to the Idea. And anything that has resisted this light or is only weakly held by it remains outside of beauty, because it has not absorbed the fullness of the Form of color.

And inaudible harmonies create the harmonies we hear, awakening the soul to the consciousness of beauty by showing it the same essence in a different form. For the proportions of the music we perceive through our senses are not

<summary>Plotinus explains that the soul recognizes beauty when material objects reflect internal Ideal-Forms, using examples like architecture, the brilliance of fire, and the relationship between physical and spiritual harmony.</summary>

...arbitrary but are determined by the Principle whose labor is to dominate Matter and bring pattern into being.
   Thus far we have spoken of the beauties of the sensory realm—images and shadow-pictures, fugitives that have entered into Matter to adorn and to enrapture wherever they are seen.

## 4.

But there are earlier and loftier beauties than these. In a life bound by the senses, we are no longer permitted to know them; but the soul, without help from the physical organs, sees and proclaims them. We must ascend to the vision of these, leaving the senses in their own lowly place.
   Just as those who have never seen or known the grace of the material world—suppose, for instance, those born blind—cannot speak of its graceful forms, so too must those remain silent regarding the beauty of noble conduct, of learning, and of that whole order who have never cared for such things. Nor may those speak of the splendor of virtue who have never known the face of Justice and of Moral-Wisdom, which is beautiful beyond the beauty of the Evening and the Dawn.
   Such vision is only for those who see with the Soul’s sight—and at that vision, they will rejoice, and awe will fall upon them, and an agitation deeper than any other could ever stir, for they are now moving in the realm of Truth.
   This is the spirit that Beauty must always inspire: wonder and a delicious agitation, longing and love, and a trembling that is pure delight. This may be felt for the unseen just as for the seen; and souls feel this for it—every soul to some degree, but those the <unclear>m</unclear>ore deeply who are more truly suited for this higher love. Just as everyone takes delight in the beauty of the body, not everyone is stung as sharply; only those who feel the keener wound are known as **Lovers**.

## 5.

These **Lovers**, then, lovers of the beauty beyond the senses, must be made to declare themselves.
   What do you feel in the presence of the grace you discern in actions,

# I. 6. 5] BEAUTY

...in manners, in sound morality, in all the works and fruits of virtue, in the beauty of souls? When you see that you yourselves are beautiful within, what do you feel? What is this <term>Dionysiac</term> exultation that thrills through your being, this straining upwards of all your Soul, this longing to break away from the body and live immersed within your true self?

These are nothing other than the emotions of Souls under the spell of love.

But what is it that awakens all this passion? Not shape, not color, not grandeur of mass: all is for a Soul—something whose beauty rests upon no color—for the moral wisdom the Soul enshrines and all the other colorless splendor of the virtues. It is that you find in yourself, or admire in another: loftiness of spirit; righteousness of life; disciplined purity; courage with its majestic face; dignity; modesty that goes fearless, tranquil, and passionless; and, shining down upon all, the light of god-like <term>Intellection</term>.

All these noble qualities are to be revered and loved, no doubt, but what entitles them to be called beautiful?

They exist: they manifest themselves to us: anyone who sees them must admit that they have reality of Being; and is not <term>Real-Being</term> truly beautiful?

But we have not yet shown by what property in them they have shaped the Soul to loveliness: what is this grace, this splendor as of Light, resting upon all the virtues?

Let us take the contrary, the ugliness of the Soul, and set that against its beauty: to understand, at once, what this ugliness is and how it comes to appear in the Soul will certainly open our way before us.

Let us then suppose an ugly Soul: dissolute, unjust; teeming with all lusts; torn by internal discord; beset by the fears of its cowardice and the envies of its pettiness; thinking, in the limited thoughts it has, only of the perishable and the base; perverse in all its impulses; the friend of unclean pleasures; living a life of abandonment to bodily sensation and delighting in its deformity.

What must we think but that all this shame is something that has gathered around the Soul—some foreign affliction outraging it, soiling it, so

<header>PLOTINUS</header>

...that, burdened with every kind of depravity, it no longer has a clean activity or a clean sensation, but commands only a life smoldering dully under the crust of evil; that, sunk in manifold death, it no longer sees what a Soul should see, and may no longer rest in its own being, dragged as it always is toward the outer, the lower, and the dark?

An unclean thing, I dare to say; flickering here and there at the call of sensory objects, deeply infected with the taint of the body, always occupied with Matter, and absorbing Matter into itself; in its dealings with the ignoble, it has traded away its own essential Idea for an alien nature.

If a man has been immersed in filth or smeared with mud, his natural beauty disappears, and all that is seen is the foul stuff covering him: his ugly condition is due to the alien matter that has encrusted him, and if he is to win back his grace, it must be his business to scour and purify himself and make himself what he once was.

So, we may rightly say, a Soul becomes ugly—by something forced upon it, by sinking itself into the alien, by a fall, a descent into the body, into Matter. The dishonor of the Soul is in its ceasing to be clean and apart. Gold is degraded when it is mixed with earthy particles; if these are worked out, the gold is left and is beautiful, isolated from all that is foreign, gold with gold alone. And so the Soul; let it but be cleared of the desires that come from its too intimate connection with the body, emancipated from all the passions, purged of all that embodiment has forced upon it, and withdrawn, as a solitary, to itself again—in that moment the ugliness that came only from the alien is stripped away.

### 6.

For, as the ancient teaching went, moral discipline and courage and every virtue, not even excepting Wisdom itself, all are purification.

Hence the <term>Mysteries</term> with good reason foreshadow the immersion of the unpurified in filth, even in the Nether-World, since the unclean love filth for its very filthiness, and swine with foul bodies find their joy in foulness.

What else is <term>Sophrosyny</term> <note>Sophrosyny: a Greek philosophical term meaning temperance, self-control, or soundness of mind.</note>, rightly so-called, but to take no part in the

<header>I. 6. 7] BEAUTY</header>

Is it not also the case with the pleasures of the body, to break away from them as unclean and unworthy of the pure? So too, Courage is simply being fearless of the death which is nothing more than the parting of the Soul from the body, an event which no one can dread whose delight is to be their own unmingled self. And Magnanimity is simply the disregard for the lure of things here. And Wisdom is but the activity of the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> withdrawn from the lower places and leading the Soul to the realms above.

The Soul thus cleansed is entirely Form and Reason, wholly free of the body, intellectual, and belonging entirely to that divine order from which the wellspring of Beauty rises and all the race of Beauty.

Hence the Soul, elevated to the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>, is beautiful to the full extent of its power. For the act of intellect and all that proceeds from it are the Soul's beauty, a grace native to it and not foreign, for only with these is it truly Soul. And it is right to say that the Soul's becoming a good and beautiful thing is its process of becoming like God, for from the Divine comes all the Beauty and all the Good in beings.

We may even say that Beauty is the <term>Authentic-Existents</term> <note>True Realities or Forms</note> and Ugliness is the Principle contrary to Existence: and the Ugly is also the primary evil; therefore its contrary is at once good and beautiful, or is Goodness and Beauty itself: and hence the same method will reveal to us both the Beauty-Good and the Ugliness-Evil.

And Beauty—this Beauty which is also The Good—must be established as The First; directly derived from this First is the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>, which is pre-eminently the manifestation of Beauty; through the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> the Soul is beautiful. The beauty in things of a lower order—actions and pursuits, for instance—comes by the operation of the shaping Soul, which is also the author of the beauty found in the world of sense. For the Soul, a divine thing and a fragment, as it were, of the Primal Beauty, makes everything it grasps and molds as beautiful as it can possibly be.

Therefore we must ascend again toward the Good, which is desired by every Soul. Anyone who has seen This knows what I mean when I say

...that it is beautiful. Even the desire for it is to be desired as a Good. Attaining it is for those who will take the upward path, who will direct all their forces toward it, and who will strip away everything we have put on during our descent—just as those who approach the Holy Celebrations of the Mysteries are assigned purifications and the laying aside of the garments worn before, entering in nakedness. This continues until, passing everything other than the God on the upward way, each person in their own solitude shall behold that solitary-dwelling Existence: the Apart, the Unmingled, the Pure, upon which all things depend, toward which all look and live and act and know—the Source of Life, of Intellection, and of Being.

And one who knows this vision—with what passion of love shall they not be seized? With what pang of desire, what longing to be melted into one with This, what wondering delight! If someone who has never seen this Being must hunger for It as for all their welfare, then someone who has known It must love and reverence It as Beauty itself. They will be flooded with awe and gladness, struck by a healthy terror; they love with a true love, with sharp desire. All other loves besides this they must despise, and they will disdain all that once seemed fair.

This, indeed, is the mood even of those who, having witnessed the manifestation of Gods or Higher Beings, can never again feel the old delight in the attractiveness of material forms. What, then, are we to think of someone who contemplates Absolute Beauty in Its essential integrity—not an accumulation of flesh and matter, not a dweller on earth or in the heavens (so perfect is Its purity)—far above all such things because they are non-essential, composite, and not primary, but descending from This?

Beholding this Being—the <term>Choragos</term> <note>The leader of the cosmic dance or chorus</note> of all Existence, the Self-Intent that always gives forth and never takes—resting, entranced, in the vision and possession of such a lofty loveliness, growing into Its likeness, what Beauty can the soul still lack? For This—the supreme, absolute, and primal Beauty—fashions Its lovers into Beauty and makes them also worthy of love.

And for This, the sternest and the ultimate combat is set before the Souls; all our labor is for This, lest we be left without a share in this

<header>I. 6. 8] BEAUTY</header>

...the noblest vision: to attain it is to be blessed in that blissful sight; to fail to reach it is to fail utterly.

For it is not the person who has failed to find joy in color or visible forms, nor the one who has failed to gain power, honors, or kingdoms who has failed, but only the one who has failed to reach This alone. To win This, one should renounce kingdoms and rule over earth, ocean, and sky, if only—by spurning the world of the senses from beneath one’s feet and straining toward This—one may truly see.

8.

But what must we do? Where does the path lie? How can we come to a vision of that inaccessible Beauty, which dwells as if in consecrated precincts, apart from the common ways where everyone—even the profane—might see?

Let the person who has the strength arise and withdraw into themselves, letting go of everything known by the eyes, turning away forever from the material beauty that was once their joy. When they perceive those graceful shapes that appear in the body, let them not pursue them; they must recognize these as copies, vestiges, and shadows, and hasten away toward that which they represent. For if anyone follows what is merely a beautiful shape playing over water—is there not a myth telling symbolically of such a fool, how he sank into the depths of the current and was swept away into nothingness? So too, anyone who is held captive by material beauty and will not break free shall be plunged—not in body, but in Soul—down into the dark depths loathed by the <term>Intellective-Being</term>. There, blind even in the Underworld, they shall interact only with shadows, there as here.

“Let us flee, then, to our beloved Fatherland”: this is the soundest counsel. But what is this flight? How are we to reach the open sea? For Odysseus is surely a parable for us when he commands the flight from the sorceries of Circe or Calypso—refusing to linger despite all the pleasure offered to his eyes and all the sensory delights filling his days.

Our Fatherland is that place from which we have come, and there the Father dwells.

What, then, is our course? What is the manner of our flight? This is not a journey for the feet; the feet bring us only from land to land; nor

nor do you need to think of a coach or ship to carry you away; you must set aside all this order of things and refuse to see them. You must close your eyes and call instead upon another vision that is to be awakened within you—a vision which is the birthright of all, though few turn to use it.

### 9.

And this inner vision, how does it operate?

When newly awakened, it is far too feeble to bear the ultimate splendor. Therefore, the Soul must be trained—habituated to noticing, first, all noble pursuits, then the works of beauty produced not by the labor of the arts but by the virtue of people known for their goodness. Lastly, you must search the souls of those who have shaped these beautiful forms.

But how are you to see into a virtuous soul and know its loveliness? Withdraw into yourself and look. And if you do not find yourself beautiful yet, act like the creator of a statue that is to be made beautiful: he cuts away here, he smooths there, he makes this line lighter, this other purer, until a lovely face has emerged upon his work. You should do the same: cut away all that is excessive, straighten all that is crooked, bring light to all that is overcast, labor to make everything one glow of beauty, and never cease chiseling your statue until the godlike splendor of virtue shines out on you from it—until you see perfect goodness firmly established in its stainless shrine.

When you know that you have become this perfect work, when you are self-contained in the purity of your being, with nothing remaining that can shatter that inner unity and nothing from the outside clinging to the authentic person—when you find yourself wholly true to your essential nature, wholly that single veritable Light which is not measured by space, nor narrowed to any limited form, nor again diffused as something without boundaries, but is always immeasurable as something greater than all measure and more than all quantity—when you perceive that you have grown to this, you have now become vision itself. Now summon all your confidence and strike forward one more step—you need a guide no longer—strain your sight, and see.

This is the only eye that sees the mighty Beauty. If the eye that

Once the soul has achieved this pure, unified state, it becomes the very instrument capable of beholding absolute Beauty.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the ascent of the soul toward the First Beauty, emphasizing that the internal eye must be purified to see the ultimate light. <page-num>89</page-num>
<header>I. 7. 1] BEAUTY</header>

If it should happen that the vision is dimmed by vice—impure, or weak, and unable in its cowardly flinching to see the supreme brightness—then it sees nothing, even though another points to what lies plainly in sight before it. To any vision must be brought an eye adapted to what is to be seen, and having some likeness to it. Never did an eye see the sun unless it had first become sun-like, and never can the soul have a vision of the First Beauty unless it is beautiful itself.

Therefore, first let each person become godlike and beautiful if they care to see God and Beauty. So, ascending, the Soul will come first to the Intellectual-Principle and survey all the beautiful Ideas in the Supreme, and it will acknowledge that this is Beauty, and that the Ideas are Beauty. For by their power all other Beauty comes—from the offspring and essence of the Intellectual-Being. What is beyond the Intellectual-Principle we affirm to be the nature of Good, radiating Beauty before it. So that, treating the Intellectual-Cosmos as one, the first is the Beautiful; if we make a distinction there, the Realm of Ideas constitutes the Beauty of the Intellectual Sphere; and <term>The Good</term>, which lies beyond, is at once the Fountain and Principle of Beauty. The <term>Primal Good</term> and the <term>Primal Beauty</term> share the same dwelling place and, thus, Beauty’s seat is always There.

---

### SEVENTH TRACTATE

->ON THE PRIMAL GOOD AND SECONDARY FORMS OF GOOD<-
->(OTHERWISE, ON HAPPINESS)<-

I.

We can scarcely conceive that for any entity the Good can be anything other than the natural Act expressing its life-force, or—in the case of an entity made up of parts—the appropriate, natural, and complete Act expressive of that which is best within it.

For the Soul, then, the Good is its own natural Act.

But the Soul itself is naturally a “Best”; furthermore, if its Act is directed toward the Best, the achievement is not merely the “Soul’s good” but “<term>The Good</term>” without qualification.

<sig>N</sig>

<vocab>Plotinus, Enneads, First Beauty, Intellectual-Principle, Intellectual-Kosmos, Primal Good, Realm of Ideas, Soul, Happiness</vocab>

<summary>Plotinus argues that the soul must become beautiful and godlike to perceive the Primal Beauty, which resides with the Primal Good in the Intellectual Sphere. He begins a new tractate defining the Good as the natural, highest expression of an entity's life-force, specifically for the Soul.</summary>

<keywords>First Beauty, Intellectual-Principle, Primal Good, soul purification, Intellectual-Cosmos, Happiness, virtue, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the Good as the ultimate source and goal of all existence, following the transition into the Seventh Tractate regarding the Primal Good and Happiness.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

Now, given an Existent which—as being itself the best of existences and even transcending existence—directs its Act toward no other, but is the object toward which the Act of all else is directed, it is clear that this must be at once the Good and the means through which all else may participate in Good.

Other entities may possess this Absolute Good in two ways—by becoming like It and by directing the Act of their being toward It.

Now, if all aspiration and every Act whatsoever are directed toward the Good, it follows that the Essential-Good neither needs nor can look outside itself or aspire to anything other than itself: it can only remain unmoved, being, in the constitution of things, the wellspring and first cause of all Act. Whatever in other entities is of the nature of Good cannot be due to any Act of the Essential-Good upon them; on the contrary, it is for them to act toward their source and cause. The Good must, then, be the Good not by any Act, not even by virtue of its Intellection, but by its very rest within Itself.

Existing beyond and above Being, it must be beyond and above the Intellectual-Principle and all Intellection.

For, again, only that can be named the Good to which all is bound while it is bound to none: for only thus is it truly the object of all aspiration. It must be unmoved, while all circles around it, like a circumference around a center from which all the radii proceed. Another example would be the sun, central to the light which streams from it and yet is linked to it, or at least is always around it, irremovably; try all you will to separate the light from the sun, or the sun from its light, the light is forever in the sun.

2.

But what of the Universe outside? How is it aligned toward the Good?

The soulless are aligned by direction toward Soul; the Soul toward the Good itself, through the Intellectual-Principle.

Everything has something of the Good, by virtue of possessing a certain degree of unity and a certain degree of Existence and by participation in Ideal-Form: to the extent of the Unity, Being, and Form which are present, there is a sharing in an image, for the Unity and

<vocab>Existent, Good, Absolute Good, Essential-Good, Intellection, Intellectual-Principle, Being, Universe, Soul, Ideal-Form, Unity</vocab>

<summary>Plotinus describes the Essential Good as an unmoved, self-sufficient source that transcends Being and Intellection, serving as the ultimate object of aspiration for all things. He explains that all entities, from the Soul to soulless objects, participate in the Good by directing themselves toward it and sharing in its unity and form.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, The Good, Essential-Good, Intellectual-Principle, First Cause, Being, Soul, Ideal-Form, Unity, Metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of how various levels of existence relate to the Good, transitioning from the nature of the Absolute Good to the specific ways Soul and life participate in it, and addressing the philosophical problem of whether death is an evil.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>I. 7. 3] GOOD: PRIMAL AND SECONDARY</header>

Existences that participate [in the Good] are nothing more than images of the Ideal-Form.

With Soul it is different; the First-Soul, which follows after the Intellectual-Principle, possesses a life closer to the Truth and, through that Principle, is good by nature. It will truly possess the Good if it orients itself toward the Intellectual-Principle, since that Principle follows immediately after the Good.

In sum, then, life is the Good to the living, and the Intellectual-Principle is the Good to that which thinks; so that where there is life combined with thought, there is a double contact with the Good.

3.
But if life is a good, is there a good for everything that lives?

No: in the wicked, life limps; it is like an eye to the dim-sighted; it fails in its task.

But if the mixed nature of life is a good to us—even though it is entwined with evil—must death not be an evil?

Evil to what? There must be a subject to experience the evil; but if the potential subject is no longer among existing things, or is still among them but is devoid of life... well, a stone is not more immune.

If, on the contrary, life and soul continue after death, then death will be no evil but a good; the Soul, disembodied, is freer to exercise its own Activity.

If it is taken into the All-Soul—what evil can reach it there? And just as the Gods are—possessing the Good and untouched by evil—so, certainly, is the Soul that has preserved its essential character. And if it should lose its purity, the evil it experiences is not in its death but in its life. Suppose it is being punished in the underworld; even there, the evil thing is its life and not its death; the misfortune is still life, though a life of a specific character.

Life is a partnership of a Soul and body; death is the dissolution of that partnership. In either life or death, then, the Soul will feel at home.

But, again, if life is good, how can death be anything but evil?

Remember that the good of life, where it has any good at all,

<summary>Plotinus argues that while life is a good for the Soul when oriented toward the Intellectual-Principle, death is not an evil because it either leads to non-existence (which cannot experience evil) or to a freer state for the Soul.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Soul, Intellectual-Principle, The Good, Life and Death, All-Soul, Ethics, Metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion on death as a potential good for the Soul and introduces a new tractate investigating the nature and origins of Evil.</meta>

is not due to anything in the partnership but to the repelling of evil by virtue; death, then, must be the greater good.

In a word, life in the body is in itself an evil, but the Soul enters its Good through Virtue, not by living the life of the Couplement <note>The "Couplement" refers to the union or joint-entity of the body and the soul.</note> but by holding itself apart, even here.

### EIGHTH TRACTATE

#### ON THE NATURE AND SOURCE OF EVIL

I.

Those inquiring into how Evil enters into beings—or rather, into a certain order of beings—would make the best start if they first established exactly what Evil is and what constitutes its nature. We would immediately know where it comes from, where its natural home is, and where it is present merely as an accident; and there would be no further question as to whether it possesses Authentic Existence.

But a difficulty arises: by what faculty in us could we possibly know Evil?

All knowledge comes through likeness. The Intellectual-Principle and the Soul, being Ideal Forms, would know Ideal Forms and have a natural tendency toward them; but who could imagine Evil to be an Ideal Form, seeing that it manifests itself as the very absence of Good?

If the solution is that a single act of knowledge covers contraries, and that since Evil is the contrary of Good, the same act would grasp both Good and Evil together, then to know Evil there must first be a clear perception and understanding of Good. This is because nobler existences precede baser ones and are Ideal Forms, while the less good have no such status and are nearer to Non-Being.

No doubt there is a question about the precise way in which Good is contrary to Evil—whether it is as a First Principle is to the last of things, or as an Ideal Form is to an utter lack (to Non-entity); but we postpone this subject for now.

<summary>Plotinus concludes that virtue allows the soul to remain separate from the body's evils and begins an investigation into the nature of Evil. He questions how Evil can be understood since knowledge usually requires likeness, suggesting it must be understood as the contrary or absence of the Good.</summary>
<keywords>Evil, Virtue, Soul, Good, Couplement, Intellectual-Principle, Ideal-Forms, Authentic-Existence, Non-Being, First-Principle, Non-entity</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of Evil by first establishing a definition of the Good as the ultimate source and goal of all existence.</meta>

# I. 8. 1] EVIL

2.
For the moment, let us define the nature of the Good as far as our immediate purpose demands.

The Good is that on which all else depends, toward which all existences aspire as to their source and their need, while the Good itself is without need, sufficient to itself, aspiring to no other, the measure and end of all, giving out from itself the Intellectual-Principle, Existence, Soul, Life, and all intellectual activity.

Everything until the Good is reached is beautiful; the Good is beyond-beautiful, beyond the highest, holding kingly state in the Intellectual Cosmos, that sphere constituted by a principle wholly unlike what is known as intelligence in us. Our intelligence is nourished on the propositions of logic, is skilled in following discussions, works by reasoning, examines links of demonstration, and comes to know the world of Being also by the steps of logical process, having no prior grasp of Reality but remaining empty—even though it is all intelligence—until it has put itself to school.

The Intellectual-Principle we are discussing is not of such a kind: It possesses all; It is all; It is present to all by its self-presence; It has all by other means than "having," for what it possesses is still itself, nor does any particular thing within it stand apart. For every such particular is the whole and is in all respects all, while yet not confused in the mass but still distinct—apart only to the extent that any participant in the Intellectual-Principle participates not in the entire whole as one thing, but in whatever lies within its own reach.

And the First Act is the Act of the Good stationary within itself, and the First Existence is the self-contained existence of the Good; but there is also an Act upon it, that of the Intellectual-Principle which, as it were, lives around it.

And the Soul, outside, circles around the Intellectual-Principle, and by gazing upon it and seeing into the depths of it, through it sees God.

Such is the untroubled, the blissful life of divine beings, and Evil has no place in it; if this were all, there would be no Evil but Good only: the first, the second, and the third Good. All, thus far, is "with the King."

<summary>This page defines the Good as the self-sufficient source of all things and describes the Intellectual-Principle as an all-encompassing reality that surrounds the Good. It concludes that Evil cannot exist within this divine hierarchy of the Good, the Intellectual-Principle, and the Soul.</summary>
<keywords>Good, Intellectual-Principle, Soul, Intelligence, Intellectual Cosmos, Being, Reality, Evil, Act, Existence, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the Good as the source of all things, transitioning into an investigation of the origins and nature of Evil.</meta>

...of All, the unfailing Cause of Good and Beauty and the controller of all; and what is Good in the second degree depends upon the Second Principle, and tertiary Good depends upon the Third.

3.
If such is the nature of Beings and of That which transcends the whole realm of Being, Evil cannot have a place among Beings or in the Beyond-Being; these are good.

It only remains, if Evil exists at all, that it is situated in the realm of <term>Non-Being</term>, that it is some mode, as it were, of <term>Non-Being</term>, and that it has its seat in something in touch with <term>Non-Being</term> or communicates to a certain degree with <term>Non-Being</term>.

By this <term>Non-Being</term>, of course, we should not understand something that simply does not exist, but only something of an utterly different order from <term>Authentic-Being</term>. There is no question here of movement or position regarding Being; the <term>Non-Being</term> we are thinking of is, rather, an image of Being, or perhaps something even further removed than an image.

Now this (the required faint image of Being) might be the sensible universe with all the impressions it produces, or it might be something of even later derivation, accidental to the realm of sense; or again, it might be the source of the sense-world or something of the same order entering into it to complete it.

Some conception of it would be reached by thinking of measurelessness as opposed to measure, of the unbounded against the bound, the unshaped against a principle of shape, the ever-needy against the self-sufficient. Think of the ever-undefined, the never at rest, the all-accepting but never satisfied, utter dearth; and make all this character not a mere accident in it but its equivalent for essential being, so that, whatever fragment of it is taken, that part is all lawless void, while whatever participates in it and resembles it becomes evil—though not, of course, to the point of being <term>Evil-Absolute</term> as it is itself.

In what substantial form (<term>hypostasis</term>), then, is all this to be found—not as an accident, but as the very substance itself?

<vocab>Plotinus, Non-Being, Authentic-Being, Evil-Absolute, hypostasis, Neoplatonism, Enneads</vocab>

<summary>Plotinus argues that since the higher realms of Being are inherently good, Evil must reside in the realm of 'Non-Being,' which is characterized as a measureless, formless, and ever-deficient image of reality. He defines Evil not as total non-existence, but as a lack of limit and order that exists as a substance in itself.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Non-Being, Authentic-Being, Evil-Absolute, hypostasis, Neoplatonism, Enneads, metaphysics, nature of evil, the Good</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of Evil, moving from the definition of Non-Being to the logical necessity of an "Absolute Evil" that exists prior to specific evil qualities in objects.</meta>

# I. 8. 4] EVIL

For if Evil can enter into other things, it must have, in a certain sense, a prior existence, even though it may not be an essence. Just as there is Good, the Absolute, as well as Good as a quality, so, together with the derived evil entering into something not itself, there must be the Absolute Evil.

But how? Can there be Unmeasure apart from an unmeasured object? Does not Measure exist apart from unmeasured things? Precisely as there is Measure apart from anything measured, so there is Unmeasure apart from the unmeasured. If Unmeasure could not exist independently, it must exist either in an unmeasured object or in something measured; but the unmeasured could not need Unmeasure and the measured could not contain it.

There must, then, be some <term>Undetermination-Absolute</term>, some Absolute Formlessness; all the qualities cited as characterizing the Nature of Evil must be summed under an Absolute Evil; and every evil thing outside of this must either contain this Absolute by saturation or have taken on the character of evil and become a cause of evil by dedication to this Absolute.

What will this be?

It is that Kind whose place is below all the patterns, forms, shapes, measurements, and limits—that which has no trace of good by any title of its own, but (at best) takes order and grace from some Principle outside itself. It is a mere image as regards <term>Absolute-Being</term> but the Authentic Essence of Evil—insofar as Evil can have Authentic Being. In such a Kind, Reason recognizes the <term>Primal Evil</term>, Evil Absolute.

4.

The bodily Kind, in that it partakes of <term>Matter</term>, is an evil thing. What form is in bodies is an untrue form: they are without life; by their own natural disorderly movement they destroy each other; they are hindrances to the soul in its proper Act; in their ceaseless flux they are always slipping away from Being.

Soul, on the contrary, since not every Soul is evil, is not an evil Kind.

<summary>This page argues that Absolute Evil must exist as a formless, measureless principle prior to any specific evil things, identifying it with the "Kind" that lies below all forms and order. It characterizes the physical world as evil because it partakes in Matter, which is disorderly and lacks true life, while noting that the Soul is not inherently evil.</summary>
<keywords>Absolute Evil, Unmeasure, Primal Evil, Matter, Formlessness, Soul, Bodily Kind, Being, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of Evil, moving from the definition of Matter as the Primal Evil to how this evil affects the human Soul.</meta>

->PLOTINUS<-

What, then, is the evil Soul?

It is, we read, the Soul that has entered into the service of that in which soul-evil is implanted by nature, in whose service the unreasoning phase of the Soul accepts evil—unmeasure, excess, and shortcoming, which bring forth licentiousness, cowardice, and all other flaws of the Soul. These are all states foreign to its true nature, which set up false judgments, so that the Soul comes to name things good or evil not by their true value but by the mere test of liking and disliking.

But what is the root of this evil state? How can it be brought under the causing principle indicated?

Firstly, such a Soul is not apart from Matter; it is not purely itself. That is to say, it is touched with Unmeasure; it is shut out from the Forming-Idea that orders and brings to measure, and this is because it is merged into a body made of Matter.

Then if the Reasoning-Faculty too has taken damage, the Soul’s seeing is hindered by the passions and by the darkening that Matter brings to it, by its decline into Matter, by its very attention no longer to Essence but to Process—whose principle or source is, again, Matter, the Kind so evil as to saturate with its own wickedness even that which is not in it but merely looks toward it.

For, wholly without part in Good, the negation of Good, unmingled Lack, this Matter-Kind makes over to its own likeness whatsoever comes in touch with it.

The Soul wrought to perfection, directed toward the Intellectual-Principle, is steadfastly pure: it has turned away from Matter; all that is undetermined, that is outside of measure, that is evil, it neither sees nor draws near; it endures in its purity, only, and wholly, determined by the Intellectual-Principle.

The Soul that breaks away from this source of its reality toward the non-perfect and non-primal is, as it were, a secondary thing, an image of the loyal Soul. By its falling away—and to the extent of the fall—it is stripped of Determination, becomes wholly indeterminate, and sees darkness. Looking to what repels vision, as we look when we are said to see darkness, it has taken Matter into itself.

<vocab>evil Soul, Matter, Unmeasure, Forming-Idea, Reasoning-Faculty, Essence, Process, Intellectual-Principle, Determination</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the evil Soul as one that has become subservient to Matter, leading to a loss of measure, false judgments, and a clouded reasoning faculty. It contrasts this fallen state with the perfected Soul, which remains pure by staying directed toward the Intellectual-Principle and away from the indeterminacy of Matter.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of Evil, specifically responding to the objection that if the Soul's evil is a "lack," then the source of evil must be the Soul itself rather than Matter. It clarifies that Evil is an absolute lack found only in Matter, which then affects the Soul and the body.</meta>

<page-num>97</page-num>
<header>EVIL</header>
<meta>I. 8. 5]</meta>

5.
But, it will be objected, if this seeing and frequenting of the darkness is due to the lack of good, the Soul’s evil has its source in that very lack; the darkness will be merely a secondary cause—and at once the Principle of Evil is removed from Matter and is made to exist before Matter.

No: Evil is not found in just any and every lack; it is found in absolute lack. What falls in some degree short of the Good is not Evil; considered in its own kind, it might even be perfect. But where there is a total absence of good, there we have Essential Evil, void of any share in the Good; this is the case with Matter.

Matter does not even have the kind of existence through which it could have some part in the Good. "Being" is attributed to it only by a quirk of language; the truth is that it has Non-Being.

A simple lack brings about merely "Not-Goodness." Evil requires an absolute lack—though, of course, any very significant shortcoming makes an ultimate fall possible and is already, in itself, an evil.

In short, we should not think of Evil as some particular bad thing—injustice, for example, or any other ugly trait—but as a principle distinct from any of the specific forms in which it becomes visible through the addition of certain elements. Thus, there may be wickedness in the Soul; the forms this general wickedness takes will be determined by the surrounding Matter, by the faculties of the Soul that are operating, and by the nature of their operation—whether they are seeing, acting, or merely receiving an impression.

But if we suppose that things external to the Soul are to be counted as Evil—sickness, poverty, and so forth—how can they be traced back to the principle we have described?

Well, sickness is an excess or a deficiency in the body, which, as a material organism, rebels against order and measure; ugliness is simply matter that has not been mastered by *Ideal-Form*; poverty consists in our need and lack of goods made necessary to us by our association with Matter, whose very nature is to be one long state of want.

If all this is true, we cannot be the source of Evil ourselves; we are not evil in our own nature. Evil existed before we came to be; the Evil which
