holds men down and binds them against their will; and for those who have the strength—which is not found in all men, it is true—there is a deliverance from the evils that have found lodgment in the soul.
      In a word, since Matter belongs only to the sensible world, vice in men is not the Absolute Evil; not all men are vicious; some overcome vice, and some, the better sort, are never attacked by it; and those who master it win by means of that part of them which is not material.

->6.<-

      If this is so, how do we explain the teaching that evils can never pass away but “exist of necessity,” and that “while evil has no place in the divine order, it haunts mortal nature and this place forever”?
      Does this mean that heaven is clear of evil, ever moving in its orderly way, spinning on its appointed path, with no injustice there or any flaw—no wrong done by any power to any other, but all remaining true to the settled plan—while injustice and disorder prevail on earth, designated as “the Mortal Kind and this Place”?
      Not quite so: for the command to “flee from here” does not refer to earth and earthly life. The flight we read of consists not in quitting earth but in living our earthly life “with justice and piety in the light of philosophy”; it is vice we are to flee, so that clearly, to the writer, Evil is simply vice along with the sequels of vice. And when the disputant in that dialogue says that, if men could be convinced of the doctrine advanced, there would be an end of Evil, he is answered, “That can never be: Evil is of necessity, for there must be a contrary to good.”
      Still, we may reasonably ask: how can vice in man be a contrary to The Good in the Supernal realm? For vice is the contrary to virtue, and virtue is not The Good itself, but merely the good thing by which Matter is brought to order.
      How can there be any contrary to the Absolute Good, when the absolute has no quality?
      Besides, is there any universal necessity that the existence of one of two contraries should entail the existence of the other? Admit that the existence of one is often accompanied by the existence of the other—

<summary>Plotinus distinguishes between human vice and Absolute Evil, arguing that while some men are overcome by vice due to Matter, others find deliverance through their non-material nature. He further explores the philosophical necessity of evil as a contrary to good, questioning how the quality-less Absolute Good can have a direct opposite.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Matter, Absolute Evil, vice, virtue, The Good, Supernal, philosophy, necessity, contraries</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of Evil as a contrary to The Good, specifically addressing how universal principles can have opposites even if individual essences often do not.</meta>

<page-num>99</page-num>
<header>I. 8. 6] EVIL</header>

—sickness and health, for example—yet there is no universal compulsion.

Perhaps, however, our author did not mean that this was universally true; he is speaking only of The Good.

But then, if The Good is an essence—and even more so if It is that which transcends all existence—how can It have any contrary?

That there is nothing contrary to essence is certain in the case of particular existences—established by practical proof—but not in the quite different case of the Universal.

But what would be the nature of this contrary—the contrary to universal existence and to the Primals in general?

To essential existence would be opposed non-existence; to the nature of Good, some principle and source of evil. Both these will be sources, one of what is good, the other of what is evil. Everything within the domain of the one principle is opposed, as a contrary, to the entire domain of the other, and this in a contrariety more violent than any existing between secondary things.

For these secondary things are opposed as members of one species or of one genus, and, within that common ground, they participate in some common quality.

In the case of the Primals or Universals, there is such complete separation that what is the exact negation of one group constitutes the very nature of the other; we have diametric contrariety if by contrariety we mean the extreme of remoteness.

Now, to the content of the divine order—its fixed quality, its measuredness, and so forth—there is opposed the content of the evil principle: its unfixedness, measurelessness, and so forth. Total is opposed to total. The existence of the one genus is a falsity—primarily and essentially a falsehood. The other genus possesses Essence-Authentic. The opposition is of truth to a lie; essence is opposed to essence.

Thus we see that it is not universally true that an essence can have no contrary.

In the case of fire and water, we would admit contrariety if it were not for their common element, Matter, about which are gathered the

<summary>Plotinus argues that while specific essences may lack contraries, universal principles like The Good are diametrically opposed by a source of Evil defined by measurelessness and falsehood. This cosmic opposition represents the extreme of remoteness between the divine order and the material principle.</summary>

<header>PLOTINUS</header>

warmth and dryness of one and the dampness and cold of the other: if only what constitutes their distinct kinds were present, and the common ground were absent, there would also be essence contrary to essence here.

In summary, things that are utterly separate, having nothing in common and standing at the remotest poles, are opposites by nature. Their contrariety does not depend on quality or the existence of a distinct genus of beings, but on the extreme difference—a clash in content and a clash in effect.

### 7.

But why does the existence of the Principle of Good necessarily involve the existence of a Principle of Evil? Is it because the Universe necessarily involves the existence of Matter? Yes: for this Universe is necessarily made up of contraries; it could not exist if Matter did not. The nature of this Cosmos is, therefore, a blend; it is blended from the Intellectual-Principle and Necessity. What enters it from God is good; evil comes from the "Ancient Kind" which, we read, is the underlying Matter not yet brought into order by the Ideal-Form.

But, since the expression "this place" must be taken to mean the Universe, how do we explain the words "mortal nature"?

The answer is in the passage (in which the Father of Gods addresses the Divinities of the lower sphere): 
> "Since you possess only a derivative being, you are not immortals . . . but by my power you shall escape dissolution."

We read that the escape is not a matter of location, but of acquiring virtue and disengaging the self from the body; this is the escape from Matter. Plato explains somewhere how a man frees himself and how he remains bound; and the phrase "to live among the gods" means to live among the Intelligible-Existents, for these are the Immortals.

There is another consideration establishing the necessary existence of Evil.

Given that The Good is not the only thing that exists, it is inevitable that, by the outgoing from it or, if the phrase be preferred, the con-

<header>I. 8. 8] EVIL 101</header>

...continuous descent or departure from it, a Last should be produced—something after which nothing more can be produced: this will be Evil.

Just as necessarily as there is something after the First, there is necessarily a Last: this Last is Matter, the thing that has no residue of good in it; here lies the necessity of Evil.

### 8.

But some will still deny that it is through this Matter that we ourselves become evil.

They will say that neither ignorance nor wicked desires arise in Matter. Even if they admit that the unhappy condition within us is due to the depravity inherent in the body, they will argue that the blame still lies not in the Matter itself, but with the Form present in it—such Form as heat, cold, bitterness, saltiness, and all other conditions perceptible to the senses, or again such states as being full or empty—not in the concrete sense, but in the presence or absence of these very forms. In short, they will argue, all particularity in desires and even in perverted judgments about things can be attributed to such causes, so that Evil lies in this Form much more than in mere Matter.

Yet, even with all this, they can be compelled to admit that Matter is the Evil.

For the quality (form) that has entered into Matter does not act as an entity separate from the Matter, any more than the shape of an axe will cut apart from iron. Furthermore, Forms lodged in Matter are not the same as they would be if they remained within themselves; they are Materialized Reason-Principles, corrupted in the Matter, having absorbed its nature. Essential fire does not burn, nor do any of the essential entities, by themselves alone, perform the operation which is attributed to their action once they have entered into Matter.

Matter becomes the mistress of what is manifested through it: it corrupts and destroys the newcomer, substituting its own opposite character and kind—not in the sense of opposing, for example, concrete cold to concrete warmth, but by setting its own formlessness against the

He refutes the idea that evil stems from physical qualities (like heat or cold) by explaining that these qualities only act through and are corrupted by Matter.</summary>

The form of heat, the transition from shapelessness to shape, and the shift from excess and deficiency to what is duly ordered—all these follow this principle. Thus, in short, whatever enters into Matter ceases to belong to itself and comes to belong to Matter. Just as in the nourishment of living beings, what is consumed does not remain as it first arrived, but is turned into, for example, dog's blood and everything else that constitutes a dog; it becomes, in fact, whatever the humors of the recipient require.

Therefore, if the body is the cause of Evil, there is no escape; the ultimate cause of Evil is Matter.

Still, it will be urged that the incoming Idea should have been able to conquer the Matter.

The difficulty is that the master of Matter cannot remain pure itself except by avoiding Matter altogether.

Besides, one's physical constitution determines both the nature of desires and their intensity, so there are bodies in which the incoming Idea cannot hold sway. There is a vicious constitution that chills and clogs activity and inhibits choice; a contrary bodily habit produces frivolity and a lack of balance. The same fact is indicated by our successive variations of mood: in times of stress, we are not the same—either in our desires or our ideas—as when we are at peace, and we differ again with every different object that brings us satisfaction.

To resume: the Measureless is evil primarily; whatever exists in a state of lack of measure, whether by resemblance or participation, is evil secondarily, by virtue of its relationship with the Primal—primarily, the darkness; secondarily, that which is darkened. Now, Vice, being an ignorance and a lack of measure in the Soul, is secondarily evil, not the Essential Evil, just as Virtue is not the Primal Good but is a Likeness to The Good, or a participation in it.

### 9.

But what approach do we have to the knowing of Good and Evil?

And first, concerning the Evil of the soul: we may know Virtue by the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> and by means of the philosophic habit; but what of Vice?

Just as a ruler marks off the straight from the crooked, so Vice is known by its divergence from the line of Virtue.

<vocab>Plotinus, Matter, Evil, Idea, Soul, Vice, Virtue, Primal Good, Intellectual-Principle, Measureless</vocab>

<summary>Plotinus explains that Matter corrupts the Forms that enter it and that the soul's purity depends on its distance from Matter. He defines primary Evil as the "Measureless" (Matter) and secondary Evil as things that participate in it, such as Vice.</summary>
<keywords>Matter, Evil, Soul, Virtue, Vice, Intellectual-Principle, Measureless, Form, physical constitution, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of Evil and Matter, specifically how the Soul or the Intellectual-Principle can perceive something that lacks all Form and Measure.</meta>

<page-num>103</page-num>
<header>I. 8. 10] EVIL</header>

But are we able to confirm Vice by any vision we can have of it, or is there some other way of knowing it?

Total vice, certainly, cannot be known by any vision, for it is utterly outside of boundary and measure; this thing, which is nowhere, can be grasped only by abstraction. However, any degree of evil falling short of The Absolute is knowable by the extent of that falling short.

We see partial wrong; from what is before us, we divine that which is lacking to the entire form (or Kind) thus indicated. We see that the completed Kind would be the Indeterminate; by this process, we are able to identify and affirm Evil. In the same way, when we observe what we feel to be an ugly appearance in Matter—left there because the <term>Reason-Principle</term> <note>The organizing principle or "Logos" that gives form to matter.</note> has not become so completely the master as to cover over the ugliness—we recognize Ugliness by its falling-short from the Ideal-Form.

But how can we identify what has never had any touch of Form?

We must utterly eliminate every kind of Form; and the object in which there is none whatever, we call Matter. If we are to see Matter, we must so completely abolish Form that we take shapelessness into our very selves.

In fact, it is another <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> <note>The "Nous" or divine mind; here referring to a specialized or "spurious" state of the mind when it attempts to perceive the void.</note>, not the true one, which ventures a vision so incompatible.

To see darkness, the eye withdraws from the light; it is striving to cease from seeing, and therefore it abandons the light which would make the darkness invisible. Away from the light, its power is more a matter of not-seeing than of seeing, and this not-seeing is its nearest approach to seeing Darkness. In the same way, the Intellectual-Principle, in order to see its contrary (Matter), must leave its own light locked up within itself and, as it were, go forth from itself into an outside realm; it must ignore its natural brightness and submit itself to the very contradiction of its being.

10.

But if Matter is devoid of quality, how can it be evil?

It is described as being devoid of quality in the sense only that it does not essentially possess any of the qualities which it admits and

<vocab>Vice, Matter, Reason-Principle, Intellectual-Principle, Evil, Form, Indeterminate, Ideal-Form</vocab>

<summary>This text explores how the mind perceives Evil and Matter through a process of abstraction and "not-seeing," comparing the perception of formless Matter to the eye attempting to see darkness by withdrawing from light. It concludes that while Matter lacks inherent qualities, it acts as a recipient for them, leading into a discussion on its status as the root of evil.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, metaphysics, nature of evil, Matter, Intellectual-Principle, Reason-Principle, Ideal-Form, perception of darkness, abstraction, shapelessness</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the nature of Evil, specifically examining whether Matter can be considered evil despite lacking any specific qualities or forms, and how this relates to the nature of the Soul.</meta>

<page-num>104</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

...which enter into it as if into a <term>substratum</term> <note>The underlying foundation or base.</note>. No one claims that it lacks a nature; and if it has any nature at all, why can that nature not be evil, even if it is not a <term>Quality</term>?

<term>Quality</term> defines something other than itself; it is therefore an <term>accidental</term> <note>An incidental or non-essential property.</note> property; it resides within some other object. Matter does not exist within another object but is the <term>substratum</term> in which the <term>accidental</term> resides. Matter, then, is said to be devoid of <term>Quality</term> because it does not inherently possess this thing which is, by nature, an <term>accidental</term>. Furthermore, if <term>Quality</term> itself lacks <term>Quality</term>, how can Matter—which is the unqualified—be said to possess it?

Thus, it is perfectly correct to say both that Matter lacks <term>Quality</term> and that it is evil. It is Evil not in the sense of possessing a <term>Quality</term>, but precisely in its lack of one. If you gave it <term>Quality</term>, even in its very Evil, it would almost become a <term>Form</term>; whereas in truth, it is a <term>Kind</term> <note>A category or genus.</note> contrary to <term>Form</term>.

> “But,” it may be said, “the <term>Kind</term> opposed to all <term>Form</term> is <term>Privation</term> or <term>Negation</term>, and this necessarily refers to something other than itself; it is no <term>Substantial-Existence</term>. Therefore, if Evil is <term>Privation</term> or <term>Negation</term>, it must be contained within some <term>Negation</term> of <term>Form</term>; there would be no such thing as self-existent Evil.”

This objection may be answered by applying the principle to the case of Evil in the <term>Soul</term>. The Evil, the Vice, would be a <term>Negation</term> and not anything having a separate existence. We then arrive at the doctrine which either denies Matter or, while admitting it, denies its Evil. We would not need to look elsewhere; we could at once place Evil in the <term>Soul</term>, recognizing it as the mere absence of Good. But if the negation is the denial of something that ought to be present—if it is a denial of <term>The Good</term> by the <term>Soul</term>—then the <term>Soul</term> would produce vice within itself through the operation of its own nature. It would then be devoid of good and, therefore, though it is a <term>Soul</term>, it would be devoid of life. If the <term>Soul</term> has no life, it is soulless; the <term>Soul</term> would not be a <term>Soul</term> at all.

No; the <term>Soul</term> possesses life by its own nature and therefore does not, by its own nature, contain this negation of <term>The Good</term>. It has much good within it; it carries a joyful trace of the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> and is not essentially evil. It is neither primally evil, nor is that Primal Evil present within it even as an <term>accidental</term> property, for the <term>Soul</term> is not entirely separated from the <term>Good</term>.

<vocab>Plotinus, Matter, Quality, Evil, Form, Soul, Privation, Negation, Substantial-Existence, Intellectual-Principle, The Good</vocab>

<summary>Plotinus argues that Matter is essentially evil not because it possesses an evil quality, but because it lacks quality and form entirely. He defends the idea of self-existent Evil by arguing that Evil cannot be a mere negation within the Soul, as the Soul is inherently linked to life and the Good.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Matter, Quality, Evil, Form, Soul, Privation, Negation, Substantial-Existence, Intellectual-Principle, The Good, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of Evil, moving from the definition of Matter as a privation of form to exploring how evil manifests within the soul as a departure from the Absolute Good.</meta>

<page-num>105</page-num>
<header>I. 8. 10] EVIL</header>
<sig>P</sig>

Perhaps vice and evil within the soul should be described not as a complete, but as a partial, negation of good.

But if this were so, part of the soul must possess the Good, while part lacks it; the soul would then have a mixed nature and the evil within it would not be pure. We would not yet have discovered the primal, unmixed Evil. In this case, the soul would possess the Good as its essence, and evil as an <term>accidental</term> <note>A non-essential property or quality that does not belong to the core nature of a thing.</note> quality.

Perhaps evil is merely an obstacle to the soul, like something affecting the eye and thus hindering sight.

But such an ailment in the eyes is no more than a cause of evil; Absolute Evil is something quite different. If, then, vice is an obstacle to the soul, vice is a cause of evil but not Absolute Evil. Virtue is not the Absolute Good, but a cooperator with it; and if virtue is not the Absolute Good, neither is vice the Absolute Evil. Virtue is not the Absolute Beauty or the Absolute Good; therefore, vice is not essential ugliness or Essential Evil.

We teach that virtue is not the Absolute Good and Beauty because we know that these exist prior to virtue and transcend it, and that virtue is good and beautiful only through participating in them. Now, just as we reach the Beautiful and the Good by ascending from virtue, we reach Essential Evil by descending from vice. Using vice as a starting point, we come to a vision of evil—as far as such a vision is possible—and we become evil to the extent that we participate in it. We have become dwellers in the <term>Place of Unlikeness</term> <note>A philosophical concept referring to the material realm where the soul loses its resemblance to the Divine.</note>, where, having fallen from all our resemblance to the Divine, we lie in gloom and mud. For if the soul abandons itself completely to the extreme of vice, it is no longer merely a vicious soul—since mere vice is still human and still carries some trace of good—but it has taken on another nature, that of Evil; and as far as a soul can die, it is dead. The death of the soul is twofold: while still trapped in the body, it lies down in <term>Matter</term> and drenches itself in it; and when it has left the body, it lies in the other world until, somehow, it stirs again and lifts its gaze from the mud. This is what is meant by our "going down to <term>Hades</term> and slumbering there."

<vocab>Vice, Evil, Soul, Virtue, Absolute Good, Absolute Beauty, Essential Evil, Place of Unlikeness, Hades, Matter, Plotinus</vocab>

<summary>This page examines whether vice in the soul is a partial absence of good or an entry into a state of essential evil. Plotinus argues that extreme vice leads to a "death of the soul," where it becomes submerged in matter and loses its divine resemblance, a state compared to slumbering in Hades.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Enneads, nature of evil, soul, virtue, vice, Absolute Good, Matter, Hades, Place of Unlikeness, spiritual death</keywords>

<header>PLOTINUS</header>

## II.

It may be suggested that Vice is feebleness in the Soul.

We shall be reminded that the vicious Soul is unstable, swept along from every ill to every other, quickly stirred by appetites, headlong to anger, as hasty to reach compromises, yielding at once to obscure imaginations, as weak, in fact, as the weakest thing made by man or nature, blown about by every breeze, burned away by every heat.

Still, the question must be faced: what constitutes this weakness in the Soul, and from where it comes.

For weakness in the body is not like that in the Soul: the word weakness, which covers the incapacity for work and the lack of resistance in the body, is applied to the Soul merely by analogy—unless, indeed, in the one case as in the other, the cause of the weakness is Matter.

But we must go more thoroughly into the source of this weakness, as we call it, in the Soul, which is certainly not made weak as the result of any density or rarity, or by any thickening or thinning or anything like a disease, like a fever.

Now, this weakness must be seated either in Souls utterly disengaged or in Souls bound to Matter, or in both.

It cannot exist in those apart from Matter, for all these are pure and, as we read, winged and perfect and unimpeded in their task: there remains only that the weakness be in the fallen Souls, neither cleansed nor clean; and in them the weakness will be, not in any privation but in some hostile presence, like that of phlegm or bile in the organs of the body.

If we form an acute and accurate notion of the cause of the fall, we shall understand the weakness that comes by it.

Matter exists; Soul exists; and they occupy, so to speak, one place. There is not one place for Matter and another for Soul—Matter, for instance, kept to earth, and Soul in the air: the soul’s “separate place” is simply its not being in Matter; that is, its not being united with it; that is, that there be no compound unit consisting of Soul and Matter; that is, that Soul be not molded in Matter as in a matrix; this is the Soul’s apartness.

<vocab>Vice, Soul, Matter, feebleness, weakness, fallen Souls, separate place, matrix</vocab>

<summary>Plotinus investigates whether vice is a form of "weakness" in the soul, concluding that such feebleness only occurs in "fallen" souls that are bound to matter rather than those that remain pure and detached. He explains that the soul's "apartness" from matter is not a physical distance, but a state of not being united or molded into a compound with material substance.</summary>

<keywords>Plotinus, Soul, Matter, Vice, weakness, fallen souls, dualism, Neoplatonism, philosophy of evil</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of how Matter affects the Soul, establishing Matter as the primary source of the Soul's fall and the origin of evil. It follows the previous discussion on whether the soul's "weakness" is inherent or caused by external factors.</meta>

<page-num>107</page-num>
# I. 8. 12] EVIL 107

But the faculties of the Soul are numerous; it has its beginning, its middle stages, and its outer edges. Matter appears and makes demands; it creates disorder and seeks to force its way inside. However, all that ground is holy, and nothing there is without a share in the Soul. Therefore, Matter submits and receives light; but it cannot reach the source of its illumination, because the Soul cannot lift up this foreign thing that is so close, since its inherent evil makes it invisible. On the contrary, the illumination—the light streaming from the Soul—is dulled and weakened as it mixes with Matter. This Matter offers the Soul the possibility of birth, providing the means by which it enters into generation <note>the process of becoming or physical existence</note>, which would be impossible for the Soul if no recipient were available.

This entry into Matter is the fall of the Soul; this is the source of its weakness. Not all the faculties of its being can act freely, because Matter hinders their expression. It encroaches upon the Soul’s territory and, in a sense, crushes the Soul back. It turns everything it has stolen into evil until the Soul finds the strength to move forward again.

Thus, the cause of both the Soul's weakness and all its evil is Matter.

The evil of Matter comes before the weakness and the vice; it is Primal Evil. Even if the Soul itself submits to Matter and brings it into being, and even if the Soul becomes evil within itself through its dealings with Matter, the cause is still the presence of Matter. The Soul would never have approached Matter if the presence of Matter were not the occasion for its life on earth.

12.

If the existence of Matter is denied, the necessity of this Principle must be proven from the treatises "On Matter," where the subject is discussed at length.

To deny Evil a place among realities is necessarily to do away with the Good as well, and even to deny the existence of anything desirable. It is to deny desire, avoidance, and every intellectual act. This is because desire has the Good as its object, while aversion looks toward Evil; every intellectual act and all Wisdom deals with Good and Bad, and Wisdom itself is one of the things that are good.

There must, then, be The Good—unmixed good—and the mixture of Good and Bad, and that which is more Bad than Good, with this last category ending with the...

<vocab>Soul, Matter, Primal Evil, earth-life, The Good, Principle, Wisdom</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that Matter is the primary cause of the Soul's fall and weakness, acting as a "Primal Evil" that hinders the Soul's faculties. Plotinus also asserts that denying the existence of Evil would logically require denying the existence of the Good and the nature of Wisdom.</summary>

<keywords>Soul, Matter, Primal Evil, dualism, Plotinus, The Good, generation, fall of the soul, ethics, metaphysics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of how Matter affects the Soul, establishing Matter as the primary source of the Soul's fall and the origin of evil. It follows the previous discussion on whether the soul's "weakness" is inherent or caused by external factors.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

We have been seeking that which is Utterly Bad, just as that in which Evil constitutes the lesser part tends, by that lessening, towards the Good.

What, then, must Evil be to the Soul?

What Soul could contain Evil unless through contact with the lower nature? Without that, there could be no desire, no sorrow, no rage, no fear. Fear affects the composite being when it dreads its own dissolution; pain and sorrow are the accompaniments of that dissolution. Desires arise from something disturbing the composite being or are a precaution against threatened trouble. Every impression is the impact of something irrational outside the Soul, accepted only because the Soul is not without parts or phases. The Soul adopts false notions because it has traveled outside of its own truth by ceasing to be purely itself.

There is one desire or appetite that does not fall under this condemnation; it is the aspiration toward the Intellectual-Principle <note>the Divine Mind or Nous</note>. This demands only that the Soul dwell alone, enshrined within that place of its choice, never falling toward the lower world.

Evil does not exist alone; by virtue of the nature and power of the Good, it is not purely Evil. It appears, necessarily, bound by the bonds of Beauty, like a captive held in golden chains. It is hidden beneath these so that, while it must exist, it may not be seen by the gods, and so that humans need not always have evil before their eyes. Instead, when evil comes before them, they may still not be lacking images of the Good and Beautiful for their remembrance.

---

### NINTH TRACTATE

#### “ THE REASONED DISMISSAL ”

> “ You shall not dismiss your Soul, lest it go forth . . . ” (taking something with it).

For wherever it goes, it will be in some definite condition, and its departure is to some new place. The Soul will wait for the body to be completely severed from it; then it makes no departure; it simply finds itself free.

<summary>Plotinus discusses how the Soul only encounters Evil through its contact with the material world and notes that Evil is veiled by Beauty to prevent it from overwhelming humanity. He also begins a tractate on the soul's departure, suggesting it should wait for a natural separation from the body to remain untainted.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the Ninth Tractate, "The Reasoned Dismissal," moving from the general nature of the Soul and its relation to Matter to the specific ethical question of suicide or the voluntary separation of the Soul from the body.</meta>

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>SUICIDE</header>
<sig>I. 9. 1]</sig>

But how does the body come to be separated?

The separation takes place when nothing of the Soul remains bound up with it: the harmony within the body, by virtue of which the Soul was retained, is broken and it can no longer hold its guest.

But when a man contrives the dissolution of the body, it is he that has used violence and torn himself away, not the body that has let the Soul slip from it. And in loosening the bond he has not been without passion; there has been revolt or grief or anger, movements which it is unlawful to indulge.

But what if a man feels himself to be losing his reason?

That is not likely in the Sage <note>the person who has attained philosophical wisdom and virtue</note>, but if it should occur, it must be classed with the inevitable, to be welcome at the bidding of the facts though not for its own sake. To call upon drugs for the release of the Soul seems a strange way of assisting its purposes.

And if there is a period allotted to all by fate, to anticipate the hour could not be a happy act, unless, as we have indicated, under stern necessity.

If everyone is to hold in the other world a standing determined by the state in which he left this one, there must be no withdrawal as long as there is any hope of progress.

<summary>Plotinus argues against suicide, stating that a soul should only leave the body naturally when the bodily harmony breaks, rather than through a passionate or forced exit. He suggests that since one's status in the afterlife depends on their state at death, one should persist in life as long as spiritual progress is possible.</summary>
<keywords>suicide, soul-body separation, the Sage, fate, afterlife, ethics, Plotinus, Ninth Tractate</keywords>

But how does the body allow itself to be separated?

The separation takes place when nothing of the soul remains bound up with it; the harmony within the body, by virtue of which the soul was restrained in it, is <unclear>lost</unclear> and it can no longer hold its guest.

But when a man commits the dissolution of the body, it is he who has used violence and torn himself away, not the body that has let the soul slip from it. And in loosening the bond, he has not been without passion; there has been fever or grief or anger, movements which it is unlawful to indulge.

But what if a man feels himself to be losing his reason?

That is not likely in the Sage <note>the person who has attained philosophical wisdom and virtue</note>; but if it should occur, it must be classed with the inevitable, to be welcome as the bidding of the facts, though not for its own sake. To cast open doors for the release of the soul seems a strange way of assisting its release.

And if there is a period allotted to everyone by fate, to anticipate the hour could not be a happy lot, unless, as we have indicated, under stern necessity.

Still, everyone is to find in the other world a standing determined by the state in which he left this one. One must be allowed to exist as long as there is any hope of <unclear>progress</unclear>.

# NOTES

## I. THE TEXT

The text used for this translation is that of Richard Volkmann (Teubner, Leipzig, 1883). Occasionally, a reading has been adopted from the textual variations or the extensive commentary provided in the three-volume edition by Frederick Creuzer (Oxford, 1835). Very rarely, the translator has felt compelled to attempt an emendation of his own.

## II. PREVIOUS TRANSLATIONS

The present translation has been meticulously compared, clause by clause and repeatedly, with the versions mentioned below:

**THE LATIN of FICINO (in Creuzer’s edition).**

**THE FRENCH of M. N. BOUILLET (three volumes, Paris, 1875, etc.).**

A complete version; often inaccurate and frequently conveying the meaning only vaguely, yet provided with the most copious and fascinating notes and commentary. To explain Plotinus’ general themes, Bouillet provides illustrations from the entire range of religious and mystical thought, beginning with the earliest thinkers, minutely comparing Plato, and borrowing from the Church Fathers, from works of Eastern mysticism, from the Kabbalah <note>The original text uses the spelling "Rabbalah."</note>, from medieval theologians, and from Malebranche, Spinoza, Leibniz, Bossuet, Thomassin, and others. He also uses Macrobius very effectively. Since Bouillet’s monumental work is long out of print and very rare, it would be a service to Plotinian studies to translate his notes and commentary in their entirety—the Greek and Latin portions as well as the French. If this were done, along with a summary of the passages of Plotinus being illustrated, the book would have great value of its own as an overview of the mystical thought that has entered into Christianity from the outside or been evolved by Christianity from its own depths.

**THE GERMAN of HERMANN FRIEDRICH MUELLER (2 volumes, Berlin: Weidmann, 1878-80).**

This valuable translation is described by its author as "*literal, but hardly palatable unless taken in conjunction with the Greek text*." Both statements are true; in parts, the version is even meaningless without a close study of the original.

**THE GERMAN of OTTO KIEFER (2 volumes, Diederichs: Jena and Leipzig, 1905).**

This is a book of selections, very extensive, which claims to omit only what is judged to be out of date, futile, or incomprehensible in the original; it is substantially a

<summary>This page details the Greek textual sources used for the translation and provides a critical evaluation of previous translations in Latin, French, and German, highlighting the extensive commentary of M. N. Bouillet.</summary>

Mueller made the text much more readable, often improving the sense, though it is sometimes feared that he also caused a deterioration.

[The translator, after reading some of the treatises translated into English by Thomas Taylor, decided—for reasons mainly literary—that the work of this devoted pioneer would not be helpful in the present undertaking. It has, therefore, not been used in any part of this work, except possibly through indirect suggestion from the quotations made occasionally in the commentaries of Bouillet and Creuzer.]

### III. METHOD OF THE PRESENT TRANSLATION

Inevitably, the present translator has sometimes differed from all his predecessors, just as they have sometimes differed from each other. He hopes it will not be thought arrogant of him to remark that his rendering of any given passage is not to be tested ultimately by the authority of any of these scholars, and still less by any preconceived idea of Plotinus' meaning or by any hasty memory of controversies and decisions regarding the peculiar uses of words in Plato or Aristotle. The text of the <term>Enneads</term> may be considered very fairly well-established, but it would be absurd to suppose that Plotinus—who was not very cautious or consistent in his verbal expression—has yet yielded his precise meaning or full content as Plato, for example, may be supposed to do now after the scholarly scrutiny of generations. It may, indeed, be said with a rough truth that Plotinus' terms—shifting at best and depending upon context, and again upon the context of the context—are never to be more carefully examined than when they seem to be most faithful to the *Platonic* or *Aristotelian* uses. This confusion is a constant pitfall: Plotinus was pouring entirely new wine into very old bottles. Plotinus is often to be understood by swift and broad leaps of the mind—a mind trained to his methods—rather than by laborious, word-straining investigation. We must know him through and through before we can be quite sure of his minor meanings anywhere; many more scholars must work on this, and many different types of minds, before we can hope to have a perfectly true translation of the <term>Enneads</term> in any language. The present worker must have made mistakes, some perhaps that will one day appear inexcusable to himself. His one consolation is that the interpretation he will welcome from other hands that day has almost certainly passed through his own and been deliberately rejected. Where he appears most certainly to have ignored the truth, it is most certain that he has passed through an agony of hesitation.

People seem always anxious to know whether a work of translation is what they call "literal"; the important question is rather whether it is "faithful." This work claims to be faithful—and, if we must be precise, literary rather than literal. This is not to say that it is a paraphrase.

Probably every translator from the classic tongues sets out cheerfully with the firm purpose of achieving the impossible: of making a literal translation that shall also be a piece of sound and flowing idiomatic writing; and certainly many critics demand such a miracle. Some years ago, upon the publication of a preliminary specimen of this present venture, one very highly accomplished scholar wrote complaining with total seriousness of an English past tense which had dared to translate a "<term>frequentative aorist</term>" of the Greek original. He had apparently never asked himself whether an English past tense may not be as frequentative as any Greek

<summary>The translator discusses his methodology, rejecting a strictly literal approach in favor of a "faithful" and literary translation that captures Plotinus' unique and often inconsistent philosophical language. He notes that Plotinus requires a holistic understanding of his system rather than a mere word-by-word analysis of his terminology.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Enneads, translation methodology, Thomas Taylor, Mueller, linguistic analysis, Plato, Aristotle, frequentative aorist</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>Continuing from the discussion on page 114 regarding the difficulties of translating Plotinus and the inconsistencies in his verbal expression.</meta>

<page-num>115</page-num>
# METHOD OF THIS TRANSLATION

aorist: in any case, readers who desire their translations to serve as an unfailing treasury of illustrations for "X. on Greek Idioms" are not asked to like this version.

Again, various arbitrary principles, established by translators of a strictly formal school, have been completely ignored here. For example, it has been decreed that "one word must translate one word," and this has a double application:

1. That if, for example, the word <term>Phusis</term> is once translated "Nature," <term>Phusis</term> must stand as "Nature" at every repetition, never "Kind" or "Essence," or "Being" or any other word which happens, in a particular context, to be equally clear and precise or even necessary in English for the sense and connection of thought.
2. That <term>Phusis</term>, for example, may never be translated by such a double phrase as "Nature or <term>Hypostasis</term>," and <term>Doxa</term>, for example, never by such a double as "Opinion or Seeming-Knowledge," still less—as occurs several times here—by "Ordinary Mentation," with or without an alternative or an addition.

All such restrictions have been treated here as belonging to the childish pedantry of a game of skill, not to the serious task of conveying a profound body of foreign thought to the reader. Probably in every writer—certainly in <term>Plotinus</term>—such a word as <term>Phusis</term>, such a word as <term>Theos</term>, or again <term>Theios</term>, may carry in its connotation not merely two but three or four or more notions, any one of which may at a given moment be the dominant one, though not necessarily to the utter exclusion of the others. <term>Plotinus</term> has about twenty words—technical terms—which he uses in very varying applications where no single fixed English word or even combination of words would always carry his meaning. In this whole matter, the translator has adopted the principle of using such a variety of terms, single or double or occasionally triple, as will exactly cover or carry the idea that appears in the original; he has claimed for himself almost the entire freedom of a philosophic writer in English who uses his words with an absolute loyalty, of course, to his thought, but without a moment’s hesitation regarding the terms in which he happened to convey or indicate a given notion five pages earlier. In other words, the present translator has not thought of his probable readers as glossary-bound pedants, but as possessed of the living vision that can follow a stream of thought by the light of its own vivid movement.

Other theorists of translation desire that a version should represent the style of the original writer: this notion is tempting and may often be safely achieved but not, the present translator ventures to say, in the case of <term>Plotinus</term>, or perhaps in the case of any writer whose main preoccupation is less with artistic expression than with the enunciation of central and very deeply important ideas. <term>Longinus</term>, as may be learned from <term>Porphyry’s</term> biographical sketch of <term>Plotinus</term>, so poorly grasped <term>Plotinus’</term> manner of expression that he judged even the most faithful transcripts to be disastrously incorrect. A version which attempted to reproduce a style that disconcerted and misled the most widely read contemporary critic of Greek literature would not be a translation in any useful sense of the word, or at least would not be English or readable.

The present translation, therefore, has been executed with the fundamental goal of carrying <term>Plotinus’</term> thought—its strength and its weakness alike—to the mind of the English reader. The first aim has been the utmost attainable clarity in the faithful, full, and pure expression of the meaning; the second aim, set a long way after the first, has been the reproduction of the splendid soaring passages with all their warmth and light.

<summary>The translator rejects rigid linguistic rules, such as "one word for one word," in favor of a flexible approach that captures the shifting meanings of Plotinus's technical terms. He prioritizes clarity of philosophical thought over the imitation of Plotinus’s original, difficult style.</summary>
<keywords>translation theory, Plotinus, Phusis, Doxa, Hypostasis, Longinus, Porphyry, technical terms, philosophical prose</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion from page 115 regarding the translator's philosophy of prioritizing the clarity of Plotinus's thought over literal, word-for-word translation or rigid adherence to technical term consistency.</meta>

Nothing at all has been, consciously, added or omitted with the absurd purpose of heightening either the force of the thought or the beauty of the expression—except insofar as force and beauty demand a clarity that must sometimes be, courageously, imposed upon what is probably the most negligent of the world's great authors.

### IV. COMMENTARIES

The translator has necessarily been indebted for guidance, both direct and indirect, to many works of metaphysics, theology, and even history—but there are some that must be specifically named: books to which he has been under the deepest, constant obligation while grappling immediately with the text, and also books to which the beginner in Plotinian studies may be usefully directed.

->IN ENGLISH<-

**THE NEO-PLATONISTS: a study in the history of Hellenism**: by THOMAS WHITTAKER (Cambridge University Press: 1901): as a formal scientific exposition of the entire history of the school and a map of the Plotinian system (about sixty pages summarizing the *Enneads*), this book is of the highest value.

Much useful insight is contained in "**THE WISDOM OF PLOTINUS: a Metaphysical Study**" by CHARLES J. WHITBY, London (William Rider and Son: 1909).

There is nothing more helpful, in its own wide field, for understanding and appreciating Plotinus than the late PRINCIPAL CAIRD’S beautiful work, "**THE EVOLUTION OF THEOLOGY IN THE GREEK PHILOSOPHERS: Gifford Lectures for 1901-2**" (Glasgow: MacLehose: 1904). This book, written in the highest spirit, contains some exquisite fragments from the *Enneads*. The author presumably disdained the humble work of a mere translator, but had he given us a complete translation, the world would have been richer by a classic of thought and a classic of language.

In "**Neo-Platonism**" by C. BIGG, D.D. (London: Society for Promoting Christian Knowledge: 1895), we have a good general summary, somewhat popular in tone, of the system and of its history and results.

Another work that would serve as an adequate explanation of practically the whole system is "**THE PROBLEM OF EVIL IN PLOTINUS**": B. A. G. FULLER: 1912. It includes translations of many essential passages and is written throughout in as clear a style as has ever explained a metaphysical system.

"**THE PHILOSOPHY OF PLOTINUS**," by DAVID SYLVAN GUTHRIE (Philadelphia, Dunlop Printing Co., 1896) is a brief summary containing a helpful index to the most fundamentally significant passages of the *Enneads*.

The valuable works of Miss UNDERHILL, dealing with the history and methods of the schools and masters of mysticism, are too well known to need more than a mention.

The translator owes acknowledgment to G. R. S. MEADE <note>Commonly spelled G.R.S. Mead.</note>, whose great labor on the Hermetic documents gave him many valuable suggestions.

EDWARD CARPENTER’S work "**THE ART OF CREATION**" (George Allen: 1907) will be found very helpful, as it exhibits—in modern terms and with the support of modern metaphysical and scientific investigation—much that is either fundamental or implied in the Plotinian system.

These recommended works range from formal scientific expositions to studies on mysticism and the problem of evil.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the discussion of English-language resources for studying Plotinus, this page evaluates key French and German scholarship and begins the introduction to Plotinus's terminology.</meta>

### FRENCH AND GERMAN

No specialist student of Plotinus can ignore JULES SIMON'S “HISTOIRE DE L'ÉCOLE D'ALEXANDRIE”: it is very thorough and detailed; it is marred in places by a strange and most unphilosophical contempt where Plotinus's magnificent attempt to explain the Universe is found to involve the contradictions or gaps <note>lacunae</note> perhaps inevitable to all such religious or philosophical efforts. Simon's numerical references to the *Enneads* are often incorrect.

VACHEROT'S volume, with the same title as Simon's, is another work of primary importance: it is described by H. Guyot as intelligent and systematic—the best existing in French and still to be read profitably even after Zeller's exposition. M. Guyot, however, finds that Vacherot's Hegelianism somewhat skews his judgments of the Plotinian system.

“L'INFINITÉ DIVINE DEPUIS PHILON JUSQU'À PLOTIN”: HENRI GUYOT: (Paris: Alcan: 1906). This small book contains, in less than one hundred of its two hundred and fifty pages, some of the most original and subtle analysis, synthesis, and interpretation yet presented of Plotinus's dominant ideas: it is written very clearly and even pleasantly. An English translation would provide the language with another valuable document of metaphysical mysticism.

“DIE LEHRE VOM LOGOS IN DER GRIECHISCHEN PHILOSOPHIE”: DR. MAX HEINZE: Oldenburg, 1872. A most valuable work.

“DIE PSYCHOLOGIE DES PLOTIN”: DR. ARTHUR RICHTER: (Halle: 1867).

In all the books mentioned, one will find more or less extensive bibliographies, including works—mostly in German—which the present translator has either been unable to obtain or has not found very useful.

It is appropriate to conclude this chapter of acknowledgments with an expression of the translator's deep obligation to Mr. Ernest R. Debenham, whose interest in Plotinus and friendly assistance in the publishing world have resulted in the production of this version of the first *Ennead*.

## V. TERMINOLOGY

THE six *Enneads*—six sets of nine treatises—do not constitute or include a formal, step-by-step statement or demonstration of Plotinian doctrine: the entire system is assumed in each of the separate treatises, which take the form of special developments or demonstrations of significant points, rather than chapters in a single work of consecutive exposition.

Hence, without a prior knowledge of the main doctrines, almost any of the treatises must appear incomprehensible or, worse, be radically misunderstood; the terminology, simple enough in itself, becomes dishearteningly mysterious or seriously misleading.

A serious misunderstanding may be caused, to take one instance among several, by carelessly attributing meanings or connotations to terms used by Plotinus that are commonly associated with those words in modern philosophy or religion; on the other hand, there is in places almost a certainty of missing these same religious or philosophical

<summary>The author reviews significant French and German scholarly works on Plotinus and explains that the Enneads lack a systematic structure, requiring readers to understand Plotinian terminology to avoid misinterpretation.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Enneads, Jules Simon, Vacherot, Henri Guyot, Max Heinze, Arthur Richter, Ernest R. Debenham, terminology, scholarship, bibliography</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>Continuing from the discussion of modern and historical scholarship, this section transitions into an introduction of Plotinus's core terminology and his cosmological framework.</meta>

implications or connotations where, to the initiate, the phrase of Plotinus conveys them intensely.

Thus, it is not easy, without knowledge and the training of habit, to tremble with any real rapture at the notion of becoming “wholly identified with the Intellectual-Principle.” When it is understood and deeply realized at every moment that “The Intellectual-Principle” is the highest accessible “Person” of the Godhead—is truly God, the Supreme Wisdom immanent within the human soul and yet ineffably superior to all the rest of the universe—then perhaps we may feel the great call to the devotion that has such a reward.

We must, then, learn at the very beginning the main lines of the Plotinian explanation of the Heavens, the Earth, and the Human Being if we are to obtain from our author—our temporary Master—the depth of his philosophical meaning and the warmth of his religious fervor.

It is not possible to cram the Plotinian system unharmed into a confined space: to be brief is necessarily to be inaccurate. What follows is merely a rough chart intended to give the first essential orientation, to indicate the main course of the great highways and to name the commanding landmarks; it is the natural and necessary introduction to the Terminology, and nothing more.

### THE DIVINE NAMES

The system of Plotinus is a system of necessary EMANATION, PROCESSION, or IRRADIATION accompanied by necessary ASPIRATION or REVERSION-TO-SOURCE: all the forms and phases of Existence flow from the Divinity, and all strive to return THERE and to remain THERE.

This Divinity is a graded Triad.

Its three Hypostases—or in modern religious terminology, “Persons”—are, in the briefest description:

1. THE ONE, or First Existent.
2. THE DIVINE MIND, or First Thinker and Thought.
3. THE ALL-SOUL, or First and Only Principle of Life.

> “The governance and the existence of all things are in these Three.”

#### I. THE ONE

The First Hypostasis of the Supreme Divine Triad is variously named: often it is simply “THE FIRST.” Viewed logically or dialectically, it is THE ONE. Morally seen, it is THE GOOD; in various other uses or aspects, it is THE SIMPLE, THE ABSOLUTE, THE TRANSCENDENCE, THE INFINITE, THE UNCONDITIONED; it is sometimes THE FATHER.

It is unknowable: its nature—or its Super-nature, its Supra-existence—is conveyed theoretically by the simple statement that it transcends all that is knowable, and practically most often by the negation of all Quality. Thus, if we call it the GOOD, we do not intend any formal affirmation of a quality within itself; we mean only that it is the Goal or Term to which all aspires. When we affirm existence of it, we mean no more than that it does not fall within the realm of non-existents; it transcends even the quality of Being.

<summary>This page introduces the core components of Plotinus's philosophical system, specifically the Divine Triad consisting of The One, The Divine Mind, and The All-Soul. It explains the concept of emanation and the nature of "The One" as an unknowable, transcendent source beyond all qualities and even Being itself.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Intellectual-Principle, Emanation, Procession, Reversion-to-Source, Divine Triad, Hypostases, The One, The Good, Neoplatonism, Divine Mind, All-Soul</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>Continuing the introduction to Plotinus's terminology, this page transitions from the general concept of the Divine Triad to a specific definition of the First Principle (The One) and the second hypostasis, the Intellectual-Principle.</meta>

It is not the Creator; it can hardly even be rightly called the First Cause. Its solitary majesty rejects all such descriptions of action. In this realm of the unknowable, the First Cause is, strictly speaking, a lower principle than THE FIRST, which cannot be spoken of in any terms of human thought.

We can say no more of it—and even then only with infinite caution, always appealing to a deep sense behind the words—than that it exists in an ineffable Supra-Existence; that it acts in an ineffable Super-Act; that it is everywhere, in the sense that without its Supra-Existence nothing could exist; and that it is nowhere, in that it is loftily detached from everything else. To the extent that language and the undeniable force of human thought drive us to speak of it as a Cause, we must keep in mind that it is so only because its Perfection implies an Act, a production, or—to use a metaphor fundamental to Plotinus—a "generation" of something other than itself. For Existence, or Supra-Existence, entails an expressive Act. The most perfect form of expressive Act is Thought or Intellection. Therefore, the Divine Existence, or Supra-Existence, produces a Divine Thought or Intellection.

### 2. THE INTELLECTUAL-PRINCIPLE

THIS Divine Thought is, of course, a Real Being, the first "thing" whose existence may be affirmed, if only in a somewhat vague sense. It is an Intelligence, or rather, it is the Universal Intelligence. As the act, offspring, and image of The First, it serves as a kind of mediation for us to the Unknowable ONE. In Greek, it is named <term>ho Noûs</term>, which has often, perhaps not very successfully, been translated as DIVINE MIND, and sometimes as DIVINE INTELLIGENCE or DIVINE INTELLECTION. In the present translation, it is most often conveyed by the rather clumsy term—which is, in practice, expressive and convenient—“THE INTELLECTUAL-PRINCIPLE.”

It must be noted that in English, as in Greek, the same term is used for the parallel Principle and Act in humans. In both the divine and human realms, the INTELLECTUAL-PRINCIPLE signifies the highest thing that is truly knowable. Therefore, to fully grasp the mystical or religious suggestion of a passage, the reader will often find it useful to re-translate—that is, to temporarily substitute the term "SPIRIT" for "Intellectual-Principle," or even the term "Supreme Soul," despite the awkward overlap.

With this <term>Noûs</term>—or Divine Mind, Divine Intellection, or Divine Intellectual-Principle—the existence of Plurality, Complexity, or Multiplicity begins. The Divine Mind contains, or rather is, <term>ta Noeta</term>, which means the Intellectual Universe or Intelligible Universe, often known as The Intelligible or The Intelligibles.

The Intellectual or Intelligible Universe is the totality of the Divine Thoughts, generally known by the phrase familiar in Platonism as "The Ideas."

The Ideas, or Divine Thoughts, are Real Beings, Intelligences, and Powers. They are the eternal Originals, Archetypes, and Intellectual Forms of everything that exists in the lower spheres. In certain respects, this sphere of the Intelligibles would be best named The Spiritual Universe. Principal Caird agrees with Whittaker in finding it closely resembles Dante’s conception of the circle of angels and blessed spirits gathered in contemplation and service around the throne of God.

The Intellectual or Intelligible Universe contains, or even in some sense is, all particular minds or intelligences; and these, in their various kinds, are images, representations,

<summary>This page defines "The First" as an unknowable supra-existence beyond the concept of a First Cause and introduces the "Intellectual-Principle" (Nous) as its first manifestation. It explains that this Principle contains the "Ideas" or the "Intelligible Universe," which serves as the archetype for all existence.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, The One, Intellectual-Principle, Nous, ta Noeta, The Ideas, Divine Mind, Intelligible Universe, Neoplatonism, Archetypes</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the Intellectual-Principle's relationship to the material world and introduces the Third Hypostasis, the All-Soul, completing the Divine Triad. It also begins to discuss how the Three Hypostases are often treated as a single, unified Divine Realm.</meta>

...phantasms, "shadows" of this Universal or Divine Mind. All phases of existence—even down to Matter, the ultimate, lowest, and faintest image of Real Being—are all "ideally" present from eternity in this Realm of divine Thoughts, this Totality of Supreme Wisdom or "Mentation."

The Supreme <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> cannot be unproductive; accompanying its Act of Thought, there is what we may roughly describe as an "Act of Act." Divine Thinking "engenders a power capable of realizing its Thought"—that is, capable of "Creation." This produced power is the Third <term>Hypostasis</term> of the Divine Triad.

### 3. THE ALL-SOUL

THE THIRD <term>HYPOSTASIS</term> of the Divine Triad is, then, the ALL-SOUL, UNIVERSAL SOUL, or SOUL OF THE ALL. It is the eternal emanation and image of the Second <term>Hypostasis</term>, the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>.

Just as the Divine <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> has, from our view, two Acts—that of upward contemplation of THE ONE and that of "generation" toward what is lower—the All-Soul also has two Acts. It simultaneously contemplates the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> and "generates," through the abundance of its own perfection, the lowest possible forms of existence. Thus, in the <term>Enneads</term>, we often find a verbal division of the All-Soul. We hear of the "Leading-Principle of the Soul," or the "Celestial Soul," concentrated in contemplation of its superior, and the "Lower Soul" (also called the "Nature-Looking and Generative Soul"), whose function it is to generate or fashion the lower, material Universe based on the model of the Divine Thoughts—the "Ideas" stored within the Divine Mind. This lower principle in the Soul is sometimes called the <term>Logos</term> of the Universe, or the "Reason-Principle" of the Universe. The All-Soul is the active cause of movement as well as of Form. More directly than the two superior or "earlier" <term>Hypostases</term> of the Divine Triad, it is the eternal cause of the existence—the eternal existence—of the <term>KOSMOS</term>, the "WORLD," or the material, sense-perceived Universe, which is the Soul’s Act and emanation, image, and "shadow." It is, therefore, the Creator and the Vital Principle of all that is lower or "later" than the Divine Triad. In a sense that does not need to be minutely detailed here, the All-Soul includes, and is, All Souls. For the general reader’s initial practical purposes, it may be conveniently described in a stanza by Richard Watson Dixon:

> “There is a soul above the soul of each,
> A mightier soul which yet to each belongs:
> There is a sound made of all human speech,
> And as numerous as the gathering of all songs:
> And in that soul each lives, and in each lives that soul,
> Though all the ages are its vast lifetime;
> Each soul that dies in its most sacred whole
> Receives life that shall forever last.”

### THE DIVINE-TRIAD AS A UNITY

The three <term>Hypostases</term> of the Supreme Being are, of course, very frequently spoken of collectively as one transcendent Being or one Divine Realm. Sometimes, even when one of the three is specifically named, the entire context shows that the reference is not to the <term>Hypostasis</term> actually named, but to the Triad collectively or to one of the two not

<summary>This page explains the Third Hypostasis, the All-Soul, which acts as the bridge between the Divine Mind and the material world, and introduces the concept of the Divine Triad as a unified entity.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, All-Soul, Divine Triad, Intellectual-Principle, Hypostasis, Enneads, Kosmos, Logos, Richard Watson Dixon, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the relationship between the three Hypostases—The First (The Good), the Intellectual-Principle, and the All-Soul—detailing how they form a unified Divinity and how their creative energy extends down to the lowest forms of existence, including Matter. It also introduces the terminology for higher beings like the Gods and Daimones as used in Plotinus's system.</meta>

named: thus, where the All-Soul is specified in a moral connection, the reference may really be to The First, to The Good; and where the connection is intellectual rather than moral or merely dynamic, the All-Soul may be used as a comprehensive term for the Godhead with a real reference to the Second <term>Hypostasis</term>, to the Divine-Mind.

The <term>Triad</term>, it must never be forgotten under any circumstances, is The Divinity, and each <term>Hypostasis</term> is Divine: the All-Soul, as Jules Simon well remarks, is the expression of the outgoing energy of the Divinity, just as the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> is the expression of the Godhead's internal Thought or Vision.

The Divinity is communicated and approached by the channel of any one of the three <term>Hypostases</term>. The <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> performs its Act regarding The First, toward Which it "looks" in eternal "contemplation," while, from its abundance, it engenders the <term>Vital-Principle</term> or Soul; similarly, the All-Soul ceaselessly "looks" toward the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>, while, from its lavish energy, it engenders or creates all lower things, down to the lowest form of being in the visible universe. Thus, the Divinity is communicated to all things. Now, this action within the Divine Circle is reflected by a parallel action in the lower <term>Kosmos</term>. All "Nature," even in its lowest forms, is in ceaseless CONTEMPLATION and ASPIRATION: while every being, until the ultimate limit is reached, tends to engender an image of itself, it also tends to rejoin the next highest level, of which it is itself a shadow or lower manifestation. Even MATTER, which is all but outcast from the sphere of Being and unable to engender anything, has the power of receiving form and is, thereby, tending feebly toward <term>Authentic-Existence</term>, toward Soul and Mind, and so is linked, distantly, with the Divine.

## THE GODS AND DAIMONES

"THE GODS" are frequently mentioned in the <term>Enneads</term>: the words are generally little more than a fossil survival, an accident of language rather than a reality of thought. Where, however, Plotinus names Ouranios (Coelius), Kronos (Saturn), or Zeus (Jupiter), he indicates the three <term>Hypostases</term> of the Divine-Being: this is part of his general assumption that his entire system is already contained in the most ancient knowledge of the world.

Where we meet "The Gods" without any specific description, we are to understand them according to the context: sometimes as the entire Divine Order; sometimes as the Divine-Thoughts, The Ideas or Archetypes; sometimes as exalted Beings vaguely understood to exist above man as ministers of the Supreme; sometimes as the stars and earth, thought of—at least in their soul-part—as Divine-Beings; sometimes the words indicate, vaguely, the souls of lofty men; and sometimes there is a vague, sleepy acceptance of the popular notion of the Olympian personalities.

The <term>DAIMONES</term> are, strictly speaking, lofty powers beneath the "Gods": in practice, they are often confounded with the Gods. The same word is translated here, according to context and English connotation, as "Supernals," Celestials, Divine Spirits, or Blessed Spirits.

## MAN: HIS NATURE, POWERS AND DESTINY

PORPHYRY'S arrangement of the <term>Enneads</term> has, at least, this one advantage: that Plotinus's work opens for us with a treatise dealing mainly—and not inadequately or, on the whole, obscurely—with the Nature of Man. Here, then, we may be very brief.

# PLOTINUS

The Third <term>Hypostasis</term> of the Divinity—the <term>All-Soul</term>, the Universal Life-Principle—includes and is all souls. The human soul is, therefore, the <term>All-Soul</term>; however, it is the <term>All-Soul</term> placed in contact with lower things. It is the <term>All-Soul</term> made particular for the duration, at least, of the mortal life of man.

This specialization is necessarily a limitation: it sets boundaries and involves a temporary application to one thing rather than another. We may, therefore, identify different phases of the <term>All-Soul</term> within us. These phases or images of the Divine Soul are found to be three:

1. The Intellective Soul (or the Intuitive, Intellectual, or Intelligent Soul, also known as the Intellectual Principle of the Soul).
2. The Reasoning Soul.
3. The Unreasoning Soul.

1. The Intellective Soul is unaffected by external influences and is almost entirely untouched by Matter. By its very nature, it is forever separated from the body. Its Act is the act of Intellection, or Intuition, or the True-Knowing of Real Existences; its being consists of the eternal contemplation of the Divine. This Act of the Intellective Soul—which is identical to the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> in Man—is, however, not perceived by the individual unless they have identified their entire being with this highest principle through a life of philosophical morality (Sanctity or Sagehood).

2. The Reasoning Soul is the principle of characteristic human life. To live by the first soul, the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>, is to live as a God; in this second soul, we find the principle that constitutes the normal nature of man. This Reasoning Soul is separable from the body but is not actually separated from it. Its Act is "Discursive Reasoning." It knows things not through the instantaneous, direct, and entirely adequate True-Knowing of the first soul, but rather step by step, arriving at knowledge through doubt and logic—a knowledge that is imperfect even at its best. In its lower functions, its result is <term>doxa</term>, an untranslatable word usually rendered as "Opinion." In this translation, depending on the context, it is represented as "Surface-Knowledge," "Ordinary Thinking," "Sense-Knowing," "Sense-Knowledge," or similar terms.

This second phase of the human soul also possesses the three faculties known as Will, Intellectual Imagination, and Intellectual Memory. Intellectual Imagination and Intellectual Memory are distinct from the lower forms of imagination and memory; they deal with the intellectual element of sensation, presenting sensations to the higher faculty for judgment and for the purposes of the semi-divine life of the philosophical Man.

3. The final phase of the soul, the Unreasoning Soul, is the Principle of Animal Life. In conjunction with the body, it constitutes the "Animal" as distinct from the "Man." For reasons of emotional association or clarity, this phase of the soul combined with the body is sometimes said to produce not "The Animal" but "The Animate" or "The Animate Entity." Plotinus also calls this conjunction the "Two-together," usually translated here as the <term>Couplement</term>.

The faculties of this "Unreasoning Soul" or "Couplement" are: the sensible (or sense-grasping) imagination and sensible memory, the appetites rooted in the flesh, passivity (or the faculty of sensation), and the vegetative, nutritive, and generative faculties.

<summary>This page defines the three levels of the human soul in Plotinus's philosophy: the divine Intellective Soul, the characteristic human Reasoning Soul, and the animalistic Unreasoning Soul. It explains how these levels interact with the body and the higher divine principles.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, All-Soul, Intellective Soul, Reasoning Soul, Unreasoning Soul, Discursive Reasoning, doxa, Couplement, Intellectual-Principle, human nature, Triad, Hypostasis</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the phases of the human soul, specifically identifying the lowest phase of the All-Soul within man, and transitions into a defining analysis of Matter, Evil, and Morality within the Plotinian system.</meta>

# TERMINOLOGY : MATTER AND MORALITY

This last soul, or phase of the All-Soul, represents the very lowest “strength” of the Divinity in man, except for the Matter which is organized by the All-Soul into the form of the body. In other words, this last soul represents the bare fact of life, descending as low as the life of a plant.

In Plotinus’s usage, the word "Soul" applied to man often conveys the idea of the highest part of man, what we would be inclined to call Spirit; sometimes, where the activity is mainly an intellectual operation, "Mind" will be the closest translation; very often, “Life-Principle” is the most accurate.

### MATTER

Matter in Man, before it is organized or shaped by the All-Soul, is the same as <term>Matter</term> everywhere else. There is a certain tendency to think of Matter as being “material”—for example, as flesh or clay in man, or as some sort of powdery beginning or residue of things in the world at large. This misconception must be carefully guarded against. “Matter,” says Jules Simon, “is a demand of thought rather than a reality of existence.” This is perhaps to state the case rashly, but it is certainly closer to the true concept than the notion the word conveys to an untrained mind.

Matter is the last, lowest, and least emanation of the creative power of the All-Soul—or rather, it is even slightly lower than that. It is, roughly speaking, the point at which the creative or generative power comes to a halt. It is the Ultimate Possible; it is almost <term>Non-Being</term>. It would be <term>Non-Being</term> except that <term>Absolute Non-Being</term> is non-existent and impossible in a world emanating from the bounty of <term>Being</term>. No doubt it is often called <term>Non-Being</term>, but this is not a strict definition; it is a convenient expression for its utter, nearly infinite remoteness from the <term>Authentic-Existence</term> to which, in the long line of descent, it owes its origin.

We are to think of it—as is indicated in the tract on Evil (I. 8)—as invisible, imperceptible to any sense, and unknowable by any reach of the mind except through its negation of everything the mind can grasp, however feebly. It is utterly outside of the realm of form, except in so far as it feebly stretches toward some determination in the universal longing of all things toward the <term>Goodness</term> and <term>Wisdom</term> from which all have sprung, however remotely.

### EVIL

To the extent that Evil exists, the root of evil is in Matter; but Evil does not exist. All that exists, in a state of half-existence, is the final effort of <term>The Good</term>—the point at which <term>The Good</term> ceases because, so to speak, endlessness has nearly faded out to an end. If this seems too violent a paradox to even be mentioned among us, we must remember that it is to some degree merely metaphorical, like so much in Plotinus: it is an almost desperate effort to express a combined idea that seems to be instinctive in the mind, the idea that Good reaches everywhere and yet has degrees; that an infinitely powerful Wisdom exists and operates, casting an infinite splendor on all its works, while we ourselves can see, or think we see, its failures or the last and weakest rays of its light.

### MORALITY

THE existence, or half-existence, of Matter brings about the necessity of morality: The Divine perfection is above morality; it is “unmoral.” The purely material is below morality;

<vocab>All-Soul, Plotinus, Life-Principle, Matter, Jules Simon, Non-Being, Absolute Non-Being, Authentic-Existence, Evil, The Good, Morality</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the lowest phase of the human soul as the "Life-Principle" and explains that Matter is not physical substance but the lowest, nearly non-existent emanation of the Divine. It further argues that Evil is not a positive existence but rather the point where the influence of The Good reaches its absolute limit and fades away.</summary>
