<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the human soul and its relationship to Matter, transitioning into the ethical and spiritual goals of the human experience according to Plotinus.</meta>

morality is for man; man—being divine at his highest level and “human” at the middle, and brute below that and merely vegetative below that and merely Matter in the lowest range of his nature—man, if he is to reach his good, the desire of every being, must “illumine what in him is dark, and raise and support what is low,” if he is to rise to the height of his great purpose, become what his highest self is, and attain his eternally destined Goal <note>Term</note>.

### THE TERM AND THE WAY

His Way is indicated in many magnificent passages of the *Enneads*—it is coldly charted for him in the treatise on Dialectic, I. 3. The Goal <note>Term</note> is more richly described in the famous sixth treatise of the same First Ennead: the main need, the cry of man’s nature, is to become actually what he is always potentially: Divine. All his faculties—each an image of the one next above it—culminate in the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> or <term>Intellective-Principle</term>, the Intuitional or True-knowing Faculty. His duty, or rather his happiness, his blessedness, and his deepest inner choice, is to work his entire being into identification with this, the Divine within him. Through this inner Divine, in an ecstasy away from all that is lower, and first from all that links him to Matter, he may even in this life attain to the “possession” of the Godhead in an ineffable act of identification—becoming UNIATE, one with God, actually God, and foretasting the blessedness of the final Return. After this, he is for all of eternity to be with the Godhead, to be Divine, or to be God.

---

## MINOR POINTS OF TERMINOLOGY

<term>AUTHENTIC-EXISTENT</term>, <term>AUTHENTIC-EXISTENTS</term>, and <term>AUTHENTIC-EXISTENCE</term> represent what is usually conveyed by the English philosophical term *Real-Being*. This choice was made mainly for reasons of literary convenience: an original writer can play with their sentence construction to avoid the awkward clash between the Noun and Participle; a translator works more freely when there is no possibility of this clash.

It happens, moreover, that the adopted term is better in itself, at least for Plotinian uses: *Real-Being* carries some undesirable suggestion of the purely abstract; “*The Authentic-Existent*” carries something of the notion of Person or Individuality in a majestic sense and, so, is often (though not by any means always) closer to the Plotinian notion. The need for such a departure from the customary term was suggested by Mr. Meade’s use of the emphatic “*That which is*” for the same concept: Mr. Meade’s term was rejected only because it sounds a little grandiose, does not fit conveniently into every sentence, and has no handy plural form.

As for Plotinus’ use of the idea, it must be pointed out that it most often represents the very highest level, but sometimes it is employed in a derogatory sense: the Sphere of Existence is often the Intellectual-and-Intelligible-Kosmos, Divine Mind, or in general, The Divine. Sometimes, however, it means the realm of process or of “*Becoming*,” as opposed to the stately immobility of the Divine Beings, who are then considered collectively as <term>Supra-Existents</term>.

SENSATION AND SENSE-PERCEPTION are used almost interchangeably for any action or

<meta>This page continues the definitions of technical terms used in the translation of Plotinus, specifically clarifying the metaphysical vocabulary and the editorial choices made by Porphyry.</meta>

passive-state by which man experiences the material world or any of its manifestations or representations.

ACT, with a capital letter, usually translates the difficult word <term>Energeia</term> and stands for the Expression of the Identity of any being or for its characteristic function—an expression and function which may often be entirely internal, the very opposite of any action upon the outside world.

In general, capitalization implies some more or less important technical use of a word.

"THERE" — "IN THE SUPREME" — "IN THE BEYOND" and other similar words or phrases translate, as convenient, the word <term>Ekei</term> used by Plotinus for the Divine Sphere, the Intelligible World.

THE SAGE translates <term>ho Spoudaios</term>, and means the accomplished Mystic, the Adept, almost the "<term>Uniate</term>," the human being who has become "wholly Divine."

PHILOSOPHY in Plotinus often means not Metaphysics but the Act or State of the <term>Uniate</term>: it might often, without much loss of tone, be taken as the equivalent of 1, Sanctity, and 2, the Mystic Way.

EARLIER and LATER refer to the order of emanation and therefore indicate the rank of nearness or distance regarding the Divine.

"WE READ" represents the "He says" with which Plotinus, like the Pythagoreans referring to their own Master, quotes or paraphrases Plato. Where Plato is mentioned by name, the name appears in this translation. It has not been considered necessary to give chapter and verse for the Platonic references, since the passages are invariably those which have most often entered into controversy or into literary allusion.

"ELSEWHERE" and similar phrases may puzzle the reader: it must be remembered that we are reading the treatises in the order of Porphyry's editing, not in the order of Plotinus' writing. An allusion or demonstration referred to in this First Ennead may be contained in the Sixth.

ACT, with a capital letter, usually translates the difficult word <term>Energeia</term> and stands for the expression of the identity of any being, as for its characteristic function—an expression and function which may often be entirely within the very source of any operation upon matter.

In general, capitalization implies some more or less important technical use of a word. **OUTNESS** is used in the **BURNET** in the sense of the "Beyond-world"; and other similar words of the translator conveniently use the word "the Beyond" or even "the Realm" for the Divine Sphere, the Intelligible World.

THE SAGE translates <term>Sophos</term> and means the achieved mystic, the adept, almost "the <term>Uniate</term>," the human being who has become "wholly the Divine."

It has not been considered necessary to give the book and verse for the references because the source is so invariably those which have been cited at some length in the Introduction.

Elsewhere "the earlier thinkers" are the Pre-Socratics. It must be remembered that we are reading the treatises in the order of Porphyry's editing—that of the Enneads—an allocation or classification by subject which often distorts the order of composition.

(Students should consult the small volume on the transcendental work of Cudworth and Henry More; and there is material from the later Platonists considerably indicating such Plotinian themes: Professor J.A. Stewart's "The Myths of Plato"; the statements and the illustrative footnotes of John Smith (Harvard University Press), which contain several useful essays on this theme.)

As for the Plotinian word **Outness**, it may be pointed out that it most often represents the early constitution of ultimate fact as **Outness** in a derogatory sense: the Sphere of **Outness** is often the Intellectual and Intelligible Essence, Divine Mind, or in general The Divine; sometimes, however, it means the realm of process or of Becoming as opposed to the stately unmoving in the Divine Being, then considered as potentially Super-Existential.

# THE PRELLER-RITTER EXTRACTS FORMING A
# CONSPECTUS OF THE PLOTINIAN SYSTEM

**THE** translator had written extensive notes regarding many passages of the Enneads when, finally and just in time, he realized that the method of specific explanation would lead inevitably to endless repetition and significant final ambiguity. In a good translation, no single passage should be difficult for anyone who has a firm grasp of the Neoplatonic system.

After studying the general exposition given under the heading "Terminology," the novice will find the initial difficulties remarkably cleared by reading the passages selected by Preller and Ritter as embodying the main substance of the doctrine.

Since all of this material will appear, extensively developed, in its proper place during the course of this complete translation of the Enneads now being published, it has been judged sufficient for the immediate purpose to translate these Preller-Ritter extracts in a somewhat informal way; they, too, serve merely as a guide.

The edition used is "*Historia Philosophiæ Græcæ et Romanæ ex Fontium Locis contexta. . . . H. Ritter and L. Preller. . . . Gotha, 1864.*" Where the compilers presented a severely corrupted text, the emendations of Volkmann have been adopted.

The notes following each of the fourteen main extracts and embodying other extracts are also the work of Preller and Ritter. In translation, some outbursts of pure academic display have been removed, as have some more substantial passages—these last where the translator, having started Plotinus' text earlier or carried it further than the compilers, found that their remarks or illustrations had become unnecessary. The compilers have occasionally jumped too quickly to the first apparent meaning of a passage without sufficiently studying its context—a common fault when citing from the Enneads—and the notes sometimes state very bluntly matters that require very subtle distinctions: the translator has sometimes decided to vary their phrasing to address such cases, and at other times has left the errors to be calmly corrected by the evidence the compilers themselves provide.

Whatever fault may be found with the compilers or with their hasty translator, it remains a fact that to know this Preller-Ritter Conspectus thoroughly and

## I
## THE FALL OF THE SOULS: THEIR RETURN

V. 1, 1. What could be the cause that has led souls to forget God, their Father, and—though they are members of Him and belong wholly to Him—to cease to know both themselves and Him?

The evil that has happened to them is due to a Rebellious Audacity <note>n</note>, to their entry into birth (or their desire to "become"), to the Primal Differentiation <note>n</note>, and to the desire of the souls to possess a life of their own.

They began to revel in free will <note>n</note>; they indulged their own movement; they took the wrong path and went far astray <note>n</note>. In this way, they lost the knowledge that they originated from that Divine Order (that they were members of the Triune). . . They no longer had a true vision of the Supreme or of themselves; they dishonored themselves by honoring the external in forgetfulness of their own lineage, admiring all other things rather than themselves. Smitten with longing for the lower realm, rapt in love for it, they grew to depend upon it; so they broke away, as far as was in their power, and came to disregard the lofty sphere they had abandoned. . . Nothing that so humbles itself to things that arise and perish—making itself smaller and less enduring than what it honors—can ever keep in mind the nature of God or His power.

Two appeals must be made to those who have fallen in this way if they are to be directed again toward the High and toward the Primals, and led up to the Supreme, to the One and First.

One method shows the soul the shame of the things which it now honors—we will treat this later—the second appeal . . . guides the first and conveys it with clear conviction: it is to teach the soul, or to remind it, of its lofty lineage and rank.

---

### NOTES

REBELLIOUS AUDACITY:—This word, <term>tolma</term>, is used elsewhere in the same sense—that is, to indicate the motive leading the soul, from the beginning, to desert its First Principle.

In <term>Theologumena Arithmetica</term>, we read, “The first Dyad separated itself from the Monad in what is called an act of rebellion or self-assertion, a <term>tolma</term>.”

<summary>Plotinus examines the causes of the soul's descent from the divine, citing self-assertion or "Rebellious Audacity" (tolma) as the primary reason for their loss of self-knowledge and divine connection. He suggests that souls can be led back to the Supreme by recognizing the shame of worldly attachments and remembering their high celestial origin.</summary>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 130, providing explanatory notes on the terminology used by Plotinus in his discussion on the soul's fall, specifically defining "Rebellious Audacity," "Primal Differentiation," and "Self-Will" before transitioning into a new section on the grandeur of the soul.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>131</page-num>
<header>GRANDEUR OF THE SOUL</header>

Plotinus in V. 2, 2, says that in plants there dwells "the more rebellious and self-assertive part (or phase) of the soul."

PRIMAL DIFFERENTIATION:—This refers to the Intellectual-Principle (the Divine Mind) which is said, in VI. 9, 5, "to separate itself from The One in an act of self-assertion."

SELF-WILL:—Plotinus upholds the freedom of the will but denies that free will can consist in the power to do things that are mutually contradictory. Thus, in VI. 8, 21: "the greatest power is in staying closest to Unity; to be able to perform contradictory acts is weakness; it is to be unable to hold to the Best."

He says that free will is shown in right action, not in acts done under the driving of the senses:—
> III. 1, 9. "Whenever the soul has been twisted from its own character by the force of the External and so acts—rushing in a blind excitement—the act or the state is not to be called an act or state of freedom. This is also true when, in a self-induced corruption, it responds to impulses within itself that are not entirely right or of its highest nature. Only when our soul acts by its native, pure, and independent Reason-Principle can the act be described as ours and as an exercise of free will."

Plotinus often affirms that the liberty or free will by which we pursue or accomplish evil is rather the very negation of freedom:—
> IV. 8, 5. "Everything that descends to a lower state descends against its own free will; but since it has followed an impulse of its own nature, it is said to pay the penalty, which is nothing other than the fall itself. But in the sense that such an act and experience was necessary from eternity by a law of nature, then one may say... that this thing, descending from what was above it to serve something else, was sent down by God."

On this doctrine of the soul's alienation from God and the necessity of returning through purification, see III. 6, 5. In a deeper sense, he denies that we are truly cut off from God; see Extract X on page 148.

## II

### THE GRANDEUR OF THE SOUL (HUMAN AND DIVINE)

V. 1, 2. Before all else, let every soul remember that it is itself the creator of every living thing, having breathed life into them: into all that the earth and the sea nourish; all that are in the air and all the divine stars in the heavens. It has formed the sun and this vast firmament of the sky; it has given them their stately

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 131, which discussed the grandeur of the soul and the nature of free will. It transitions from a discussion on the soul's descent into the material world to its role as the governing and life-giving principle of the entire cosmos.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>132</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

...ordering them and leading them in their ranks. It is a Nature distinct from all those things to which it gives order, movement, and life, and it must necessarily be more honorable than they are; for they are things whose existence had a beginning, and they perish when the Soul that leads the dance of life departs, but the Soul itself has eternal being since it cannot undergo change. . . . Just as rays from the sun pour light upon a dark cloud and make it shine in golden glory, so the Soul, when it comes to a body, touches it into life, brings immortality to it, and awakens it from where it lies prostrate. The heavenly system, taking up its eternal movement under the guidance of the Soul’s wisdom, becomes a blissful living being, made venerable by the Soul that dwells within. Before the Soul arrived, it was a dead body—or rather, the mere darkness of Matter, Non-Being, "hated by the gods."

What the Soul is, and what its power is, will be more clearly and splendidly evident if we consider how its counsel encompasses and directs the heavens, how it imparts itself to all this vast mass and gives it soul throughout its entire extent, through both large and small. Thus, every part of the great framework, though each has its own need and function and some are closely linked while others are far apart, every part has its own place in the Soul.

But the Soul itself is not divided in this way; it does not give life piece by piece, a fragment of Soul to a fragment of matter. Every fragment lives by the whole Soul, which is present everywhere, present both as a unit and as a Universal, just like the Father who created it.

And the heavens, diverse in content and in spatial location, become a Unity through the power and ability of the Soul; and through the Soul, this world is a God. The Sun, too, is a God, for it also possesses a soul; so too are the stars. And if we ourselves have any value, we achieve it through the Soul: "A corpse is nastier than dung." . . .

If it is the soul that gives worth, why does anyone ignore themselves and follow anything else? You revere the Soul elsewhere; then revere yourself.

->NOTE<-

(The compilers say, "This passage clearly refers to the World-Soul"; it does, but as they go on to point out, it also refers to the human Soul, which is one with the Divine All-Soul.)

The ninth treatise of the Fourth Ennead is entirely devoted to proving That All Souls are One Soul.

The main argument is that only bodies are separated by mass, place, and limit.

> IV. 9, 1. "Why should the Soul within myself be One and the Soul of the Whole not be One? Especially since in the divine there is no mass and no body?"

<summary>Plotinus describes the Soul as an eternal, indivisible force that gives life and unity to the universe, arguing that the individual soul and the World-Soul are fundamentally one. He emphasizes that the soul is the source of all value and divinity, urging individuals to recognize and revere the soul within themselves.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Soul, Enneads, Fourth Ennead, Matter, Non-Being, All-Soul, World-Soul, God, heavens, immortality, unity, divinity</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 132, which discussed the Soul's grandeur and its role as the life-giving principle of the cosmos. It moves from the general nature of the Soul to the specific philosophical paradox of how souls can be many and yet remain one.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>133</page-num>
<header>SOULS AS MANY AND ONE</header>

> VI. 4, 4. "The Souls are separate without being distinct; they are present to each other as one; they are no more separated by boundaries than are the many elements of a science in any one mind: the one Soul is of such a nature as to include all, for it knows nothing of limits."

Remark, however, that there is still a multiplicity of souls:—

> IV. 9, 2. "We do not declare the Soul to be one in the sense of entirely excluding Multiplicity: this absolute Unity belongs only to the prior Kind (The Transcendent): we make it both one and manifold: it has part in the Nature which is divided among bodies; but it has part also in the Indivisible, and so we find it One again."

Proof of the Soul's unity is provided by human sympathy and by the effectiveness of the magic arts:—

> IV. 9, 3. "We share each other's feelings; if we see another in distress, we suffer with them; we are irresistibly impelled to form friendships: incantations and other magical practices draw us together and call out a sympathetic response from afar: all this is a sign to us of the unity of the Souls."

How we are to understand the coexistence of Unity and Multiplicity in the Soul is shown in a nearby passage:—

> "The Indivisible Soul (the Unity of the Soul) is seated in the Intellectual-Principle, which is not divided among the bodily forms; the divisible Soul is seated within (or around) the bodies; it is essentially one in identity but, associated with different bodies, it brings about sensation and may be called another faculty or power of the Soul; so too with (the still lower) that faculty which has creative power and brings about the multiplication of bodily life. This manifoldness of faculty does not take away unity; a seed has the power of manifold production and yet is one thing, and out of this Unity springs the unity of its product."

Plotinus frequently uses the simile (as above) of the Unity of knowledge to illustrate the Unity of particular Souls in the All-Soul: thus—

> IV. 9, 5. "The particular Souls merge into one Soul which has given itself to form the Multiplicity and yet has kept its character: it is of a quality to remain one though it bestows itself upon all; its power reaches all at once; it is present in every particular Soul and is the same in them all: no one need hesitate at this doctrine if he will but think how a science, with all its detail, constitutes one whole: the whole remains a unity and yet is divisible into its parts."

<summary>This page explores Plotinus's concept of the Soul as both a unified whole and a multiplicity of individual souls, using analogies of science and seeds to explain this paradox. It points to human sympathy and magic as evidence of the underlying unity of all souls.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Enneads, Soul, Unity, Multiplicity, Intellectual-Principle, All-Soul, Transcendent, sympathy, magic arts</keywords>

<header>PLOTINUS</header>

### III

#### THE SOUL IN RELATION TO ITS PRIOR, TO THE INTELLECTUAL-PRINCIPLE

V. 1, 3. Since your Soul is such an exalted and divine power, be confident that by possessing it you are close to God. Begin, therefore, with the help of this Principle, to make your way to Him: you do not have far to go; there is not much in between. Lay hold of that which is more divine than this godlike thing; lay hold of that Summit of the Soul which borders on the Supreme from which the Soul directly derives—the Intellectual-Principle, of which the Soul (though we have shown it to be a glorious Principle) is merely an image.

For just as spoken thought is an image of the thought within the Soul, so the Soul is an image (or thought) of the Intellectual-Principle and is the entire activity by which the Intellectual-Principle sends out life to produce later forms of Being. Fire contains a warmth always within itself and a warmth which it sends out to do its work; similarly, the Divine-Mind both has its own inner Act and sends forth a creative force, the Soul.

We are to consider the Soul now in its loftier phase, not merely as an emanation, but as eternally a member of the Supreme, even though in part it also operates elsewhere.

Springing from the Intellectual-Principle <note>the second hypostasis, or Divine Mind</note> it is intellective, operating in the sphere of the Divine Reason. It draws its perfection from this superior Principle, which is like a nurturing father who has given it existence, though not a nature as perfect as his own.

The Soul’s substantial existence springs from the Divine Intellect, and its expression in its characteristic Act is achieved by virtue of its vision of this Divine Intellect. For as its vision penetrates into This, it possesses within itself, as its very own, what it sees as much as what it effects. Nothing can be called an Act of the Soul except what it does according to its intellective nature and, therefore, entirely in its own character. Everything lower than such an act has another origin and is merely an accidental experience.

The Soul becomes even more divine through the Intellectual-Principle because This is at once its Father and its ever-present companion. Nothing separates the Soul and the Divine-Mind except that they are not one and identical; the Soul is a successor and a recipient, while the other is the Divine thing received. What serves as Matter to the Intellectual-Principle must be noble; it is itself intellective and simplex <note>simple or uncompounded</note>.

In short, nothing more clearly shows the grandeur of Divine-Mind than that it is nobler than such a noble being as the Soul.

<summary>This page describes the Soul as a divine image and emanation of the higher Intellectual-Principle (Divine-Mind). It explains that the Soul derives its perfection and existence from its vision of this superior Principle, maintaining an eternal connection to the Supreme while acting as a creative force.</summary>
<keywords>Soul, Intellectual-Principle, Divine-Mind, Divine Reason, Divine Intellect, Being, emanation, substantial-existence, Plotinus, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<header>MATTER IN DIVINE MIND</header>

## Notes

"**SPRINGING FROM THE INTELLECTUAL-PRINCIPLE**":—Many metaphors are used to indicate how the universe rises from its principles—cast out like light from the sun, flowing forth like water from a well, branching out like a tree from the root. That from which the Emanation takes place remains always complete and undiminished:—

> III. 8, 9. "Imagine a spring which has no beginning, giving itself to all the rivers, never exhausted by what they take, always tranquilly its full self."

Such is the abundance of the First that it gives forth its Emanations without premeditation.

> V. 2, 1. "The One is not a Being but the source of Being, which is its first offspring. The One is perfect, meaning it has nothing, seeks nothing, and needs nothing, but, as we may say, it overflows, and this overflowing is creative: the produced entity . . . looks toward the One and becomes <term>The Intellectual-Principle</term>; resting within itself, this offspring of the One is Being."

No idea of time or of any motion or change may be admitted in reference to this Generative Act:—

> V. 1, 6. Far be it from our thoughts to imagine any generation in time when we treat the Ever-Existents. . . . Nor may we attribute action to the Generator. If there were action, this very action would have to be counted among the Divine Principles, and the produced <term>Hypostasis</term> (<term>The Intellectual-Principle</term>) would be a Third, not a Second. The First is immobile; any second must spring from it without any choice on its part, apart from its will, and without any movement in it. . . . And all things that have Being, as long as that Being belongs to them, must—by the virtue that is in them—give forth from their essence an underlying reality (<term>hypostasis</term>) belonging to them but going out to the exterior while still closely linked to them; an image, as it were, of this original and archetype. Thus, fire gives off its heat; snow does not keep its inner cold to itself; perfumes, too, may serve as an illustration; as long as they exist, they spread something of themselves abroad for the pleasure of all who are near.

All that has reached its perfection produces; The Eternally Perfect produces an eternal product, though a product of less perfection than itself.

"**MATTER TO THE INTELLECTUAL-PRINCIPLE**":—The Intelligible (or Intellectual) Matter in the Divine Mind is explained in—

> II. 4, 4. "If, then, the Divine Thought-Forms (The Ideas) are many, there must of necessity be something common to all and something unique to each to distinguish them: this particularity or specific difference is the individual shape; but if there is shape, there must be something that has taken the shape . . . that is to say, there is a foundation, a substrate, a Matter. Further, if there is an Intel-

<summary>This page explains the Neoplatonic concept of emanation, where "The One" overflows to create the Intellectual-Principle and subsequent realities without losing its own perfection. It also introduces the necessity of "Intelligible Matter" as a substrate for the diverse Thought-Forms within the Divine Mind.</summary>

lectual Kosmos of which our Kosmos is an image, and if ours is compound and includes Matter, there must be a Matter in the Intellectual Kosmos as well.

## IV

### THE INTELLECTUAL PRINCIPLE AND THE INTELLECTUAL-REALM IN RELATION TO THE ONE

V. I, 4. If anything more is needed to establish the magnificence of Divine Mind, look with awe at this sense-known Universe; consider its vastness, its beauty, and the harmony of its eternal course, the gods within it—both the seen gods and the unseen—the blessed spirits <note>n</note>, and all the life of animals and plants. Then ascend to its Original, to its more authentic form, and there contemplate the whole Intellectual Host, immortal by their own indwelling right, in the fullness of their own conscious life. See, presiding above these, the immaculate Divine Mind; consider that fathomless Wisdom, that true Saturnian Age—the age of Kronos, who is the Son of God and the Intellect of the Divinity—embracing all the immortal orders: all Intelligence, every god, and every soul, in His calm eternal identity. This Principle must be eternally identical, for what change or "otherness" could be sought there where all is well? To where could that Being move outside of itself, since it has everything within itself? What increase (and therefore, diversity) could the Most Perfect desire?

In Him, complete in all ways, all things are consummate; of all that He has, all is perfect; and of all that is within His being, all is perfect. Of all that is His, there is nothing that He does not know—knowing with a knowledge that is never sought but is always immediately present <note>n</note>. His blessedness comes from nothing outside but exists entirely in the one Eternity; it exists for the Divine Mind, which is itself the true Eternity, mimicked by that soul-circling movement of Time which is forever flinging aside the outworn and clutching at the new.

The Soul (the principle of movement and author of Time) is always occupied with consecutive things—always with some single object, now Socrates and now a horse—but Divine Mind knows all as one. Within this, all things are contained at rest in Unity; it alone has Authentic Existence, and its "I am" is forever. Nowhere is there any future for it; it is already all of its infinity. Nowhere is there any past, for nothing there can pass away; but as all is True Being there, so all holds steadfast always, with all things in the divine finding joy in their state. Every Being among all those that are there is Divine Mind and Authentic Existence; and their entirety constitutes the whole of Divine Mind and Authentic Existence. This Divine Mind, through its Act—its Intellection—becomes Existence; and Existence, because it is the object of that Intellection, brings both Intellection and Existence to the Divine Mind.

<vocab>Plotinus, Enneads, Divine-Mind, Intellectual Principle, Kronos, Authentic Existence, Intellection, Eternity, Kosmos</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the majesty and perfection of the Divine Mind (the Intellectual Principle), describing it as an eternal, unified realm of "Authentic Existence" that contains all beings and forms. It contrasts the Divine Mind’s instantaneous knowledge and eternal "now" with the Soul's sequential and time-bound experience of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Divine Mind, Intellectual Principle, Kronos, Authentic Existence, Eternity, Time, Soul, Intellection, Kosmos</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the exposition of Neoplatonic metaphysics, focusing on the relationship between the Intellectual-Principle, the primary Cause, and the nature of the Intellectual Realm, while clarifying the distinction between reasoning and immediate knowledge.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>137</page-num>
<header>REASONING AND INTUITION</header>

The primary Cause of the Intellectual-Principle is another Principle, which is also the Cause of Existence. This Cause, distinct from both, is common to them because they possess their substantial existence together, never leaving one another. Thus, while they are two, they still constitute a Unity. This Unity is simultaneously Intellection and Existence, Thinker and Thought—the Intellectual-Principle is the thinking Subject, while Existence is the object of the intellection <note>n</note>. For intellection could not happen if this Intellectual Kosmos did not contain both identity and difference at once.

Thus, the Primals are Intellection (the Intellectual-Principle), Existence, Differentiation, and Identity. To these, Motion and Rest <note>n</note> must also be added—Motion corresponds to the act of intellection, and Rest corresponds to the unbroken identity. Differentiation is implied by the existence of a Thinker and a Thought (an Intellectual-Principle and an object of intellection); if this difference did not exist, everything would be a silent Oneness.

There must also be a difference among the Divine Thoughts (the Ideas), yet they share an identity because the Intellectual-Principle is unified in itself, acting as a common principle for all. This Difference means the Ideas are distinct (that is, it is not a difference in their essential nature).

It is this multiplicity of difference within the Divine Realm itself that produces Number, Quantity, and even Quality, which is the specific character of these Principles—the sources of everything else that exists.

### NOTES

“**Blessed Spirits**”: These are the Daimones.

“**Knowledge immediately present**”: Inquiry implies an imperfect mind and therefore cannot be attributed to the Intellectual-Principle. The Soul, by itself, possesses only reasoning intelligence. Thus:

> V. I, 10. “Our Soul is a divine thing, outside the realm of the senses, as the nature of the Soul must be. It is complete when it has attached itself to the Intellectual-Principle, which is partly wrapped eternally in thought within itself, but is also partly occupied elsewhere, leading the Soul toward intellection.” “The higher part of the Soul,” Plotinus continues, “is always absorbed in the Divine Mind.”

So also:

> V. I, 11. “And if the Soul—though it must sometimes reason toward the Just and the Good—may sometimes know them immediately, there must be within us the Intellectual-Principle, which never seeks but eternally knows the Right.”

->T<-

It distinguishes between the Soul's reasoning intelligence and the Intellectual-Principle's immediate, eternal knowledge.</summary>

<header>PLOTINUS</header>

### All Reasoning belongs to the inferior order :—

> IV. 3, 18. "Does the Soul use reasoning before it enters the body and when it has left the body, or only while it is here? Only here, where it knows doubt and care and weakness; for Intelligence is less self-sufficient when it needs to reason, just as in the crafts or arts reasoning means hesitation in the workman, but when everything is clear the craft takes its own masterly way. In the Intellectual Order there is no <term>ratiocination</term> <note>logical reasoning or deduction</note> and the person who has arrived at this level does not employ it."

> V. 5, 1. "The <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> must always know and never overlook or forget; its knowing can never come by way of conjecture, never be doubtful, and never depend upon learning from outside or upon any proof. This <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> (even in humans) has nothing to do with reports from the senses; it contemplates nothing outside of itself."

## " THE INTELLECTIVE SUBJECT AND THE OBJECT OF INTELLECTION " :—

Authentic-Existence and The Divine Mind are customarily named together by Plotinus as one entity: to him there is no real being except what belongs to the intellectual and intelligible order:—

> V. 4, 2. "The <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> is itself the Intelligible—the object of True-Knowing—and at the same time the Intelligent—the True-Knower. <term>Real-Being</term> and the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> are identical, for the Divine Mind does not act upon its object as sensation acts upon material things, which must exist apart from the sensation: the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> actually is all the Existence upon which it exercises its Act."

So again:—

> V. 1, 10. "And in sequence following <term>The First</term> comes Existence, that is the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>."

This Existence (or Being) is also called Essence:—for example, in I. 7, 1 we read of "Intelligence and Essence" where the reference is to the Second Principle, the <term>Emanation</term> directly proceeding from the First, <term>The One</term>.

## " MOTION AND REST " :—

Since the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> is identical with its Act (<term>Intellection</term>) and with its object (Existence) it follows that Motion and Rest must be attributed to it: for "<term>Intellection</term> is the First Life" (III. 8, 7) and Life is identical with Act (expressive or functional activity) as we are told more than once. Compare VI. 2, 8. "Activity and movement accompany the <term>Intellection</term> in the Divine Mind: in its Self-<term>Intellection</term> is the foundation of Essence and Being: It exists while it thinks and it knows both itself and its objects as possessing Being . . . and Being is the absolutely unmoving, the very base in which all that is most motionless is founded."

<summary>This page explores Plotinus's view that discursive reasoning is a sign of weakness used only in the physical realm, whereas the Intellectual-Principle possesses immediate, self-contained knowledge. It emphasizes the identity between the Knower and the Known within the Divine Mind and explains how Motion and Rest coexist within this supreme state of being.</summary>

<keywords>Plotinus, Intellectual-Principle, Intellection, Authentic-Existence, Divine Mind, Essence, Emanation, The One, ratiocination, Real-Being, Motion and Rest</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Plotinus’s metaphysics, distinguishing between the static, contemplative nature of the Intellectual-Principle and the active, outward-moving nature of the Soul.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>ACT IN THE SUPREME AND IN MAN</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

Often, however, all motion is restricted to the <term>Soul</term>, with rest being designated as the characteristic of the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>:—

II. 9, 1. “The <term>Divine Mind</term> remains true to its own Being, always in the same state, unmoving in its stable Act: all movement around It, as well as toward It, begins with the <term>Soul</term>.”

IV. 4, 16. “Making <term>The Good</term> the center, the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> will be a motionless circle and the <term>Soul</term> a circle in motion, moved by its inward aspiration: for the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> contains <term>The Good</term> immediately, while the <term>Soul</term> must strive toward it.

The <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> rests always in Itself, contemplates nothing else (except The First), never moves elsewhere, and is therefore not an agent (*praktikos* <note>active or practical in a worldly sense</note>).”

V. 3, 6. “This principle is not an agent under the necessity of acquiring knowledge of the external, as a man must, for instance, when he goes outside of himself into the business of life.”

The <term>Soul</term> does not possess this <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> by its own right and nature; rather, the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> only takes possession if the <term>Soul</term> turns toward It:—

V. 6, 4. “The <term>Soul</term> possesses the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> as something added to it: <term>Intellection</term> belongs essentially to the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>.”

The <term>Soul</term>, therefore, performs external action when it builds the <term>Kosmos</term>:—

IV. 8, 3. “The <term>Intellective Soul</term> has <term>Intellection</term> for its Act, but not pure <term>Intellection</term>; if it did, it would be identical with its prior <note>that which precedes it in the divine hierarchy</note>. To its nature as an <term>Intellective</term> being, it adds another Act which constitutes its characteristic ‘person’ (<term>hypostasis</term>) and so it becomes something distinct from the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>. The <term>Soul</term> also has its function, as every member of the Divine must; looking toward its prior, it has <term>Intellection</term>; looking toward itself, it preserves its own essence; looking toward what follows it—toward what it orders and conducts—it rules the lower world.”

Still, upon deeper examination, it is discovered that all action serves thought and is performed for the purpose of contemplation:—

III. 8, 5. “All action is for the sake of contemplation . . . what we do not have the will-power to obtain by the direct way, we seek by a roundabout path. When we acquire what our action sought and achieve what we proposed, what is it for? It is not meant to be ignored, but to be known and seen: we act for the sake of some satisfaction we desire, and this not so that it may remain outside the limits of our possessions, but so that it may be ours. This means that it must be . . . where? Where else but in the mind? Thus, every act circles back to thought; for what the act stores up in the soul, which is a <term>Reason-Principle</term>, can be nothing else than a <term>Reason-Form</term>, a silent thought (a thought not spoiled by the noise of action).”

<summary>Plotinus defines the Intellectual-Principle as a state of eternal, unmoving rest focused on The Good, whereas the Soul is the source of movement and external action in the world. However, he argues that all action ultimately serves the purpose of returning the soul to internal contemplation and mental possession of truth.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Intellectual-Principle, Soul, Divine Mind, The Good, contemplation, action, hypostasis, Reason-Principle, Kosmos</keywords>

<page-num>140</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

Even the very <term>Kosmos</term>, shaped or created by the <term>Soul</term>, is itself a “<term>Contemplation</term>” (or, as we might say, a <term>Mentation</term>, an intellectual activity).

> III. 8, 4. “Treating of Nature, we said that all generating was a <term>Contemplation</term>: we come now to the <term>Soul</term>, which is prior to Nature, and say that by the <term>Soul</term>’s <term>Contemplation</term>—its zest for knowledge, its desire for experience, the birth pangs induced in it by what it knows, its teeming plenty—the <term>Soul</term>, itself a complete <term>Contemplation</term>, has produced another <term>Contemplation</term> . . . one weaker than itself.”

---

# V

## THE BEGINNING OF MULTIPLICITY

V. 1, 5. This God (The <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>), already a Being of <term>Multiplicity</term>, is present in the <term>soul</term>. . . . But what is the God that has thus produced offspring; what is the <term>Simplex</term>, existing before all such <term>Multiplicity</term>, the source at once of its existence and of its <term>Multiplicity</term>, the source of Number itself? For Number is not primal: before the Two, there is <term>The One</term> and the Unit must precede the <term>Dyad</term>: coming later than <term>The One</term>, the <term>Dyad</term> has <term>The One</term> as the standard of its differentiation—that without which it could not be the separate, differentiated thing it is. And as soon as there is differentiation, number exists.

---

## NOTES

The need for a Principle above the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> is established by the consideration that <term>Divine Mind</term> is, and contains, a <term>Multiplicity</term>: it is both <term>Intellection</term> and Existence and includes the <term>Multiplicity</term> of the Divine Thoughts, the <term>Ideas</term>; its <term>Multiplicity</term> demands a Unity to include or contain it.

> III. 8, 8. “The <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>, thus, contains the Multiple: therefore it is not the First: there must be a Principle transcending it. . . . <term>Multiplicity</term> is subsequent to Unity and the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> is number: the Source of this number is <term>The One</term>: <term>Divine Mind</term>, as being both the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> and the <term>Intellectual-Kosmos</term>, is twofold: as long as we have duality, we must go still higher until we reach what transcends the <term>Dyad</term>.”

From this it follows that nothing can be attributed to <term>The One</term> except what is purely Itself: if It possessed or included anything other than Itself, <term>The One</term> would be, again, a <term>Multiplicity</term>:—

> V. 4, 1. “Standing transcendent above all things that follow It, existing in Itself, not mixing or to be mixed with any emanation from Itself, veritably <term>The One</term>, not merely possessing Oneness as an attribute of Its essence—for that would

<summary>This page explains that the Kosmos and Soul are products of contemplation and argues that the Intellectual-Principle, because it contains multiplicity, must originate from a prior, absolute unity called The One.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, The One, Intellectual-Principle, Multiplicity, Soul, Kosmos, Contemplation, Dyad, Simplex, Divine Mind</keywords>

<page-num>141</page-num>
<header>THE ALL-TRANSCENDING UNITY 141</header>

> be a false Oneness—a Principle surpassing all reasoning, all knowing . . . a Principle standing over all Essence and Existence . . . only when it is *simple* and *First*, apart from all, can It be perfectly self-sufficing : a *non-primal* needs its prior and a *non-simple* demands the simple which is its source.”

VI. 9, 6. > “ All that is multiple, and not One, is needy ; made up of many elements, it craves the Unity ; the Unity itself cannot crave the Unity which it is.”

Another consideration is that unless there were a Unity there could be nothing else : things exist by virtue of a Unity :—

VI. 9, 1. > “ All beings exist by the One—and this whether their being is primal or merely partial. . . . Take away their Unity and they lose their Being.” Plotinus goes on to instance an army, a choir, a herd, a house, a ship, planets, animals, a man : a principle of Unity makes them what they are, and this principle in them is an image, a distant reflection, of the Unity that is the essence of the all-transcending One.”

Still, in a certain mode, Multiplicity may be affirmed of The One—in that it possesses infinite power :—

VI. 9, 6. > “ The One must be taken to be infinite not in the sense of some mass or number never to be measured or traversed, but in the sense of inconceivable power.” And see, later, on Extract VII, page 144.

---

## VI
### THE ABSOLUTE TRANSCENDENCE OF THE ONE

VI. 9, 3. Since the Nature or Hypostasis of The One is the producer of the All, It can Itself be none of the things in the All ; that is, It is not a thing ; it does not possess quality or quantity ; It is not an Intellectual-Principle, not a Soul ; It is not in motion and not at rest ; not in space, not in time ; It is essentially of a unique form or rather of no-form, since it is prior to form as It is prior to movement and to rest : all these categories hold only in the realm of Existence and constitute the Multiplicity characteristic of that lower realm.

### NOTE

THE One can be indicated only through negations :—

VI. 8, 11. > “ How can we make such a statement about It, seeing that all else we say of it is said by negation ? ”

No attribute can be affirmed of It : we penetrate to It only by mystic contemplation, with the senses sealed :—

See I. 6, 8. Page 88 of this work.

<vocab>Plotinus, Neoplatonism, The One, Unity, Hypostasis, Intellectual-Principle, Soul, Existence, Multiplicity, Transcendence, negations, mystic contemplation</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that "The One" is the absolute, self-sufficing source of all existence, defined by its simplicity and infinite power rather than by measurement or mass. It concludes that because The One is prior to all forms and categories, it can only be described through negations and reached through mystical contemplation.</summary>

<keywords>The One, Unity, Multiplicity, Plotinus, Neoplatonism, Transcendence, Hypostasis, Intellectual-Principle, Negation, Mystic Contemplation</keywords>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of The One's absolute transcendence, specifically focusing on its lack of self-consciousness, will, and the limitations of naming it.</meta>

<page-num>142</page-num>

<header>PLOTINUS</header>

The One is without thought but also without ignorance:—

> VI. 9, 6. “The fact that It neither knows nor has Intellection of Itself does not constitute any ignorance in It. Ignorance implies something outside the ignorer . . . but what stands absolutely alone neither knows anything nor has anything to ignore; being one and always present to itself, it has no need of self-knowing; in fact, even that self-presence ought not to be attributed to It if we are to preserve Its unity; we must rule out all knowing and all consciousness, whether of Itself or of anything else; we must conceive It not as having Intellection but as being the object of Intellection <note>the object of the Divine Mind's knowing</note>.

Hence it follows that The One is not intelligible in Itself but only to the Divine Intellectual-Principle:—

> V. 6, 2. “In regard to the Intellectual-Principle, The One will be Intelligible, an object of true knowing, but within Itself It will strictly neither possess Intellection nor be the object of Intellection.”

The One does not even possess will; if Plotinus, after Plato, names It The Good, even this must be understood in a modified sense:—

> VI. 9, 6. “All that can be said to lack or desire, lacks or desires the Good that will complete it: The One, therefore, can experience no Good nor any will toward Good; It is the Beyond-Good, or it is good, not in regard to Itself, but in regard to the lower things that are capable of partaking in it.”

Similarly, if It is called the Source and the Cause, this is not a definition of The One as It is in Itself but the statement of a relation in which the lower stands toward It:—

> VI. 8, 8. “All things, however exalted and noble, are later than This: It is the source of all, though in some sense It is no source: we must keep all things apart from It . . . even freedom of action. . . It can enter into no relation with the realm of Existence.”

> VI. 9, 3. “When we call it a Cause, we are not making an assertion about It but about ourselves; we speak of what we derive from It while It remains steadfastly within Itself.”

Plotinus is insistent that this name, The One, is a poor substitute for indicating a Nature which can never be expressed, and of which no knowledge is possible:—

> VI. 9, 5. “This Wonder, this One, to which in truth no name may be given . . . but since we must discuss It, we may name It this way, provided we bear in mind the special sense and guard against confusing It with any form that may be suggested by the numerical designation.”

> V. 3, 13. “Hence It cannot be truly designated; any name used makes It some specific thing; but That which is above all things—above that most noble of

<summary>Plotinus argues that The One is beyond thought, will, and language, explaining that attributes like "Good" or "Cause" only describe our relationship to it. Because It is perfectly unified, It lacks the subject-object distinction required for self-knowledge or Intellection.</summary>

<keywords>Plotinus, The One, transcendence, Intellection, Intellectual-Principle, The Good, Divine Mind, causality, apophatic theology</keywords>

<page-num>143</page-num>
<header>THE SUPREME TRIAD</header>

Existents, the Intellectual-Principle—This alone of all is authentic; It is no thing among things; It is nameless, for It falls under no class; we can attempt no more than to use words which will in some helpful way indicate It for the purposes of discussion.”

VI. 8, 8. “Language fails even for the adequate discussion of the Transcendent, and much more for defining it.”

# VII

## WHY THE SUPREME IS A TRIAD

II. 9, 1. . . . It follows that we need to have recourse to no other Principles than these Three: we have first The One, then, following upon The One, the Divine Mind and the Primal Intellectual-Principle; after this, the Soul. This is the order ruling in the nature of things, and we may not assign either fewer or more Principles than these to the Supreme. If we affirm fewer than Three, we must bring together either Soul and the Intellectual-Principle or the Intellectual-Principle and The First: but we have abundantly shown that these are separate. It remains, then, to consider whether there can be more than Three.

Now what Divine <term>Hypostasis</term> could exist outside of these Three?

The First Principle of the All, as we have indicated it, is at once the most simple and the most exaltedly transcendent that can be discovered: it is unsound to double the Persons by distinguishing between potentiality and act in an immaterial Existence whose Act is its <term>Essence</term>.

Similarly, we cannot propose two Intellectual-Principles, one at rest and one in movement. . . . The Divine Mind has its eternal, immobile Act toward The One; the Reason which descends from It to the Soul and makes the Soul intellective cannot constitute a distinct <term>Hypostasis</term>. . . . Nor may we conceive of two Divine Minds, one knowing and the other knowing that It knows; thinking may be distinguished, no doubt, from thinking that one is thinking; but in the entire process, there is really only one consciousness aware of its activity; it would be absurd to suppose that the Authentic Intelligence could ever be so unconscious of its act. . . . No: the Intellectual-Principle has <term>Intellection</term> of Itself; and the Intellectual and Intelligible <term>Kosmos</term> which is its Thought is also Itself: therefore, the Intellectual-Principle in Its <term>Intellection</term> has self-consciousness; It necessarily knows Itself. (Plotinus urges, further, that if we are to suppose, as certain <term>Gnostics</term> do, a second Intellectual-Principle conscious of the action of an unconscious first, we open the list of an unending series of Divine Minds.)

<vocab><term>Intellectual-Principle</term>, <term>The One</term>, <term>Divine Mind</term>, <term>Soul</term>, <term>Supreme</term>, <term>Hypostasis</term>, <term>Essence</term>, <term>Intellection</term>, <term>Kosmos</term>, <term>Gnostics</term>, <term>Plotinus</term></vocab>

<summary>Plotinus establishes the Neoplatonic triad of The One, the Intellectual-Principle, and the Soul, arguing that these three principles are sufficient and distinct. He refutes the Gnostic notion of multiple intellectual principles, asserting that the Divine Mind must be a single, self-conscious unity.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, The One, Intellectual-Principle, Divine Mind, Soul, Triad, Hypostasis, Intellection, Gnostics, Metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument against unnecessary subdivisions within the divine hierarchy, specifically refuting the idea of an intermediate Intellectual-Principle between the Divine Mind and the Soul, and defining the nature of the Three Hypostases.</meta>

<page-num>144</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

So, too, the supposition that a first Intellectual-Principle generates in the Soul-of-the-All a second Intellectual-Principle intermediate between Divine Mind and Soul, takes away from the Soul its intellective nature (n) . . . on this hypothesis, the Soul would possess not the Divine Intellectual-Principle but merely an image of it. . . . We can, therefore, admit only these Three <term>Hypostases</term> in the Supreme: one Transcendent; one Intellectual-Principle, self-identical, unswerving ever, imitating the Father as nearly as possible (n); then Soul, with the reservation that the Soul in us, while in part always dwelling with the Intellectual Existences, has in part fallen to the realm of sense and in part again occupies an intermediate region.”

### NOTES

“**THE SOUL’S INTELLECTIVE NATURE**”: This refers to the Plotinian doctrine that the Soul is a <term>Logos</term>, a Reason-Principle or Idea or Thought of the Divine Mind:

> III. 2, 2. “For what emanates from the Intellectual-Principle is a Reason-Principle, a <term>Logos</term>.”

> III. 6, 18. “The Soul, itself a Divine Thought and possessing the Divine Thoughts, or Ideas, of all things, contains all things concentrated within it.”

If the Soul is one of many Ideas in the Intellectual-Principle, it is hard to see why there should be only one All-Soul; but Plotinus is concerned with opposing not so much the “superfluous multiplication” of lower forms of Being as the multiplication of grades in the Intellectual Realm; the ninth tractate of the Second Ennead, “Against the Gnostics,” contains an elaborate refutation of such needless subdivision of the Divine.

“**ONE INTELLECTUAL-PRINCIPLE**”: The one Divine Intellectual-Principle is, as we have seen, Intelligent no less than Intelligible (it is at once the subject and object of True-Knowing). The Ideas which it contains are understood, in Philo’s sense, as Intellectual Powers:

> V. 9, 8. “No Idea is anything other than the Intellectual-Principle: each is the Intellectual-Principle; and the entire Intellectual-Principle is the entirety of the Ideas . . . as a science entire is the entirety of the truths it sums.”

> IV. 8, 3. “Every Intelligence dwells in that Place of Intellection, and there, too, dwell the Intellectual-Forces, the Ideas, with all particular Intelligences; for Divine Mind is not a pure Unity but a Unity in Multiplicity.”

Hence it is that there comes to exist in the Divine a <term>Kosmos</term> which contains all the Ideas of things existing in the world of sense: in that Divine Realm, the Divine Thoughts, which are imperfectly manifested below, are consummately beautiful, perfect, and truly one:—

<vocab>Plotinus, Hypostases, Intellective-Nature, Logos, All-Soul, Ennead, Against the Gnostics, Kosmos, Intellectual-Principle</vocab>

<summary>Plotinus argues for a strict hierarchy of Three Hypostases—The One, Intellectual-Principle, and Soul—rejecting Gnostic attempts to add intermediate levels. He defines the Soul as a Reason-Principle (Logos) and the Intellectual-Principle as a unity containing all Ideas.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Enneads, Three Hypostases, Soul, Intellectual-Principle, Logos, Gnosticism, Divine Mind, Ideas, Philo</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Three Hypostases, shifting from the refutation of Gnostic subdivisions to the specific roles and mediation between The One, the Intellectual-Principle, the Soul, and the physical universe.</meta>

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>MEDIATION BETWEEN MAN AND THE SUPREME</header>

> V. 9, 9. “This sense-perceived universe is a living being including the totality of life, but it derives its existence and the specific mode of its existence from a Power (the Soul) which is ever being led back toward the Divine Mind from which it emerged: therefore the entire model of this universe must be in that <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> which must be, thus, a Cosmos, an ordered collectivity.”

The Three Supreme Principles are most closely linked; each of the lower derives from its predecessor and the entire lower universe derives from The First, but through the mediation of the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> and the Soul.

The <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> has its <term>intellection</term> by virtue of the self-contemplation of The One:—

> V. 1, 7. “We say the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> is the image of The One. . . . But The One is not an <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>; how then does it generate an <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>? The answer is that The One has Vision and this very Vision actually is the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>.”

The Soul is twofold in Act; standing between Divine Mind and Nature, it looks to both, as is indicated in the quotation from IV. 8, 3, page 139.

So, too:—

> IV. 8, 5. “For every soul has something of the lower for the purposes of the body and of the higher for the purposes of the Divine Mind.”

All existents are brought back, by these channels of mediation, to the First Principle. See I. 7, 2, page 90.

> IV. 3, 12. “The <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> entire rests ever Above but sends down to the sense-known world through the Soul which in turn gives out to its own next.”

> IV. 5, 9. “All through the scheme of things, lower things are included under what is one degree less low, and higher things under the higher yet; one thing under another, until the First is reached; this First, having nothing before It, can be surpassed by nothing . . . it must therefore surpass all.”

Thus all follow the same line, up and down, and for their ultimate Principle all depend upon The One, which for this reason alone is called The Good:—

> I. 7, 1. “The Absolute Good must be described as That to Which all things aspire and It to none.”

Compare I. 8, 2, page 93.

As to the mode in which The One or The Good is present to all and all exist and have their essential being in It, this is conveyed, after Plotinus’ manner, by many images and metaphors. One of these illustrations makes it, so to speak, a universal Life:—

<summary>This page describes the hierarchical relationship and mediation between the Three Supreme Principles, explaining how the physical universe derives its life from The One through the Intellectual-Principle and the Soul. It emphasizes that all existence is linked in a descending order from the First Principle, to which all things ultimately aspire.</summary>

<header>PLOTINUS</header>

> VI. 5, 12. “It is present as one Life: in a living organism, life is not located at a single point... it is diffused throughout the entire frame. If this seems impossible, remember that the Divine Energy knows no bounds of quantity; divide it mentally forever and It remains still the same. It is fundamentally infinite; It has no contact with Matter, so It does not vary according to the size of the object upon which It acts.”

---

### VIII

## THE SOUL AND THE WORLD OF NATURE

> IV. 4, 13. How does this Wisdom <note>Wisdom here refers to a higher state of the Soul.</note> differ from what we know as Nature?
> The distinction is that Wisdom is an earlier, more divine form of the Soul; for Nature, too, is an image of the Divine Wisdom, but, as the final emanation of the Soul, it possesses only the lowest degree of the Reason illuminating it... Nature, then, does not have consciousness <note>Nature acts instinctively rather than through conscious thought.</note>: it has merely productivity, which consists of its transmitting—without choice or knowledge—the Form it has received from the Soul to that which follows it: the various kinds of bodies and Matter.

> III. 2, 2. From that Divine Cosmos, authentic and One, this lower Cosmos derives its existence: it is not a true Unity; it is manifold, subdivided into multiplicity, with thing standing apart from thing in spatial differentiation. Discord takes the place of harmony; where everything is something less than perfect, item clashes with item; no part is self-sufficient; each, to complete its own function, demands the aid of another, and so there is general strife.
> This lower Cosmos was produced not because the Divinity saw a need for it, but from the sheer necessity for a secondary or derivative kind of existence, since it was not in the constitution of existence that The Divine should be the last and lowest of things.
> The Divine is The First; It also possessed a multiform power—an all-power—fitted to produce other forms of being. But Its action could not be the result of seeking and planning; if there were planning, It would not possess the Cosmos as something entirely Its own, something emanating from Its own Essence. It would be like a craftsman in whom creation is not an inborn personal power but an acquired skill, like a learned trade. But the Universe is the work of the undisturbed, unmoved Divine Mind giving something from Itself to Matter <note>Matter is the receptive substrate for the Divine gift of Form.</note>: this Gift is the <term>Reason-Principle</term> which flows from It.

<summary>This page explains that the Divine is an infinite, indivisible life force that permeates the universe through the Soul and its lower manifestation, Nature. The physical world exists as a necessary but imperfect emanation of the Divine Essence, governed by the Reason-Principle rather than conscious planning.</summary>

<keywords>Plotinus, Wisdom, Nature, Soul, Divine Wisdom, emanation, Reason, Cosmos, Matter, Divine Mind, Reason-Principle, Infinite Energy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Plotinus’s philosophy, specifically providing explanatory notes on the hierarchy of the Soul and its relationship to the Intellectual-Principle and the material world.</meta>

<page-num>147</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AS IN NATURE</header>

### NOTES

"THIS WISDOM" is that Soul which borders on the Intellectual-Principle—that is, the superior Soul above the one that deals directly with Nature and with bodily forms:—

> III. 8, 4. "What we call Nature is a Soul, the offspring of a prior Soul, of a Soul living with greater power."

This prior Soul is compared elsewhere to the Celestial Aphrodite, while the inferior Soul—the one that is Nature—is compared to the Earthly Aphrodite. This inferior or later soul is described as bringing Nature into being through the process by which Contemplation or Mental Purposing passes into action.

In other places, sensation in animals and the principle of growth in plants are said to be produced by an outward flowing or procession of the Soul:—

> V. 2, 1. "The Soul, as it looks toward the Divine order, is perfect; by going outside of itself into a movement foreign to its essence, it produces an image which is sensitive and vegetative Nature."

Speaking somewhat loosely, Plotinus says in IV. 9, 4 that the multiplicity of Souls all proceed from the one Soul-of-the-All; in IV. 8, 4, we find the concept, truer to his system, that they are, instead, a multiplicity within the one Soul.

"SOMETHING OF ITSELF TO MATTER":—VI. 4, 16. "This is not a coming-down as if into a physical place; the Soul’s descent consists in its being associated with a body. When we say that the Soul is in the body, we mean that it communicates something of its own to the body."

The Soul "entering" the multiplicity of the sense-known world suffers, as it were, a loss of its unity—a lower part of it visiting the lower sphere:—

> II. 9, 2. "The less exalted part of the Soul is dragged down . . . the eternal law could never allow it to descend in its entirety."

In joining the body, it loses something of its liberty; for in all that it now does, even though its life may be lived mainly in Reason, there is a certain admixture:—

> VI. 8, 2. "All that involves action, even where Reason rules, is of a mixed quality, and complete freedom cannot exist."

<vocab>Plotinus, Enneads, Soul, Nature, Intellectual-Principle, Aphrodite, Contemplation, Soul-of-the-All, Descent, Reason</vocab>

<summary>This page provides commentary on Plotinus’s distinction between the higher Soul and the lower Soul (Nature), explaining how the Soul’s descent into matter results in a loss of unity and freedom. It highlights that Nature acts as an unconscious offspring of a more powerful, prior Soul.</summary>

<header>PLOTINUS</header>

# IX
## MATTER

I. 8, 7. “But why does the existence . . . necessity of Evil” (pages 100-101).

### NOTE

Plotinus goes much further than Plato in the low rank he assigns to Matter. In the *Enneads*, it is the absolute boundary of the Truly-Existent and of The Good; it is that which possesses nothing of reality or of value. Sometimes, however, like Plato, Plotinus describes Matter as “The Other-than-Being”: cf. I. 8, 3, and I. 8, 5, pages 94, 97 of this volume.

> III. 6, 7. “Matter is without body; body is of an earlier origin (less distant from the Divine) and merely includes Matter . . . Matter is neither a spirit nor a mind; it is not life, it is not a <term>Reason-Form</term> or Idea, and it is not a limit; it might be more accurately described as a boundlessness. It is not a power or potentiality; it produces nothing, and since it is none of the things belonging to this sphere, it cannot be called an Existent. It is, rather, Not-Being; and it is not even this in the sense in which Motion and Rest may be called Not-Being. It is merely a phantasm or shadow of space, an aspiration toward existence; it is present where no one sees; it always eludes the eye that searches for it . . . it contains all opposites: the small and the great, the more and the less . . . it is a ghostly thing, incapable of either staying or going since it has drawn no force from the Divine: and, so, all its pretense of existence is a lie.”

From Plotinus’s doctrine, it would follow that wherever there is Non-Being or a defect of Being, there must be some presence of Matter; that would seem to be the case even in the Soul, and even in the Divine Mind; but Plotinus will not allow Matter there (?).

---

# X
## SOUL AND BODY

> IV. 3, 9. The entry of a Soul into a body may take place in one of two ways. In one case, it has already been in an earthly body and changes it for another, or having been in a body of fire or air (an “astral” body), it enters an earthly body for the first time. . . . In another case, it has previously been outside of any body but chooses one now and so enters into relation with the material universe for the first time. At present, we are to deal only with this second case. . . . We begin with the <term>Soul-of-the-All</term>. . . . We must use such phrases as “entry of the Soul”

<summary>This page defines Matter in Plotinian philosophy as a ghostly "Not-Being" and a boundary of existence, while also introducing the two methods by which a Soul enters a physical body.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Enneads, Matter, Not-Being, Soul, incarnation, astral body, Soul-of-the-All, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Plotinus's philosophy regarding the relationship between the Soul and Matter, specifically explaining how the Soul animates the physical world and why Body and Matter are necessary for its expression.</meta>

<page-num>149</page-num>
<header>SOUL AND BODY</header>

and "ensouling the world," though there never was a time when this All was without Soul, never a time when the frame of the universe held together in the absence of Soul, never a time when Matter was crude and unordered (n). We separate them—Soul and Body, Form and Matter—only to be able to discuss them clearly; there is no combination which the reasoning faculties may not resolve into its elements.

If Body, the body-kind, had not existed, the Soul could never have gone forth from itself, for there exists no other place to which its nature would allow it to resort. If it is to go forth from itself, it must provide a suitable place; it must shape itself a body.

Now the Soul (as a Divine Hypostasis) is motionless, with an immobility rooted in immobility itself (the immobility which is one of the Categories of the world of Authentic-Existence), but it may be thought of as a powerful light shining forth afar; at the furthest reach of its fires there must be darkness (n): once this darkness exists, the Soul must see it, and, by seeing it, give it form, for the Law could not allow anything that is near to Soul to be without some share in the Divine Idea. . . .

The Kosmos, the ordered and patterned system thus produced, becomes like a stately and varied mansion not disowned by its architect though not identical with him; it is judged worthy in every inch of all its builder's care in adding beauty to its being, as far as existence is possible to Matter and without detriment to the Maker who presides over it from the eternal seat Above (n).

Thus is the All ensouled, with a spirit not its own but communicated to it: governed by Soul, not governing it; not so much possessing as possessed by Soul. For the Universe lies within this maintaining Spirit and no part of it is wholly void of Soul (n): it may be compared to a net that takes all its life from being wet in the waters and still is never able to move of its own motion there, but as the sea tosses it the net is spread out, exactly to the full of its reach, no mesh of it able to push beyond its own set place.

The Soul, outside all the limits of space and quantity, is able to embrace within its unvarying force the entire body of the All, and is ever at the furthest and the nearest point which the All includes (n). The Universe spreads as broad and wide as the presence of the Soul, and it stretches as far as the outflow of life from the Soul proceeds.

### NOTES

<note>"NEVER A TIME." Plotinus makes the world eternal on the grounds that there could never be a time at which the Eternal Principles were unproductive.</note>

<note>"DARKNESS":—Compare I. 8, 4, and I. 8, 5, pages 96–7 of this volume.</note>

<vocab>Soul, Body, Form, Matter, Divine Hypostasis, Authentic-Existence, Kosmos, Plotinus, Eternal Principles, All, Universe</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the eternal coexistence of Soul and the Universe, explaining that the Soul shapes Matter into a body to express its nature. The universe is described as being contained within and animated by the Soul, much like a net is spread and sustained by the sea.</summary>
<keywords>Soul, Matter, Divine Hypostasis, Kosmos, Plotinus, Neoplatonism, Enneads, Body-Soul relationship</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Plotinus's philosophy regarding the Soul's relationship with the physical world, moving from the mechanism of creation to the Soul's constant presence and the beauty of the Kosmos.</meta>

<page-num>150</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

"**PRESIDES OVER IT**":—This refers to the prior Soul. In other places Plotinus utterly denies a fall of the Soul:—

II. 9, 4. 
> "Is it to be thought that creation comes about because the Soul has lost its wings? Such a catastrophe cannot be conceived of the Divine All-Soul. We hear of its Fall: but, why and how, and when? If it fell from all eternity, then it is eternally a fallen thing; if we fix a time, why not earlier or later? We hold that the Kosmos was produced by no such fall: the creation, rather, came about by the Soul’s not falling. If the Soul fell, that could be only by its forgetting the things of the Supreme; but if it forgets that Sphere, how could it create this? From what model does it work but from what it sees There? If then it creates from the vision of the Divine Realities, it can never have fallen.”

"**ADDING BEAUTY**":—"All things that exist in the Universe have Soul and vital force and are images of the eternal life in the Divine and Intellectual Kosmos. The Universe is consummate in beauty and only the foolish could revile it:—

II. 9, 4. 
> "Nor can we admit that this universe is ill-constructed because of the many flaws that may be found in it: such a complaint would rank it high indeed—as if it were the Intellectual Universe itself and not merely an image of that Divine Sphere.”

The very evil in the universe contributes toward the good: the Providence of God nowhere shines more brightly than in His power to turn evil to His purposes:—

III. 2, 5. 
> "Vice itself is not without its usefulness to the All; it exhibits the beauty and the rightness of virtue; it calls up the intelligence to oppose the evil course; it manifests the value and grace there is in goodness by displaying the cost of sin. No doubt evil has not essentially anything to do with these purposes, but once it is there it serves in working out great ends; and only a mighty power could thus turn the ignoble to noble uses and employ for the purposes of form what has risen in formless lawlessness.”

"**THE FURTHEST AND NEAREST POINT**":—For the world is a living being penetrated with life, and all its members are working together toward one end:—

IV. 4, 32: 
> "We must look upon this Universe with all the lives within it as one living being having for all its parts one soul which reaches to every member, to every object existing in the sense-known scheme. . . . This world, by virtue of its unity, is linked in fellow-feeling; it is like one animal and its furthest extremities are near and share their experiences.”

IV. 4, 36. 
> "The universe is very varied: all the Reason-Principles (Divine Thought-Forms) meet in it, infinitely diverse powers. . . . It is a being awake and

<summary>Plotinus argues that the Soul did not "fall" to create the world but shaped it through a vision of Divine Realities, using even evil and vice to serve the greater good. He describes the Universe as a unified, beautiful living being where all parts are interconnected by a single soul.</summary>
