<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Soul’s presence in the world, focusing on the internal life of all things and how prayer and magic function through universal sympathy.</meta>

<page-num>151</page-num>
## PRAYER AND MAGIC

...alive at every point. . . . Each thing has its own unique life in the All, though we, because our senses do not perceive the activity going on inside wood and stone, deny that life. . . . Their life is secret, but they live: everything that lives according to our perception is composed of things that live imperceptibly and give to the visibly living the powers which are manifested in life. Man could not rise to his great height if his activity were determined by utterly soulless powers; nor, again, could the All be of so exalted a life unless everything in it had a life of its own; choice perhaps does not belong to these invisible lives, but their activity has no need of choice; they are of earlier origin than choice <note>that is, they act by an "instinct" closer to the Divine intuition</note> and therefore have far-reaching efficiency.”

Plotinus’s theory of the sacredness of temples and statues and the effectiveness of Magic is linked to this general idea:—

> IV. 3, 11. “The ancient sages, in seeking to bring about the presence of the Gods by erecting temples and statues, seem to me to have possessed deep insight into the nature of the universe: They felt the <term>All-Soul</term> to be a Principle always at our call; it is merely a matter of properly preparing a place in which some phase of it may be received, and a thing is always ready to receive the operation of the Soul when it is brought to the condition of a mirror, suited to catch the image.”

> IV. 4, 26. “Our prayers are heard in the sense that they fit into the interconnected scheme of the All; they are effective by virtue of the same universal harmony. This is the secret of Magic also.”

> IV. 4, 40. “Enchantment is possible because of the sympathy and accordant nature of similar things and also through the difference that, equally, exists; in summary, through the variety in the forces which cooperate to form one living being. Even without human intervention there is much magical operation: the true magic is the Love reigning in the Universe—and the Hate, as well.” . . . <note>Plotinus explains that Prayer and Magic are effective through a single cause: the sympathy which every part of the Universe has for every other. If you pull at any one point of a rope hanging free, it swings through its entire length; if you touch one string of a lyre, every other string resonates. Much more must the universe respond to any single action, since it embraces all things—even opposites—reconciled into a perfect harmony.</note>

Plotinus, Magic, All-Soul, sympathy, universal harmony, Enchantment, Divine intuition

<summary>Plotinus argues that all matter possesses an internal, invisible life and that the universe is an interconnected whole. He explains that magic and prayer work through "universal sympathy," where actions in one part of the cosmos naturally resonate through the rest due to their shared harmony.</summary>

<keywords>Plotinus, All-Soul, universal harmony, sympathy, magic, prayer, ancient sages, enchantment, Divine intuition, Neoplatonism</keywords>

### XI

## THE SOUL IN MAN

IV. 3, 15. “As souls proceed from the Intellectual Sphere, they pass first to the heavens and there take on a body. By means of this celestial body, as they acquire more and more spatial extension, they descend to bodies of a more earthly nature. Some of the souls will have entered into a first and only body; others will have passed down from body to body. These latter souls do not have the strength to lift themselves upward again; they are heavily burdened and numbed into forgetfulness; they carry a great weight that bears them down (n).”

IV. 3, 17. “It is a matter of common sense that this is the order of descent. The heavenly region is the most exalted part of perceptible space and touches the borders of the Intellectual Sphere; celestial things are, therefore, the first to receive the Soul, being better suited for participation in the Divine. Earthly matter is of later origin, is further removed from the incorporeal kind, and is of a nature to receive only a lesser and later soul. All the souls, then, illuminate the heavens, casting there the first and most powerful radiance of their splendor (n); the lower world receives only the later rays. Those souls that plunge deepest in the descent shine upon the lowest bodies, but they themselves gain nothing from that service.”

VI. 4, 14. “But what are we? Before our birth into the world we were in the Divine—men of another rank than now, of the order of the Gods, souls unmixed with Matter, an <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> integrated into the entirety of <term>Authentic-Existence</term>; we were members of the Divine Mind, not limited then, not cast out, but wholly part of the All.
“Even now we are not cast out; but upon that <term>Primal-Man</term> which we were, another man has been thrust . . . we have become a double person . . . and our first and higher nature lies dormant.”

### NOTES

“**THE DESCENT OF THE SOULS**”:—Sometimes the descent is explained by the idea that the soul has need of Matter, and Matter has need of the soul: see I. 8, 11, on page 106 of this work. Sometimes it appears that a law of eternal necessity leads the souls to follow their own character and will and enter a body, choosing one suited to the distinctive nature of each. After this, the forgetfulness and self-will mentioned in the first extract arise, causing the fall.

“**FIRST RADIANCE etc.**”:—Compare Extract X on page 148 of this work. To Plotinus, the heavens are a God; so, too, are the sun and stars, which are Gods of the second

<vocab>Plotinus, Soul, Intellectual Sphere, Divine, Matter, Intellectual-Principle, Authentic-Existence, Primal-Man, Descent of Souls</vocab>

<summary>Plotinus describes the Soul’s descent from the Intellectual Sphere through the heavens into earthly bodies, leading to a state of forgetfulness. He explains that humans possess a dual nature, consisting of a dormant Divine self and an added earthly persona.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Soul, descent of souls, Intellectual Sphere, Divine Mind, Authentic-Existence, Primal-Man, celestial bodies, reincarnation, dual nature of man</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Plotinian philosophy, specifically the structure of the divine hierarchy and the soul's relationship to the physical universe and its fate after death.</meta>

<page-num>153</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND DEATH</header>
<sig>x</sig>

order; for, besides the Supreme God, The One, Plotinus admits three orders of Gods: 1. The Intellectual-Puissances, constituting the Divine Mind: see notes on Extract VII, page 144 of this volume; 2. The heavenly bodies; 3. The <term>Daimones</term> or “Blessed Spirits,” midway between earth and moon and between the Gods and men.

Plotinus challenges the theory of Astrology but concedes that the stars signify what is to be; he bases this doctrine on the intimate connection of all things great and small in the Universe:—

> II. 3, 7. “We may think of the stars as letters being ceaselessly traced upon the sky, or traced once for all but being constantly rearranged in such a way that while they do their own work in the universe they also signify to us. The unity of an organic body enables us to reason from one part to another; from the eyes or some other organ one can judge of a man's character, perils, and resources. . . . So the Universe is full of signs, and the wise man is the man who reads reality from the symbols.”

### “WHAT ARE WE?”:—Compare—

> IV. 3, 12. “The human Souls, which can see their own image in the world as in a Dionysos' mirror, have not abandoned their place in the Divine; despite their descent, they are not cut off from their Principle, from the Divine Mind. The Intellectual-Principle does not descend with them, but while they walk the earth, their Summit remains above the heavens.”

-> XII <-

## THE SOUL AFTER DEATH
<note>The word “AFTER” was originally printed as “ATFER.”</note>

I. 1, 10. Page 38 of this volume.

### NOTES

THE Soul liberated by death goes where it has tended and as it has deserved—up to the heavens and among the stars, or into the Divine Mind itself, or into other human bodies, or even deeper into Matter, such as into animals or plants. See I. 8, 13, page 105 of this volume.

Plotinus does not allow that the authentic, the separable Soul, is in the body: rather, the body is in the Soul: see I. 1, 7, page 35 of this work; and

> IV. 3, 20. “The body is visible; the Soul is not. We observe that the body is possessed by a Soul since it moves and feels . . . hence we are led to say that there is a Soul in it. If the Soul were an object of sight or of any sense, we should

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Plotinian philosophy regarding the nature of the Soul, its relationship to the body, and the relative unimportance of worldly misfortunes and death.</meta>

<page-num>154</page-num>
<header>PLOTINUS</header>

perceive that it is wrapped around the entire living being, equally covering it from end to end; we should judge that the Soul is in no way within the body, but rather that the secondary is within its principal, the content within the container, and the passing within the enduring.”

> IV. 3, 22. “The Soul is in the body only as light is in the air (permeating but not enclosed).”

The essential constituent of man is that prior Soul which always remains a member of the Intellectual Realm from which it sprang: see I. 1, 7, page 36 of this volume.

> VI. 7, 5. “The diviner Soul never leaves the Divine Mind: while it clings there, it allows the lower Soul, as it were, to hang down from it, while it holds itself bound by its own <term>Reason-Principle</term> to the <term>Reason-Principle</term> of the Divine sphere to which it belongs.”

It follows that the perceptions of the senses and the disturbances of the mind do not belong to the true man but to the body or to the <term>Couplement</term> of Soul and body, so that the Authentic Soul is not affected but is merely aware of an affection elsewhere:—

> IV. 4, 18. “Sorrow and pleasure of the senses belong to the body thus modified; pain and joy of the body come to us—to the true man—in the form of knowledge without feeling.”

The virtues that are not philosophic but practical or civic belong to this <term>Couplement</term>—as do vices and flaws which cannot touch the true Soul: see I. 1, 11 and 12, page 39 of this volume. True virtue is seated only in the true essence of the man: see I. 4, 14, page 70 of this volume.

For the true Soul, or true man, is the Soul released from the body either by death or by a life of philosophic contemplation: see Porphyry, “There are two modes of death: one, known to everyone, where the body is released from the Soul; the other, that of the Sages, where the Soul is released from the body: the one death may or may not be followed by the other.”

All the trouble of this life and all the changes of the earthly career affect only those who give in too much to what is lower and outer:—

> III. 2, 15. “Man-made weapons directed against fellow mortals in skillfully arranged battles, like <term>Pyrrhic dances</term>, show that all our human affairs are merely children's games; and they show us, too, that death is nothing very serious: to die in wars and battles is to grow old a little before one’s time; it is a sudden departure, only to return again. Or suppose that you are stripped of your wealth; remember that there was a time when you did not yet possess it, and that the one who robbed you will either lose it in turn or find its possession to be a greater evil than its loss could have been.

<summary>This page explores Plotinus's view that the true Soul remains in the Divine realm while only a lower part engages with the body, meaning that physical sensations and worldly misfortunes do not affect the "Authentic Soul." It also frames human life, war, and loss as relatively insignificant "children's games" compared to the enduring nature of the Soul.</summary>

<header>LIKENESS TO GOD</header>

Murders, death in all its shapes, the capture and sacking of towns—all must be considered as so much stage-show, so many shifting scenes, the horror and outcry of a play; for here, too, in all the changing fate of life, it is not the true man, the inner Soul, who grieves and laments, but merely the phantasm of the man, the outer man, playing his part on the stage of the world. Who could be troubled by such griefs, except one who understands only the lower and outer life, never dreaming that all the tears and mighty business are but a sport? . . . If the Sage has to take part in the revels, he will not forget that he has fallen among children and has, for the moment, discarded his own grave truth."

### XIII

## LIKENESS TO GOD

I. 2, 3. Page 44 of this volume.

V. 3, 9. One who seeks to penetrate the nature of the Divine Mind must see deeply into the nature of his own Soul, into the most divine part of himself. He must first make abstraction from the body, then from the lower soul which built up that body, then from all the faculties of sense, from all desires and emotions and every such triviality, and from all that leans towards the mortal. What is left after this abstraction is the part which we describe as the image of the Divine Mind, an emanation preserving some of that Divine Light.

### NOTE

All the virtues are referred to the "<term>Purification</term>," which consists in separating the rational or intellectual soul from the body-soul and from the body: compare I. 6, 6, page 84 of this volume.

It is an error to seek perfect happiness in action or in the moral (or civic) virtues, since all action belongs to the outer, to the mixed life, not to the pure Act of the prior Soul: see I. 1, 10 and the reference to Hercules in I. 1, 12, pages 38, 40, of this volume.

Our task is not merely to expel evil but to become good, not to be without fault but to be God. Still, if we expel evil, the Good comes of itself:—

V. 3, 6. "The Soul, brought to its purity, welcomes the indwelling imprint of the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>."

V. 3, 6. "When we were in the Divine, we rested content in the nature of the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term>; we had intellection and saw all things in <term>The One</term>; for the <term>Intellectual-Principle</term> had the intellection and spoke to the Soul of what..."

<vocab>Plotinus, Enneads, Soul, Divine Mind, Intellectual-Principle, Purification, Sage, Virtues</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses the concept of worldly life as a theatrical performance and the necessity of "Purification" to separate the intellectual soul from the body. It emphasizes that true happiness and likeness to God are found in the pure state of the soul, rather than in outward actions or civic virtues.</summary>

## XIV
### THE VISION OF THE SUPREME

VI. 9, 3. What, then, is The One and what is Its nature? We cannot be surprised to find It difficult to describe, since even Existence and the Ideas resist our penetration, though all our knowing is based upon the Ideas.

The further the human Soul, or Mind, ventures toward the Formless (to what is either above or below Form and Idea), the more it is troubled; it becomes itself, as it were, undefined and unshaped in the face of the shifting variety before it, and so it is utterly unable to take hold; it slips away; it feels that it can grasp nothing. It is in pain in these alien places and often is glad to give up its purpose and to fall back upon the solid ground of the sense-grasped world and there take rest—much as the eye, wearied of the minute and fine, is eased when it meets the large and bold.

Besides, the Soul, when it ventures the vision unaided, thinks itself balked by the very fact that it can see only by completely possessing its object—that is, by becoming one within itself and one with The One. Perfectly assimilated to the object of its contemplation, it recognizes no vision. Despite all this difficulty, there is a way; and this way must be taken by those who desire the life of Wisdom within The One. That which we seek is The One, the Principle of the Universe, The Good, and the First; therefore, the way is to keep ourselves in close proximity to Unity, never allowing ourselves to fall away toward the lower sphere of Multiplicity; we must keep calling ourselves back from the sense-known world . . . to the Primals, from all that is evil to the Absolute Good; we must ascend to this Principle within ourselves, making ourselves one out of our multiplicity; that is, we must become the Intellectual-Principle alone by throwing the entire Soul in confidence into the Intellectual-Principle and so establishing it There that henceforth, in the fullness of life, it shall take to itself all that the Intellectual-Principle sees and thus shall see The One, no longer asking aid from any sense, no longer paying heed to anything that comes by sense, but with pure Intellection and the highest <term>Puissance</term> <note>Power or potency</note> of the Intellective-Principle contemplating the All-Pure.

<summary>This text explores the difficulty of the human soul in perceiving "The One," as the mind struggles with the formless nature of the ultimate principle. It describes the necessary spiritual ascent from multiplicity and sensory experience toward a unified state of pure intellection to achieve a vision of the Supreme.</summary>

<keywords>Plotinus, The One, Soul, Intellectual-Principle, Ideas, Unity, Multiplicity, Intellection, Puissance, Formless, Primal, Mysticism</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Plotinus's philosophy regarding the Soul's ascent, transitioning from the difficulties of perceiving the formless to the actual state of union with "The One" and the nature of the Supreme Vision.</meta>

<page-num>157</page-num>
<header>THE SUPREME VISION</header>

VI. 9, 4. Our greatest difficulty is that consciousness of The One does not come by knowledge, not even by such an intuitive Intellection as possesses us regarding the lower members of the Intellectual Order, but by an actual Presence superior to any knowing. The Soul, when it deals with matters of knowledge, suffers a certain decline from its Unity, for knowing is still an act of reasoning, and reasoning is a multiple act—an act which leads the Soul down to the sphere of number and multiplicity. The Soul, therefore, must rise above knowledge and above all its wandering from its Unity; it must hold itself aloof from all knowing, from all that is knowable, and from the very contemplation of Beauty and Good; for all Beauty and Good are later than This, springing from This as the daily light springs from the sun.

Hence it is that we read of the "Greatness, not to be spoken of, not to be written." If we speak and write here, it is only as guides to those who long to see: we send them to the Place Itself, bidding them go from words to the Vision. The teaching is of the path and the plan, but seeing is the work of each Soul for itself. Some there are who, for all their effort, have not attained the Vision: the Soul in them has come to no sense of the Splendor There; it has not taken warmth; it has not felt burning within itself the flame of love for what is there to know—the passion of the lover resting on the bosom of his beloved. They have received the Authentic Light; all their Soul has gleamed as they have drawn near; but they come with a load on their shoulders which has held them back from the Place of Vision. They have not ascended in the pure integrity of their being but are burdened with that which keeps them apart; they are not yet all one within.

The Supreme is not absent from anyone—and yet is absent from all. Present everywhere, It is absent except only to those who are prepared to receive It, those who have worked themselves into harmony with It, who have seized It and hold It by virtue of their own Likeness to It and by the power in themselves akin to the power which rays from It. These and these only, whose Soul is again as it was when it came out of the Divine, are free to experience whatever Vision of the Supreme Its mighty nature allows.

VI. 9, 11. The Soul restored to Likeness goes to its Like and holds from the Supreme all that a Soul can hold . . . that which is before all things that are, over and apart from all the universe of Existence. This is not to say that in this plunging into the Divine the Soul reaches nothingness: it is when it is evil that it sinks toward nothingness. By this way, this path that leads to the Good, it finds itself; when it is in the Divine it is truly itself, no longer a thing among things. It abandons Being to become a Beyond-Being when its communion is with the Supreme. He who knows himself to have become such, knows himself now as an image of the Supreme; and when the phantom has returned to the Original, the journey is achieved.

<summary>This passage explains that union with "The One" transcends intellectual knowledge and requires the Soul to achieve internal unity and likeness to the Divine. Plotinus describes the mystical journey as a return to one's true self, where the Soul moves beyond ordinary existence to achieve communion with the Supreme.</summary>
<keywords>The One, Intellectual Order, Presence, Soul, Unity, Beauty, The Good, Greatness, Vision, Splendor, Authentic Light, Divine, Likeness, Existence, Beyond-Being, Plotinus, Enneads</keywords>

<header>PLOTINUS</header>

Suppose he falls again from the Vision; he will call upon the virtue within himself and, seeing himself all glorious again, he will take his upward flight once more—through virtue to the Divine Mind, and through the Wisdom there to the Supreme. And this is the life of the Gods and of God-like men: a life without love for the world, a flight of the alone to the Alone.
